# v3: the great refactoring

The charter and checklist of the `release/v3` branch. Every PR against `release/v3`
names the item it closes and ticks its box here. The branch merges
`main` every few days. This file goes when `release/v3` merges into `main`.

Written 2026-10-07 from the planning threads listed at the end. Where
a number or a claim below matters to a decision, it was measured or
checked on that day; re-measure before relying on it months later.

Contents: why; decisions; working on the branch; tooling and workflow;
target layout; the engine map; designs; vocabulary; process; steps;
audit findings; measurements; risks; left alone; open; sources.

## Why

v3 changes how the codebase is built, not what the product does. Its
aims, in order: development velocity, robustness, and less to hold in
one head. Line count is not the metric; the metric is whether one
person and their agents can hold the whole thing in their heads.

Two premises. Everyone working here knows the libraries introduced
below, so their learning cost is not an argument. And YAGNI applies to
implementations, never to requirements (`PRODUCT.md`): nothing the
product does gets trimmed to make the build smaller.

What the audits found, in one line each: the code is lean (0.08% copy-
paste, about 4.5k cuttable lines in 177k), the same knowledge lives in
several hand-synced places in two languages, the hard bugs come from
lifetimes, cancellation and errors crossing process and device
boundaries, and the rules for those live in comments (20% of all
lines) rather than in anything the compiler checks. The architecture
scored 8 of 10; its weak spots are five orchestrators near 1,000 lines,
three hand-rolled wire loops, results trusted unvalidated on the
client, and a hub version floor that the comments promise and no code
implements.

## Decisions

Settled with the owner. Each one says why, so a later PR does not
relitigate it.

1. **Effect, all the way, in every TypeScript package.** Services and
   Layers in place of impl slots and global setters, one layer graph
   per subsystem under a scoped runtime, Schema in place of zod, tagged
   errors end to end, Effect RPC in place of the hand-rolled socket,
   and Schedule, Stream, Semaphore and Cache in place of the
   supervisor, heartbeat, watchers, limiter, singleFlight and ttlCache.
   Half-Effect is the worst outcome: two error models, two ways to own
   a lifetime, and a seam between them everywhere. Effect 4 went stable
   on 2026-10-01; its `rpc`, `socket`, `process`, `cli`, `http` and
   `sql` modules are still marked `@stability unstable`, so
   every Effect package is pinned exactly to the newest release at the
   time it is added, and bumped in its own PR. The owner excluded
   `effect` and `@effect/*` from the 7-day release cooldown on
   2026-10-07, since 4.0.x patches ship every few days. The exemption
   is removed once the pinned versions are seven days old (2026-10-14
   for 4.0.2), and later Effect bumps wait for the cooldown unless the
   owner says otherwise. Effect itself is never patched; a gap is wrapped on our side. The
   conventions are `EFFECT.md`, written before any Effect code.
2. **The Go CLI becomes a TypeScript engine package.** Projects,
   worktrees, state, config, git, clone checkout, land and merge,
   doctor, names, launchers, package scripts, the updater: one
   implementation, written once as Effect services, imported directly
   by the host and wired to commands by a thin terminal entrypoint.
   This deletes `cliDelegate.ts`, `cliRunner.ts`, the Go struct twins
   of every zod schema, the mirrored utilities, and the parity proofs.
   It is the single biggest simplification available: two codebases
   become one language. `file-sync/` stays Go because it embeds
   Mutagen.
3. **The terminal `sm` is a Bun-compiled binary.** Measured on a
   trivial script, warm: Go 4.5 ms, Bun compiled and ad hoc signed
   18 ms, a Node single executable 23 ms, plain node 24 ms, Electron as
   node 85 to 95 ms. Bun is the easiest to build and ship and keeps the
   `RunAsNode` fuse off. Two rules keep it safe: the engine uses Node
   APIs only, never Bun globals, and CI runs the CLI proofs against
   the compiled binary, not only the source under vitest. Electron as
   node was rejected: it needs the fuse on, costs 7x startup on every
   shell open, and reworks the updater.
4. **macOS syscalls go in a small Go helper, not a native addon.** Node
   cannot `clonefile` a tree, read or set `st_flags`, list or strip
   xattrs, read APFS private size through `getattrlist`, name the
   filesystem type, or `lstat` to the nanosecond. A helper bundled like
   `file-sync`, lifting the existing `clone_darwin.go` and
   `diskusage_darwin.go` code, exposes those six verbs. It batches: a verifier asks for the flags and
   xattrs of a whole tree in one call that streams results, never one
   spawn per file. Nothing in the engine is native, so Bun and Node
   behave identically.
5. **One SQLite store in place of JSON files with locks.** The
   registry, project configs, marks and state, plus the mirror
   registry, script persistence, mirror history, ports and the device
   id are hand-rolled JSON persistence today, with two writers, a lock
   protocol, atomic renames, file watchers and echo suppression. SQLite
   in WAL mode is the boring mechanism for two processes sharing
   state: writers serialize, readers never block, a relocate is one
   transaction, migrations are code, and change detection is a cheap
   data-version check. The engine codes against Effect's `SqlClient`
   with the node sqlite driver in the host and the bun one in the CLI
   (`@effect/sql-sqlite-node` at 4.0.2, built on `node:sqlite`, so no
   native addon). v3 imports the JSON once.
   `sm --json` covers what hand-reading the files did.
6. **The host runs as its own process, and the local machine is just
   another device.** After step 4 the renderer reaches a host over
   Effect RPC on a socket. The host moves into an Electron utility
   process serving that socket on loopback, so the desktop renderer,
   the web client and remote devices use one path. The Electron IPC
   bridge, the preload transport, the web loopback binding, the
   local-host gating and the two parallel composition roots go.
   Electron main keeps window, menu, dialogs, tray, keychain and
   updater. Host work can no longer stall the window, a host crash is
   a reconnect, and `sm serve` on a box with no screen comes for
   free. The overhead is one process of roughly 60 MB and a startup that
   overlaps with the window loading; a loopback call costs tenths of a
   millisecond, which every remote device already pays. If it ever
   shows in a profile, Effect RPC also runs over a MessagePort handed
   to the renderer with the same contracts.
7. **Streams in place of invalidation.** The host exposes each view as
   a stream (the worktrees of a project, mirrors, script runs) and the
   renderer subscribes. The query-key registry, the remote-viewer cache
   ping, the `movesHostState` flag and the push handlers that write
   into the cache disappear. A database commit becomes a host stream,
   which becomes an RPC stream, which becomes an atom.
8. **Renderer: views and containers.** Every component is one of two
   things. A view takes data and callbacks and only draws. A container
   binds data to a view and has no markup of its own. With atom-react a
   container is a few atom subscriptions. The marketing site renders
   the same views over fixture scenes at build time and hydrates the
   real containers over a fixture-backed host layer for the live
   frames, which is what the lab's fake host does today in an iframe.
   `@effect/atom-react` replaces React Query (64 call sites) and the
   `useSyncExternalStore` stores (12 files), decided at a go/no-go
   after step 4 when the streams exist to feed it. The rule is in
   `app/DESIGN.md`. At the go/no-go (2026-10-09) a spike put three paths on
   atoms beside their React Query twins: a host change reached the view
   about 110 ms sooner (no refetch after the push) with the same renders
   and the same optimistic latency (Measurements). The line is the
   host's views: a stream is an atom, a request stays a query, and a
   family crosses only when the host serves a view of it, so React
   Query stays for requests and the stores that hold view state go.
9. **Real workspace packages.** `contracts`, `engine`, `ui`, `cli`,
   `hub`, `marketing` and `app`, one pnpm workspace at the root, one
   catalog pinning each dependency once. A package that does not
   depend on `electron` cannot import it, so the boundary proof
   becomes the dependency graph. The single-project layout came from
   the Go split; the marketing PR hit it a second time (two Reacts,
   install ordering, a CSS rewrite plugin), and packages remove all
   three. The host stays in `app/host`, run as the app's utility
   process (decision 6): it ships only inside the app, built by the
   app's Vite config beside the shell and the renderer it serves, so
   a package of its own would draw a boundary that nothing outside the
   app crosses. The desktop and web shells stay in `app` for the same
   reason.
10. **Vitest for the proofs, before any Effect code.** It replaces the
    custom runner, the harness half of `checkKit`, and the per-proof
    lefthook selection with `vitest related`. The hub already runs
    vitest. Forks pool, no file parallelism, since proofs spawn real
    git. It changes how every later PR is tested, so it goes first,
    not in the middle.
11. **The wire break is what makes it 3.0.** Effect RPC between devices
    and to the hub replaces the custom framing. The hub gets a real
    version floor (promised in comments today, implemented nowhere),
    the production hub and the web client flip together at 3.0.0, and
    a v2 device gets a clear "update required". A device reaches v3 only
    through the last v2 release, so every pre-v3 migration is deleted.
12. **`hue.go` is dropped.** It only colors project names in CLI
    listings, the app never uses it, and a port needs image decoders.
    The `projects list` JSON keeps its `hue` field, always null.
13. **The CLI's surface is frozen.** Verbs, flags and the JSON output
    stay as they are through the port, so `skills/` and shell habits
    never change. The parity harness against the Go `sm --json` is
    that contract. The Go `sm` stays the shipped binary until the
    switch-over PR.
    With the Go CLI gone, the engine's and the terminal's own tests
    hold the verbs' JSON shapes: the parity cases that survived it.
14. **`main` keeps moving.** No feature slows down for v3. The branch
    merges `main` every few days and pays the cost of re-doing a
    landed feature against refactored code when it happens.
15. **An early go/no-go.** When the first Effect subsystem (step 3) and
    the first engine slice (step 2) land, the owner reads both and
    decides whether the code is something they want to hold in their
    head, before the other hundred-plus PRs.
    Not held as written. On 2026-10-07, at step 0, the owner chose to
    go ahead on trust ("just go ahead… I would rather trust you"),
    with a pause after each step to check in and correct course. That
    stood in for the go/no-go: no read of the first slice and
    subsystem was asked for or given, and the step reports were the
    check that followed.
16. **PR and worktree titles start with `V3: `**, then an ordinary
    title, and branch names start with `v3/`. No step-and-slice
    naming. This checklist tracks which item a PR closes.
17. **The app owns the terminal.** Interactive shells open from a
    worktree, starting there with the environment its scripts get and
    closing when the worktree goes, or free-standing, starting in the
    last folder used (home the first time) and living as long as the
    host. They belong to the host, so a renderer reload or a remote
    device attaches to the same session, and they are built once, on
    the v3 streams, not on today's wire and again after it.
18. **State is a view, a refresh is a request, an action is a
    request.** Ongoing state the host already watches (git status, a
    worktree's changes, history, a pull request's state) is a host
    view; a user's refresh is a request that makes the host read the
    view now and answers once that read landed; actions stay React
    Query mutations. `effect/reactivity`'s AtomRpc (query atoms with
    reactivity keys over the RPC client) was spiked on the changes
    page on 2026-10-12 and not adopted, which closes it as a library
    candidate (Measurements): the three models sit within about 40 ms
    of each other on one machine; it keeps the push-to-key mapping of
    `hostWatch.ts` and `queryKeys.ts` under another name; it would
    rebuild what React Query gives the requests (the error toasts,
    focus refetch, staleness, abort on unmount); and the link would
    need an adapter to be its protocol. Only a view saves the round
    trip over the hub hop.
    A worktree page's pull request (`githubCli:worktreePullRequest`)
    stays a request: GitHub's state, asked when a page shows it, not
    state the host watches. What that read learns moves the project's
    PR map, which is the host's view.
19. **No onboarding.** The owner's decision after the alpha's first
    launch, over two redesigns of a first-run flow: a fresh install opens
    on the home, v2's orientation page while it has no project, and
    nothing in the app stands between a person and it. What a first run
    could offer stays where it lives anyway (Settings, the account
    page), and the v2 migration's page is the one page shown in place of
    the app.
20. **One owner per operation, one commit point per workflow, one owner
    per fact.** From the maintainability review of 2026-10-15 (the
    branch at 43b560f01 against main), whose verdict was to keep the
    architecture and finish its ownership. The rule for every change
    from here, and the acceptance test of the simplification pass: a
    representative change finds the behaviour's owner, understands its
    state and lifetime, changes it once, and verifies it through that
    capability. Three parts. An operation's rules live in one module
    that owns the domain (worktrees, projects, scripts, terminals,
    mirrors, devices), and a transport handler is an adapter that
    calls it: a workflow that involves host-only state (scripts,
    terminals, mirrors) is owned by a host-level service, not spread
    over the handler, `engineOps` and the process coordination, and
    not pushed into the engine for uniformity. A multi-step change of
    state names its commit point and what holds after a failure or an
    interrupt at every step: which step commits, what finishes after
    it (at the next start or by the doctor), what is undone, with a
    test at each transition; the simplest of completion, rollback or
    recovery per operation, never a general transaction framework.
    Each fact the renderer shows has one owner, a view or a request
    (decision 18), and a bridge that keeps two representations of one
    fact in agreement stays only while it pays for itself and says
    why. Framework surface (Effect's RPC, process, SQL and CLI, pinned
    exactly) is kept where it buys ownership, consistency, reuse or
    testability, not for uniformity; simplicity is measured by how
    much must be held in one head to change a behaviour, not by file
    counts, package names or green checks.
    Routed: the workflow inventory (every multi-step state change in
    the engine and the host with its commit point and recovery, the
    worktree move and the migration first) to the migration hardening;
    the owners per domain and the host worktree service to the
    simplification pass's host PR; the renderer's remaining bridges
    (`hostWatch.ts`, `queryKeys.ts`, the PR detail against the PR map)
    to its renderer PR, each kept or retired with its reason.

## Working on the branch

One orchestration thread owns this file and hands out items. A thread
handed an item starts from this file and the branch's `CLAUDE.md`,
which says the rest: read this file and `EFFECT.md`, take a worktree
with `sm worktrees create --base release/v3`, name it, keep to the item, tick
the box in the same PR, finalize, and report back.

- PRs target `release/v3`. Titles start with `V3: `, then an ordinary title,
  with the `easy-pr` body style. The worktree's title gets the same
  prefix and its branch starts with `v3/`.
- Lefthook runs the targeted checks on commit; CI runs everything on
  the PR. Nobody runs the repo-wide suite by hand.
- `main` is merged into `release/v3` by the orchestration thread, every few
  days, as a merge commit; `release/v3` is never rebased.
- Dependencies: one catalog, Effect pinned exactly to the newest
  release at the time it is added, bumps in their own PRs. The 7-day
  release cooldown stays for everything except `effect` and
  `@effect/*`, which the owner excluded on 2026-10-07. That exemption
  is removed once the pinned versions are seven days old (2026-10-14
  for 4.0.2), and later Effect bumps wait for the cooldown unless the
  owner says otherwise.
- Two kinds of PR need a human even when green: one that adds or
  widens a diagnostic suppression, and one that changes a product
  default.
- A PR body ends with what was left out and why.

## Tooling and workflow

Kept deliberately small for a one-person project. Each is an item in
step 0 unless it says otherwise.

- **Lint rules as a tested plugin.** `lint/` becomes one rule per file
  with a test per rule, run by vitest, instead of one module. The
  rules for Effect: namespace imports from subpaths, no runtime inside
  a service, no node builtins inside a service, every disabled
  diagnostic says why.
- **knip in CI.** A dead export fails the build; it is the conciseness
  goal made mechanical.
- **`pnpm check`.** One script that is exactly what CI runs:
  typecheck, lint, format check, knip, the proofs, the hub suite.
  Lefthook runs the targeted subset of the same thing.
- **Conventions as review rubrics.** `EFFECT.md` and `app/DESIGN.md`
  are what the finalize review holds changed lines to.
- **The pinned Effect source is the reference.** `node_modules/effect`
  in any checkout is the exact version; agents read it instead of
  guessing an unstable API. No vendored copy, no sync script.
- **Measurements, not budgets** (step 2). The compiled `sm`'s startup
  and size are measured against the Go binary and compared now and
  then, not gated on every commit.
- **Scene comparison on demand** (step 6). Every scene in both themes,
  light and dark, desktop and phone, shot and compared against a base
  (`release/v3` while v3 is built there, then `main`) by a command run
  by hand or in CI, never on commit. The marketing
  thread did this by hand with ImageMagick to prove the view split
  changed nothing.
- **Working rules:** a test that needs a timeout to pass is wrong,
  once its subsystem is on `TestClock`; complexity belongs at the
  adapter boundary and the UI stays dumb; comments describe how a
  thing is used and move with the code; repo-wide checks are CI's job,
  not a local habit. They are in
  `EFFECT.md`, `app/DESIGN.md` and the branch's `CLAUDE.md`.

Considered and left out for now: a vendored Effect source tree, an AI
review action in CI, nightly prereleases and a packaged-app smoke, a
generated error catalog, Renovate. This file stays because it is the
shared context of a hundred agent threads and goes at merge.

## Target layout

Where the code ends up. A path on the left is today's; the package on
the right is where it lives once its step lands. Nothing moves before
its step; the scaffold in step 0 only creates the workspace.

| Today | Package | Step |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `app/shared/schemas`, `app/shared/ipc`, `app/shared/hub/protocol.ts`, `app/shared/errors.ts` | `contracts`: Schema, RPC groups, errors, the protocol version | 1 |
| `cli/` (Go), `app/host/lib/git`, `app/host/lib/projects`, `app/host/lib/config`, `app/host/lib/worktrees`, `app/shared/git` | `engine`: the data model and the store, git, worktrees, landing, doctor, updater | 2 |
| `app/host` (the rest), `app/main/core`, `app/shared/remote`, `app/shared/hub` (node side), `app/shared/account` | `app/host`: what a machine serves, as the app's utility process, shipped only with the app (decision 9) | 3, 4 |
| `app/renderer/components/ui`, the views, `app/renderer/*.css`, the fixtures and scenes from `app/lab` | `ui`: views, primitives, stylesheets, fixture world | 6 |
| `app/renderer` (containers, hooks, router), `app/main/electron`, `app/main/ipc`, `app/index.html`, forge config | `app`: the Electron shell (decision 9) | 4, 6 |
| `app/web` | `app/web`: the browser shell, an RPC client over the hub (decision 9) | 4 |
| `cli/main.go`, `menu.go`, the pickers, `cmd_shell.go`, `cmd_run.go` | `cli`: the terminal entrypoint on `effect/cli`, compiled with Bun | 2 |
| `hub/` | `hub`: unchanged location, Effect inside | 5 |
| `marketing/` | `marketing`: unchanged location, depends on `ui` | 6 |
| `file-sync/`, `macfs/` (the darwin helper) | stay Go, bundled as today | 2 |

`app/test` becomes each package's tests plus a `proofs` folder for the
end-to-end ones; `app/lab` stays the lab, with its fixtures moved to
`ui`.

## The engine map

What each part of `cli/` becomes. Services, not files: a service may
span a few modules, and the terminal commands are the thin layer on
top.

| Today (`cli/`) | Engine service |
| --- | --- |
| `state.go`, `context.go`, `repoidentity.go`, the paths helpers | `Store` (the SQLite store and its migrator), `Paths`, `Identity` |
| `cmd_config.go`, `embed/launcher-catalog.json`, `launchers.go`, `scripts.go` | `Config` (keys and defaults from one Schema), `Launchers`, `Scripts` |
| `gitx.go` and `app/host/lib/git/*` | `Git` (one layer for both) |
| `worktree.go`, `cmd_list_path.go`, `cmd_status.go`, `cmd_rm.go`, `cmd_adopt.go`, `cmd_describe.go`, the names | `Worktrees` |
| `clonecheckout.go`, `carryover.go`, `clone_darwin.go`, `diskusage_darwin.go` | `CloneCheckout`, `CarryOver`, `Hygiene`, and the Go helper behind one `Darwin` service |
| `cmd_land.go`, `cmd_merge.go`, `stack.go`, the PR lookups | `Landing` (land, merge, stack, pull requests) |
| `doctor_*.go`, `cmd_doctor.go` | `Doctor` |
| `cmd_transfer.go`, the shelf | `Transfer`, `Shelf` |
| `updater.go`, `cmd_update.go`, `semver.go` | `Updater` (with the tests it never had) |
| `cmd_shell.go`, `cd`, `cmd_run.go` | the `cli` package: shell integration, `sm run` as a signal-forwarding child |
| `main.go`, `menu.go`, the pickers, the output helpers | the `cli` package on `effect/cli`; menus on ink |
| `control.go` | the RPC client from `contracts`, in step 4 |
| `hue.go` | dropped |
| the terrier client | `Terrier` |

The parity harness runs the Go `sm --json` and the engine against the
same sandboxes and diffs each verb's output until they match. The
existing `cli-reads` and `identity` proofs become it. The differential
clone test (`clonecheckout_fuzz_test.go`: seeded random trees against
`git worktree add`) is ported, not re-imagined.

## Designs

The shape of the pieces behind the big decisions, so a thread does not
have to rediscover them.

**The store.** One database in the data dir, opened by the engine
only. Tables, roughly: projects, worktrees, marks, project config,
device config, package script order, ports, the device identity, the
usage log, mirrors, mirror history, script runs, the shelf. Launcher
catalog and themes stay data files in the package, not rows. Writers
serialize through SQLite; the app notices the CLI's writes by polling
the data version on a short tick and re-reading what changed, which
replaces the state watcher and its echo suppression. Migrations run on
open; the first imports the JSON files and, once the store is verified
to hold what they said, deletes them (on any doubt, every one stays and
the doctor offers to delete them).

**Contracts and annotations.** Every call is an `Rpc` in a per-module
`RpcGroup`. The flags from today's `InvokeDef` become annotations read
by middleware, with the same meanings: `remote` reaches a peer; `gated`
is served to a peer only while the command switch is on; `invitable`
is the mirror invitation's exception, scoped to landing, copy or
project; `tracksProjectUsage` feeds the
usage sorts; `grant` names the consent text that covers the call. A
host-scoped call must classify itself, middleware fails closed on a
missing annotation, and a proof checks every group. The client facade
and the `HostApi` type are generated from the groups. Results are
validated on the client for remote calls and pushes.

**The host process.** The desktop shell spawns the host as a utility
process at launch and connects to it on loopback like any device.
The shell owns the account (Clerk, the keychain) and hands the host
what it needs (the hub token) through an RPC call at start and on
refresh. The device identity is the host's, in its store, and the
shell learns it as the host comes up. The host owns everything else: the engine,
scripts, mirrors, the device link, port forwarding. Quit is the shell
asking the host whether it may, then closing the scope. Terminal bytes
and forwarded ports ride the same byte streams remote worktrees use
today. The web client is the same RPC client over the hub with no
local host, which is what it already is in spirit.

**Time.** Every time decision names its meaning, its owner and its
clock. Epoch time is for dates shown to a person, persisted records,
file metadata and an authority's token expiry. An elapsed budget in a
process is measured on a monotonic clock, which in the pinned Effect 4
is `Clock.monotonicTimeNanos`: `currentTimeMillis` and
`currentTimeNanos` are both the wall clock, and so is what Effect's
RPC pinger, `Schedule`'s elapsed limits and `Cache`'s TTLs read. The
Clock is never replaced with a monotonic one wholesale, which would
corrupt every epoch field. Every elapsed policy also says what a sleep
or a suspension does to it: a budget counts awake time, or time
including the sleep, or is an absolute deadline. A Mac's monotonic
clock stops in a sleep and a stopped process's does not, so an
observation that spans either is measured again after the wake, not
trusted. These rules were added on 2026-10-16 from the time and
liveness audit of that day; the sites it found outside the links are
the next thread's.

**Local links and liveness.** Three rules for links.
- Local links have lifecycle, not heartbeats. The window's and the
  host's ports to the shell, and the loopback dials of a window, the
  shell and the terminal `sm` (`Control.ts`) to their own host, close
  when the port or the process at either end goes, and when the
  operation is cancelled. No end of them judges the other by pings,
  and the loopback listener runs no silence cut for them. The shell's
  watchdog (`main/core/host/watchdog.ts`) is the one judge of whether
  the host still answers: it kills a silent host and starts another,
  and the links redial that one. A window's timer never decides on
  its own that its shell or its host is gone.
- One owner retries a link, and one authority reports its liveness. A
  one-attempt ticketed transport (`deviceLink.ts`'s `recurs(0)`) sits
  under an owner (the keeper, `hostLink`) that dials again with a
  fresh ticket. The host's cut of a silent peer frees its slot; it is
  not a second verdict on the peer. A link's teardown happens once and
  tells its owner once.
- No overdue timer is evidence that the other side had a chance to
  answer. A link to another machine, a device's or the hub socket, is
  judged only on a fresh probe that was out for its whole window while
  this end ran (`shared/remote/liveness.ts`): a probe past its timeout
  is the cue for a fresh one, a turn that comes late by the wall or the
  monotonic clock (a hidden page's timers, run about once a minute, a
  sleep, a stopped process) interrupts the probe out and asks again,
  and any frame after the probe answers it. A question asked once
  follows the same rule (`answerWithin`): the watchdog's round, a
  quit's busy ask and the mirror daemon's ping, one whose limit came
  late asking again rather than kill.
  The host's cut of a silent remote peer does too: silence is
  monotonic, and however late a sweep comes (the host stopped, its
  machine asleep) its lateness is no peer's silence
  (`host/socket/DeviceLink.ts`).

Effect's pinger broke all three: it checks the age of the last frame
against its limit before it sends a ping, on the wall clock, with no
retry beneath it. A port's link, left its default limit (the hour's
interval), was given up for good after an idle hour or a sleep, and
the loopback's dials and the peers' links were cut at every wake and
in a hidden window. Its pings still flow, keeping the far end's slot,
and judge nothing.

**The web client's tabs.** A browser profile is one enrolled device,
and each of its tabs is a connection of its own, told apart by an id
the tab mints as it dials. A device's kind says how many it may hold.
A web device holds several: the hub relays to all of them, a host's
link holds a set per device with pushes and views per connection, and
presence reads online if any is. A desktop device holds one: a second
connection supersedes the first, and the superseded one stays blocked,
since there it means two app instances on one root. So no tab leads,
none takes over on focus, and nothing can ping-pong between them. The
host's half is in: the link's hello carries the device's kind and the
connection's id (contracts' `link.ts`), and a hello with an id already
held replaces that connection alone, its own redial.

**Time: a path back from every failure.** Every failed or parked state names
the way out of it. A transient failure (the network, a timeout, DNS, a
deploy, configuration, a child that would not start) gets a retry with
a cap, owned by a scope, or a retry the user sees and presses. Only a
fact about identity, version, deletion or the user's intent parks for
good: a revoked or superseded device, a build the hub turns away, a
web client with no listener to dial. A wake, the network coming back
and a session landing each reach the owner that is waiting, and that
includes a renewal that failed: a window renewing a peer's attention
asks again on a capped ladder while it stays focused and the session
up, and at once when the network comes back
(`renderer/lib/remote/remoteSweep.ts`). Before that, one failed
renewal stopped the peer's sweep, so its remote refs, its PR dots and
its auto-pull stood still until the window lost focus and got it back.
The ladders' uptime is monotonic, and a wake starts again one not yet
past the stable threshold
(`shared/remote/supervisor.ts`, `shared/hub/directKeeper.ts`). The hub
socket judges silence as a device link does, any frame answering its
ping (`shared/hub/connection.ts`), and logs a death after a sleep as
how long it slept. The host's attention lease is monotonic too,
and its fetch and PR windows go stale by whichever clock reads older
(`host/lib/BackgroundFetch.ts`).

**Time: a deadline bounds a wait, not an outcome.** A budget for an
operation that commits elsewhere is one elapsed budget through its
phases, in monotonic time or in polls (time awake): GitHub's stack
merge (`GitHub.ts` `mergeStackAsync`), stack settling
(`landing/stacks.ts`), the one-shot mirror transfer
(`host/mirror/oneShot.ts`), the worktree removal's retry while a folder
refills (`worktrees/removing.ts`), the updater's fetches, quits,
restarts and lock waits (`updater/documents.ts` `deadlineIn` and
`polling`), and the app's backoff between failed update checks,
counted in ticks (`main/electron/updater.ts`). A sleep or a clock step
spends none of it. A poll that fails is asked again within it, and
past it the real result is read before anything is reported: a merge
that landed while its poll timed out reads as merged. Nothing replays
a create, merge, send, install or delete to cure a timeout.

**Time: a helper's exit is not its health.** A supervisor that waits
only for its child's exit leaves a stopped child (a SIGSTOP, a sleep
that left it wedged) "running" for good. Each long-lived helper is
asked through the protocol it already speaks, at the moments that
matter, with no heartbeat of its own. The mirror daemon is asked by a
ping when a request has waited 30 s and after a wake: its reader
answers any op at once, a refusal included, so 10 s of silence is a
stopped process. It is killed outright and started again once, and
its waiting requests fail as "unresponsive"
(`host/mirror/MirrorDaemon.ts`). After a wake, and once per run when a
device asks how to connect after two of its dials in a row reached
nothing with a tunnel ticket, cloudflared's tunnel is probed at the edge. One that no longer routes stops being advertised
and is probed on the reused ladder, so a network still coming back
costs no restart, and one still unroutable past that deadline is
replaced (`host/direct/Tunnel.ts` `recheck`). A child past its deadline
or its caller's interrupt gets SIGKILL 3 s after its SIGTERM wherever
the engine's graph, the host's or the shell's spawns one, unless its
command sets a grace of its own (`engine/processes.ts`
`childDefaults`), and a git fetch from a remote has five minutes. The
account-shell lookup is bounded at 5 s besides.

**Time: proving a timing rule.** A proof of a time decision moves the clocks
apart, as a machine does. The two variations that matter are run
together: a sleep (on a Mac the wall clock runs on while monotonic
time and every timer stand still) or a stopped process (both clocks
run on and no timer runs), carried past the timeouts in play; and the
epoch stepped forward and back with monotonic time still
(`sleepingClock` in `app/test/lib/checkKit.mts`). A TestClock jump
alone is not a sleeping laptop, and a helper's proof stops the real
process (`pnpm test mirror`, the stopped daemon). The proof checks that the user sees
the thing recover and that no committed action ran twice
(`pnpm test clock-steps`).

**Streams.** Each view the renderer shows is a streaming RPC on the
host, fed from the store's change notifications and the in-process
state (script runs, mirror sessions). The renderer subscribes through
atoms; nothing invalidates anything. A peer's view is the same stream
over the device link.

**Views and scenes.** Views and primitives live in `ui` with the
stylesheets and the fixture world. A scene composes views over
fixtures; the lab's fake host becomes a fixture-backed host layer the
real containers run over, in-process. The marketing site renders
scenes at build time and hydrates the containers over that layer for
live frames: one React, one shared bundle, islands mounting as frames
near the screen. The `scenes` proof renders every view in Node; the
on-demand scene comparison shoots every scene in every mode against
`main`.

**The migration's screen.** A first v3 start owes a 2.x data dir an
import into the store, a move of every worktree into `wt/`, and this
device's key and enrollment. The engine's `Migration` service holds
what this start owes and how far each step is (waiting, running, done,
stuck); the store reports its import as it opens and the move each
worktree as it goes. It is built before the store, and the host tells
its shell each change over the port they share. The shell serves it to
its windows (`migration:read` and `migration:changed`, client-scoped)
rather than the host as one of its views, because the host's loopback
answers only once its graph is up, and the move has to finish before
the graph's services start, since they look at the worktrees' roots (a
mirror whose original went missing ends). The shell's port to each
window is up from the first moment, so a window, one opened meanwhile
included, shows one page in place of the app: the steps, the move's
count and the worktree on the move, a stuck worktree with git's reason
in one line, and the device key's enrollment as its sign-in, the
window's own (`useEnrollment`), which goes by itself while the session
lives and is a button once it has lapsed. With every step done the app
opens by itself; past a stuck step or a sign-in left for later,
Continue opens it. The counts come from the store, so a start after a
crash shows the run it resumes. A start that owes nothing never shows
the page. Only the app's start and `sm doctor --fix` migrate: an agent's
hook or a script runs `sm` with work open in the worktrees the move
would take from under it, so the terminal's other commands read a 2.x
data dir as v2 left it, refuse to change it, and a hook's event goes
quietly unrecorded (`packages/cli/src/engine.ts`). The move leaves a worktree a
process works in (a shell, a dev server, an agent), and its stuck line
names what to close. The page's Retry tries the stuck moves again once
it is closed (`migration:retry`, the host's `session:migrationRetry`),
and says when a retry left one stuck. An import that fails (a file the
store refuses, a store that won't open, a driver that won't load) is
stuck with its reason on the page, the error and the doctor's failed
checks, in place of the dialog: the host made its `Migration` before
its graph, so a graph that fails still tells the shell where the
migration stood, and the shell parks it rather than forking it again.
A window mounts the app only once its host is up, so until then it
shows that page alone (`StuckImport.tsx`); its Retry forks a host again
(`restartParkedHost`), and the window boots again once the import is
past stuck. A store a newer build migrated is refused with "made by a
newer version of the app; update the app", the store's side of the
updater never offering a lower version.

**Commit points (decision 20).** Each multi-step change of state, the
one step after which it counts as done, and what a crash or an
interrupt at any step leaves for the next start. The inventory of
2026-10-16 graded them; the ones marked open go in the next PRs.
- The worktree move (`worktrees/moving.ts`, every move: `worktrees
  move`, a rename, the migration's, relocate's rehome, adopt's). A row
  of `moving` is written before anything moves, by a process that
  refuses one another live process holds. git's move commits: from
  the record to the transaction that re-keys what is kept under the id,
  records the move in `wt_moves` and deletes the record, nothing
  interrupts it. A copy across volumes goes into a `.partial-*` sibling
  renamed in once whole, and only the copy is interruptible (undone).
  The app's start (before the migration's drain) and `doctor --fix`
  (`project-interrupted-moves`) take over a record whose process is
  gone, one process alone. The move landed when git lists its own
  checkout (the `.git` link the record holds) at the destination and no
  longer at the source; then it is finished, else undone, deleting only
  what the move made (its partial sibling, or a folder holding its
  link). The re-key is marked carried in its own transaction and never
  repeated, a create refuses a path a record still names, and the
  record goes last, after the capture's ref followed. Held by `Worktrees.interrupt.test.ts` and the app's
  `interrupted-workflows` proof, which SIGKILLs the terminal at each
  step.
- The re-key and the forget (`worktrees/kept.ts`): the rows in one
  transaction, the dirty capture's ref after it, idempotently, never
  over a capture already kept under the new id, a move's only the
  capture its journal names, and an undo's only that one back.
- The worktree removal (`removing.ts`): git's remove commits; it and
  the forget run uninterrupted, a clock on a cleanup script stops only
  that script, and a create clears what a removal a crash cut short
  left under the id it mints.
- Land (`landing/verbs.ts`): GitHub's merge commits; the removal's
  guards (`checkRemovable`, the commits the PR doesn't hold) run again
  after every merge, one GitHub answers at once included, since a
  commit made while gh merged is in no ref the merged PR holds. Before,
  only a merge that waited ran them again, so that commit went with
  the branch.
- A transfer's source teardown (`host/lib/sync/receipts.ts`): the
  delete commits, forced when the move captured uncommitted work. The
  receipt is asked before the cleanup, so a source that changed keeps
  its cleanup unrun, and again by the removal on the source's own
  device after its cleanup, right before the delete
  (`movedSourceChange`, `Worktrees.remove`'s `expect`), since a
  cleanup script or an editor can write to it meanwhile.
- Adopt: a locked checkout is refused, then the replacement is added
  (its own checks first: a folder there, a move naming it), git
  checking the branch out though the external checkout still has it,
  and only then the external checkout goes and what it kept is
  re-keyed, uninterrupted. Just before the removal the original's HEAD
  must still read as the replacement's, or both checkouts stay, each
  holding its commits, and the adopt is refused (`adopt-moved`), since
  a commit made in the original after it was listed is in no ref the
  replacement holds; a commit in the moments between that read and the
  removal is left. A
  removal that fails takes the replacement back only while the
  original's HEAD still resolves to the commit the replacement checked
  out (git may have let go of it, or kept a listing whose HEAD is
  gone); otherwise the replacement stays, holding the commits. A crash between the add and the removal leaves both
  checkouts on the branch, never neither; removing the external one
  finishes it. The add claims its
  place as git makes it, so a creator racing for the folder fails
  there, before anything is removed.
- The v2 import (`Store.ts`): the migrations commit together, so a
  crash before reimports and one after leaves the import; the v2 files
  are deleted only once verified, and any a crash left are the ones
  `v2_import` names, which the doctor deletes. Held by
  `Store.crash.test.ts`, which SIGKILLs an open at forty points of its
  run.
- The migration's move into `wt/` (`WtFolder.ts`): each worktree's
  move as above, no write lock held across it, and a row whose
  worktree git already lists in `wt/` with no record of the move stays
  stuck rather than carried by its path alone; a store that fails the
  drain leaves the step stuck with why and a Retry, never a host that
  can't start; the emptied v2 roots go at the next start too.
- Project removal: one transaction, its primary's marks, shelf, agent
  sessions, unshelve time, clone records and icon included.
- Project relocate (`worktrees/relocating.ts`): the new path and what
  every worktree it moved kept, re-keyed, commit in one transaction;
  git's repair before it is idempotent, and the dirty captures' refs
  follow after it, as a move's do.
- Nuke (`main/electron/nuke.ts`, `main/core/nuke/finish.ts`): the
  marker in userData commits it. It is written beside itself and
  renamed in, so a start finds the whole marker or none, and the start
  that finds it deletes what it names and userData, the marker last. A
  marker that can't be read deletes nothing and stays: half a nuke
  (userData gone, the data dir kept) would be no install at all.
- The data-folder move (`host/lib/dataDirMove.ts`): the pointer commits
  it. The journal is written before the re-key; a start that finds it
  at the source undoes the move, one that finds it at the destination
  finishes it. A rename a crash or a cancel cuts before the pointer
  moves leaves the pointer naming a folder that is gone, so every
  process, the terminal's included, finds the data dir through `Paths`,
  which looks for a rename's journal (a copy's never counts: its source
  is kept and undoes it) at the staged pointer's target and at the
  default location, and commits the pointer when that journal names
  the folder that left (`engine/dataDirMoveJournal.ts`); one that can't
  write the pointer refuses to resolve, naming both folders, rather
  than lead to the one that left. The staged pointer is kept once the
  folder has landed. A copy to another volume
  holds the store's write lock for its length and, before letting go,
  makes the store it leaves refuse every write, so one that waited
  fails ("the data folder moved") rather than landing in a store about
  to be retired. Its recovery keeps the journal until every step it owes is done: a
  worktree's link git couldn't repair, a re-key back or a copy's
  removal that failed, each leaves it for the next start to try
  again, and one it can't read stays, for a look, rather than go with
  the only record of what the move left.

  The journal, step by step. It lives in the data folder, so it moves
  with it, and it is written whole and flushed (the file and its
  folder) at every step. Each worktree that moves is an entry: its
  project, old and new path, its dirty capture's commit (null for none)
  and its mark, `planned`, `rekeyed` or `returned` (absent, an older
  journal's, reads as `planned`). "Read strictly" is a read whose error
  is an error, never taken for absence: a capture ref git can't open or
  parse (`Git.readRef`: only `rev-parse --verify --quiet`'s exit 1 is
  missing, and a loose file still there or a packed-refs it can't read
  says otherwise), and an owner journal that can't be read or decoded.

  **The move is the one change under way.** A database of its own beside
  the pointer file, where every process of the flavor finds it wherever
  the data dir is, is the data-folder lock (`DataDirLock`). A change that
  touches git, its refs or files holds it shared for its run, at the
  engine's entry points: every call that changes code, which a peer
  needs the `changeCode` grant for (the device link serves the app's
  windows, the terminal's loopback and peers alike); a landing's create,
  its capture's ref and its apply, held as one change wherever it is
  asked from (a pull or a bring as much as a send); every change the host
  makes through the engine (`asChange`; the quit prompt counts the git
  work a person started, a create's or an adopt's run included after
  its call answers); the auto-pull's fast-forward; and each terminal command that
  changes a worktree on disk, the doctor's `--fix` included (`changing`;
  a create's subshell after it holds nothing). A move
  holds it alone from its first step to its last, waiting 30 s for the
  changes under way, and a recovery holds it alone too. A process holds
  it shared once however many changes it runs: the first takes and
  checks it, the rest join in the order they came, so the host still
  starts calls in that order, and the last lets it go; a take a move
  refused refuses the changes that queued while it was asked for. A
  change nested in one that holds it joins even while a move of the
  same process waits, so no change is left half done. A hold held
  elsewhere is asked for again every 50 ms, not waited for in SQLite,
  whose wait under node's driver stops the whole process, and with it
  the changes of its own a move waits for. Taken alone, the write is
  reserved first, and an attempt refused it drops its connection, so
  two asking at once (two starts settling) never hold each other off
  with the reads their attempts took; the reservation and the commit
  share one wait. A process that can't open a lock it may write is
  refused, since a move elsewhere may hold it; one that isn't a lock
  database at all refuses every move, and the changes run. A move is
  refused while this device sends or brings a worktree (`Moves`, from
  before its landing to its last file), asked once the move holds the
  lock, so one that starts after is refused by the lock instead; a
  peer's send into this device is Left (below). SQLite's locks
  are the kernel's, so a hold goes with its process however that ends,
  and nothing reads a pid to tell whether a mover is alive: whoever
  holds the lock alone is the only one that can act on a journal. A
  change that finds a move holding it is refused ("the data folder is
  being moved"), and one in a process that found the data dir before a
  move took it elsewhere is refused too (`Paths.current`, read under the
  hold, against the data dir the process started with, with a rename a
  crash cut before its pointer moved committed first, as a start does;
  a data dir that is gone counts as moved). A write that
  only touches the store holds nothing: the store refuses every write
  but the move's from before the journal's first write until after it
  is gone, in every process (the store's own triggers, `retireStore`),
  and a write of the move's, or of the recovery that settles it, runs in
  one write transaction that lets the store write and makes it refuse
  again before it commits (`asMoveOwner`). Every engine graph, the
  terminal's as much as the app's, settles what a move left as it comes
  up, before anything reads a worktree by its id (`settleLayer`): a
  journal, or a store left refusing with none, settled holding the lock
  alone (2 s; while a move holds it, the store keeps refusing and the
  next start tries again). The settle acts only where the pointer leads
  now: what a move left at a folder the data dir has left, a store left
  refusing included, is left for the start there. While a move waits
  for the changes under way, new ones are refused already.

  | Step | Trusts | Journal before → after | A crash after it leaves |
  | --- | --- | --- | --- |
  | 0. The lock alone, then settle | the lock, held alone, waiting 30 s for the changes under way (one still running refuses the move); the journal file's presence (an error reading it counts as present) | an earlier one, settled (below) → none | an earlier journal: the move is refused, and every process keeps refusing writes until one settles it |
  | 1. Scripts killed, pointer staged and flushed | — | none | a staged pointer naming a folder that never came; Paths' rescue needs a journal, so it is ignored |
  | 2. The store refusing, then the journal | the store's triggers, in its file | none → no entries | before the journal: a store refusing with no journal, which the next settle lets write; after: undo, nothing moved |
  | 3. Plan | git's listing of each project's worktrees, made holding the lock alone, so a create, an adopt or a move anywhere can't change it; the listing's own writes are the move's; a listing that fails refuses the move, unless the project's folder is gone | unchanged | as 2 |
  | 4. Preflight | nothing kept under any entry's new id: no marks, sessions, shelf or data rows, and no capture, read strictly; anything refuses the move. Holding the lock alone, nothing can put anything there after | unchanged | as 2 |
  | 5. Snapshot | each entry's capture, read strictly (an error refuses the move) | → every entry `planned` | undo: nothing to reverse |
  | 6. Each entry re-keyed, then marked | the move's own write: the rows in one transaction; the capture only if it is the snapshot's commit (else the move fails, `changed`) | entry `planned` → `rekeyed` | an entry re-keyed but still `planned`: undo reverses it, since only its re-key can have put anything under its new id (steps 2 and 4) |
  | 7. Watchers closed | — | unchanged | as 6 |
  | 8. Folder moved, flushed | a rename is atomic, and it and its mark are one step a cancel can't cut; a copy writes `partial` first, holds the store's write lock, flushes every file and folder it copied (on macOS past the drive's cache), then renames the copy in; the folders the rename changed are flushed | `partial` set (a copy) | before the rename: undo at the source; after: the journal is at the destination with the pointer unmoved, which Paths' rescue commits (a rename) or the source's journal undoes (a copy) |
  | 9. Pointer committed, flushed | an atomic rename (or the pointer removed, for the default place), and its folder flushed; one a start's rescue committed first counts | unchanged | finish at the destination |
  | 10. Finish | in the moving process, git's repair alone; then, by the first process to open the store at the new place, holding the lock alone: the old tree is this move's only while it holds this move's journal, read strictly; git's repair of every worktree it lists under the old folder, the journal's entries and any other, a project whose folder is gone passed over; the store there | → none, once the old tree is gone (its journal last), every repair is done; then the store writes again | finish again, by the next process to open the store there. Until then a copy's old tree stays, and its space stays taken until the app comes back up at the new place |

  Undo, from the last entry to the first, each reversal the move's own
  write: `returned` is skipped; `rekeyed` is reversed; `planned` is
  reversed only if something is kept under its new id. A reversal moves
  the rows back in one transaction and the capture only if it is the
  snapshot's commit, read strictly; any other capture stays where it is,
  and an unreadable one fails the undo. Each reversed entry is marked
  `returned` before the next, so an undo retried never reverses one
  twice. Then a copy's partial folder goes, and its landed one (ours by
  its journal, which goes last), then the journal, and only then does
  the store write again; a failure keeps the journal and the refusal
  for the next process. A journal that can't be read is never settled:
  it stays as it is, for a look, and the store keeps refusing. Left: a
  power loss the flushes don't cover (a disk that lies about its
  flush); a ref store that is neither loose files nor packed-refs
  (reftable), whose failure to open reads as missing; a migration of
  the store's schema due while a journal is out, which the refusal
  stops until the journal is settled; a git a change started that
  outlives its process (killed while git ran), whose lock went with
  the process, so a move can start while that git still writes; the
  mirror daemon and the git follower, which write mirrored worktrees'
  files and their index refs on their own fibers through a move, so a
  copy can take a mirrored file mid-write (the peer's next cycle sends
  it again); `SHIGOMORI_DATA_DIR` naming the folder a move moved, which
  still names the old place; a lock folder this user can't write (a
  `~/.config` made by root) or a lock file SQLite can't read, where
  changes run without the lock and every move is refused; a write from
  outside sm (a terminal, an editor, an agent) into a copy's old tree
  while it copies or before the relaunch, which goes with the old tree,
  as v2's copy lost it; a ref git writes unflushed (its `core.fsync`
  default flushes objects, not refs), so a power loss just after a move
  can lose a re-keyed capture's ref as it can any ref git wrote; and
  the app's start-up moves into `wt/` (`WtFolder.drained`), which run
  unguarded, since only the app moves the data folder; a peer's send
  landing here, whose files arrive through the mirror daemon after the
  landing; the undo of a landing's create, refused while a move waits,
  which leaves the worktree the move then carries; work a change forks
  to outlive it, which joins as part of that change (a create's run,
  which goes on after its call answers); and a
  lock journal a process killed inside its one-row commit leaves hot,
  whose next taker keeps the lock alone while it holds it.
- The updater's swap (`engine/updater/swap.ts`): one exchange commits
  it, `renamex_np`'s RENAME_SWAP through `macfs exchange` where the
  process has the helper (the terminal, beside its binary), so the
  target holds an app at every moment, a crash's included; the old
  bundle left at `.X.app.new-*` is pruned by the next stage. Without
  the helper, the two renames as before, the aside holding the app
  between them.
- The clone from a peer (`host/lib/sync/cloneFromPeer.ts`): the rename
  of a whole clone into its folder and its register, run
  uninterrupted, commit it. It is built in a `.name.partial-<pid>`
  sibling held in a scope, so a failure or a cancel removes it, and a
  dead process's is swept by the next clone of that name. A
  `sm-clone-pending` file in its `.git`, written before the rename and
  removed after the register, lets the next clone of the folder
  register a clone a crash left between the two. A project's register
  and its seeded settings are one transaction, a seed write that fails
  taking the register with it. The landing
  after it commits as it answers: until then a failure or a cancel
  undoes its create (`Worktrees.createHeld`), and after, a cancel of
  the move's later steps does.
- Carry-over (`engine/CarryOver.ts`): each entry is copied whole into a
  staging folder beside the worktree and renamed in, so an entry is
  there whole or not at all; a dead create's staging folder is swept
  by the next one, and the doctor doesn't read it as a stray worktree.

**The migration matrix.** The migration run on a packaged test-flavor
build (`SM_FLAVOR=mig`, at #926's tip) against copies of Lychee's real
2.x data: `~/.sm` and every repo it names, cloned with the repos' links
pointed at the copies, never the originals, every copy and process
gone after each run. The harness is kept outside the repo.
1. A copy imported end to end: every project, title and shelf mark
   carried under the new ids, the worktrees moved into `wt/` where git
   lists them, the 2.x files deleted once verified, the real data
   untouched.
2. The import killed (SIGKILL, the whole group) at 21 moments from 1
   to 3 s, before the store opened, before the import committed and
   after 0 to 3 moves, then started again: every run ended whole.
3. Hostile states. A worktree folder gone (no stuck row, listed as v2
   lists it), a worktree on another drive's root (moved under the
   flavor's name with its title), a project folder moved (relocated
   from the picker), a read-only data folder and a half-written
   `registry.json` (the stuck page with its reason, Retry finishing
   the import once the cause is gone, nothing half imported), a store
   from a later version (refused, nothing written).
4. The way back to 2.x from a backup (the steps under the production
   cutover below): data files, git records and worktrees as before the
   update, opened by a 2.x build as one that never saw v3.
5. The cutover: the first start of a device signed out (v2's display
   settings kept), a real sign-in and enrollment on the dev hub (on a
   dev profile: a test flavor's origin isn't one Clerk's dev instance
   takes), the device revoked after; the terminal's doctor, listing and
   the rc's `sm shell init` line on the migrated data. v2's updater
   check passes a release-signed v3 bundle; the hand-off itself needs
   a signed prod build on the live v2 profile and isn't rehearsed.

What it found, each fixed with a proof: an emptied 2.x root holding only
Finder's `.DS_Store` was left behind (now swept); a data folder the
store can't be made in read only "Could not open the store" (now the
read-only words); a newer version's store sent you to a doctor that
can't repair it (its dialog now ends at updating the app). A move to a
new id drops the worktree's shelf snapshot rather than carry it, the
safe side, so work done on a shelved worktree before the import no
longer takes it off the shelf.

**The first run.** There is none (decision 19). A fresh install opens
on the home, which with no project is v2's orientation page: "A forest,
eventually.", its three steps and Add a project. Everything a first run
could ask for lives where it already does: the terminal `sm` is linked
from Settings (a start repairs a link only once there is one), the
sign-in on the account page, the agent hooks in Settings, a command
another program owns in Settings' CLI section.

**The terminal binary.** `effect/cli` commands over the engine, built
with `bun build --compile`, ad hoc signed in dev and signed in the
release. Startup and size are measured against the Go binary now and
then. `sm run` spawns the script as a child, forwards signals without
being ended by them, and re-raises the child's signal death so the
shell sees 128+n, except a crash signal, which only exits 128+n. At a
terminal, SIGINT, SIGQUIT, SIGTSTP, SIGHUP and SIGCONT already reach
the child (from the keyboard, the kernel and the shell), so they
aren't sent twice. Node can't tell those from a `kill` aimed at `sm`
alone, which therefore never reaches the script at a terminal. The app
stops runs by process tree, so it isn't affected. SIGTSTP stops `sm`
along with the child: ignoring it, as first planned, froze the terminal
on Ctrl-Z in a pty, since the shell waits for a stop that never comes.
`cliInstall` links the binary into
`~/.local/bin` as it does today.

**The terminal.** A `Terminals` service beside `ScriptRuns`, sharing
its pty layer, theme and replay gate. Each session is a resource in a
scope of its own, its release the kill chain. A session's identity is
`{deviceId, terminalId}`, and its owner is a worktree or the device. A
`terminals` RPC group: `list` as a stream, `open`, `close`, `attach`
(streaming the history, then the output, then the exit), `write` and
`resize`. A window reaches a peer's attach through `hub:watchPeer`, the
streaming twin of `hub:invokePeer` on the hop from the renderer to the
process holding the peer's link, so nothing passes through the cloud
hub. Output is batched as today and carried by the pull-based
stream, so backpressure is the stream's and there is no ack protocol
of our own. Every chunk carries a sequence number, so a reconnect
resumes from the history with no byte duplicated or lost. The size is
a host-side fact: a resize sets it for every attached client. The
history is a ring capped in bytes, with terminal query sequences
stripped so a replay does not answer them again. Sessions survive a
quit or a crash as saved history and a fresh shell in the same folder:
a terminal's ring is saved once its output has been quiet for two
seconds, and at least once a minute while it keeps changing (a flood,
an agent CLI's spinner), in one write each time, and again as it
closes. Both count awake time, monotonic: a clock step moves no save,
and a sleep spends none of the minute. A crash loses at most the
output since the last pause, a minute at worst, and an idle shell
writes nothing. Each save also
reads the shell's folder with `lsof`, in a subprocess. A terminal
must run a full-screen TUI (an agent CLI) smoothly, so the renderer
uses xterm's GPU renderer and does not render hidden terminals.
Nothing closes a shell behind its user: it closes when they close it,
when its worktree goes, or at a quit, where a shell with a foreground
job counts as busy like a script (the quit asks) and an idle one
closes and comes back as its saved history in the same folder. A remote device's terminals are
the same group over the device link, under the existing run-commands
consent. Custom scripts already run anything, so nothing new is
granted. In the UI, a worktree's terminals sit in a bottom drawer on
its page, with tabs. Free terminals are listed under each device in
the sidebar and reachable from the palette. `TerminalView` and
`TerminalTabsView` are views (`app/DESIGN.md`), and their containers
bind them to `terminals.attach`. The script console keeps its
run-a-slot model and becomes a tab in the same drawer.

**The keymap.** Every key the app answers is a named action in one
table (`packages/ui/src/lib/keymap.ts`): its words, its default chord,
the scope it acts in (the window, a page, a dialog), and in the
renderer what it does. A chord is written once, `Mod` standing for ⌘
on macOS and Ctrl elsewhere; on macOS the palette's, the commit's,
the palette's changes' and an edit's save also answer Ctrl in place of
⌘ (⌃K and ⌃↩, as v2's did; ⌃K not from a text field, where it is
kill-line), and no other chord has a Ctrl twin. Whatever shows or
hears a key reads the table: the shell's menu takes its accelerators
from the window, the palette's verbs and the rows' hints draw the
chord beside the verb, and one listener per window runs the rest, so
no component binds a key of its own. A control's own keys, the ones its role defines (Enter in
a field, the arrows in a list, Escape out of a dialog or an editor),
stay with the control. A text field keeps every key but a `Mod` chord,
and a focused terminal keeps every key a shell can be sent: it gives up
the ⌘ chords, which never reach one, and the terminal's toggle,
Ctrl+` on every platform since ⌘` cycles windows on macOS. The toggle
shows this window's drawer on a worktree's page and focuses it, and
hides it from inside. Bindings are the device's, in its client config
(`keybindings`, an action's id to its chord, a default kept as
nothing). Settings' Keyboard section lists every action, records a new
chord from the next keys pressed, refuses one that an action in an
overlapping scope holds and names it, and resets one or all. The
switches for what is still an experiment stay outside it: the dev
builds' theme keys, and Ctrl+Alt+Shift+L, which steps through the
palettes' hidden variants.

**The guide.** A user opens their own agent from the app with a
question about it, and it answers from the app's source and docs, and
shows them in the window they opened it from when the answer is
something to do there. It is a launch for a question, not a tour.
The agent is theirs, Claude Code or Codex in their chosen terminal, so
the app gains no chat and no running cost (`PRODUCT.md`: the app does
not own the agent), and the user asks in the agent, not in the app.
The Help menu has an item for each agent installed here ("Ask Claude
Code about the app"), which the window hands the menu as it hands its
launch tools (`menu:setGuideHarnesses`), and the palette is no way in.
The sidebar's footer has a question mark ("Ask a question about the
app") that opens the app's own menu above it: that heading, and an
item for each agent installed, by its name, with one agent too; with
none the question mark isn't there. The Help item tells the focused
window (`nav:showAround`), and the window and the sidebar's items both
ask its shell (`guide:start`), which mints a session: a random token
bound to that window alone. The shell has the host open
the agent (`session:guideLaunch`, the engine's `Agents.guide`) in the
data dir's `guide` folder, one folder so a harness asks to trust it
once: an `AGENTS.md` of the guide's instructions, a `CLAUDE.md` naming
it, and `source`, the running version's: a release's tag, cloned in
the terminal where its progress shows and again once the app updates,
or a dev build's checkout. The launch line hands the harness `sm guide mcp
--session <token>` as an MCP server with the app's data dir, its tools
allowed without asking, and the guide's role as the harness's
system-level instructions (`GUIDE_SYSTEM_PROMPT`, one text for both:
Claude Code's `--append-system-prompt`, Codex's
`developer_instructions`). No message is sent for the user: the agent
starts idle at its prompt and the user types their question. The
role says the user has a question, to answer it from `source/`,
`skills/` and the READMEs, to say when the answer isn't there, and to
offer a walkthrough only when asked; `AGENTS.md` has the tools and
how to show something in the window. The server is `effect/ai/McpServer` over
stdio, its tools the actions of `guideDrive:drive`, each with the
action's own Schema: `look` (the window's accessibility tree, a ref for
each control), `screenshot` (a PNG file), `navigate`, `click`, `fill`,
`press`, `point` (a ref and a sentence) and `clear`. The host passes
each to its shell (`shellCalls:guide`), which drives the window through
`webContents.debugger`, the DevTools protocol inside the process with
no port opened, attached at a session's first action and detached as
it ends, and attached again when something else detached it (opening
DevTools does). Some controls are the user's alone: a click or Enter or
Space on a control that deletes, removes, discards or signs out (its
`data-variant` ends in "destructive", or its name says so) is refused,
and so is any text or key in a terminal.
The window shows a band naming the agent with Stop while a session
lives, and a ring with the sentence beside what it points at. The
window keeps them on that control when a re-render replaces its
element, which takes the shell's mark with it: it finds the control
again by its tag, the attributes that name it and its text, the
nearest of two, and marks it (`renderer/lib/guideTarget.ts`). A
session ends when its window stops it, starts another or closes, the
agent quits (its server ends the session as it stops), or the app
quits, and its token is refused from then. A start for an agent whose
CLI isn't installed is refused in its words. `sm guide mcp` is a
verb of its own, not a change to the frozen ones, and the help leaves
it out, since only the app starts it.

**End-to-end encryption.** Whatever carries it, the LAN, the tunnel
or the hub's relay, what two devices say to each other is readable on
those two devices alone. Each device makes a static X25519 key pair at
every enrollment, keeps the private half beside its credential (the
same store, the same threat model) and registers the public half with
the hub. The hub's presence roster, already the word on which devices
exist, names each online device's key, and it is the trust root: no
key is pinned, and a device that enrolls again simply holds a new one,
which its peers note in the log. Every exchange is a Noise IK handshake
(`Noise_IK_25519_ChaChaPoly_SHA256`, `app/shared/crypto/noise.ts`): the
initiator seals to the key the roster names for the device it wants,
and the responder checks the initiator's static key, which the
handshake carries, against the roster's key for the device it claims
to be, so both ends are authenticated by the handshake itself. The
connectInfo ask and its answer are one handshake's two messages,
relayed as opaque strings. A direct connection runs a handshake of its
own over whichever candidate won, its ticket still gating the socket
before any crypto runs, and every frame after it, an RPC message or a
byte channel's chunk, is one ChaCha20-Poly1305 message under a counter
per direction, so a reconnect never reuses keys. The loopback stays
clear, since nothing on it leaves the machine. The hub sees its own traffic in clear (a socket's admission, the
presence roster, nacks, tickets and its HTTP routes), the connect ask
and answer as ciphertext addressed by two device ids, and no device
data at all, which goes over the tunnel or the LAN. The primitives are
the noble libraries; the one file above them follows the
specification's state machine and names (CipherState, SymmetricState,
HandshakeState), so it reads beside the spec, and its proof replays the
specification's test vector. A device enrolled before keys holds a
credential and no key, reads as signed out, and enrolls again: on its
own where the Clerk session lives, after one sign-in where it lapsed.
That is a step of the move to v3, decided in one place
(`app/renderer/hooks/account/useEnrollment.ts`).

**The flavors.** A build is one of three flavors: prod, what ships;
v3, the alphas (`v3.0.0-alpha.N`) and, once the feature set is frozen,
the betas (`v3.0.0-beta.N`) of 3.0.0, which the owner runs beside prod
until v3 merges into main; and dev,
`pnpm dev`. One table (`packages/engine/src/flavor.ts`) names every
per-build thing for each: the app's name as the OS shows it, the bundle
id, the deep-link scheme, the data dir and its name on a drive, the
userData folder (which holds the single-instance lock, the Clerk tokens
and the hub credential), and from that name the logs folder and the
keychain item, the terminal command and its alias (which name the
shell hook and the agent hooks), and the releases the updater offers.
The packaging, the terminal's build, the release workflow and the
running app read that table and nothing else. A packaged build's flavor
is baked in when it is built, from the tag, and main reads it in one
place (`main/flavor.ts`) and hands it to the host, so no file of one
flavor is reachable from another and three builds are three devices.
A v3 build follows the v3 prereleases alone (alpha, beta and rc of
3.0.0), from GitHub's list: a full release is the prod app's bundle. A
prod build is never offered a v3 one. On either line only a version
above the running one is offered, in semver's order (alpha before beta
before rc before the full release), so a build never moves back a
stage: a prerelease build takes any later prerelease or full release,
and a full build full releases alone. The v3 build's terminal command is `smv3`, installed
beside `sm` with its own links and hooks, so dogfooding includes the
terminal and its link from Settings without touching the installed
`sm`. It starts on an empty `~/.smv3`, which is the point: the migration
imports what v2 left in a data dir, and a v3 build has none.

**Nuke everything.** The Danger zone's button returns a build to a
fresh install: the next start opens the empty home with no device, no
project, no setting and nothing remembered. It runs in two halves.
The live half needs the store and a running app: the host stops every
script and terminal, removes every worktree the app made (forced, so
leases, teardowns and the empty managed folders go as in any removal;
external worktrees and the projects' repos stay), and takes this
flavor's terminal command, shell hooks and agent hooks off the
machine; then the shell revokes this device on the hub, drops its
credential, turns launch at login off and writes a marker into
userData naming the data dir, the logs and the data dir's pointer.
The other half runs at the next start, right after the single-instance
lock and before anything opens a file: it deletes what the marker
names and everything in userData (the Clerk tokens, the hub
credential, the client config, Chromium's storage), the marker last,
so nothing is deleted under a running store, and a start that dies
halfway finds the marker and deletes the rest. A signed build's
keychain item is recreated once its marker in userData is gone. Every
step reads what is there now, so a second run after an interrupted
one finishes the job. It refuses only for a trap that would take the
user's files with it: a project inside the data dir, or the home
folder as the data dir. Otherwise it finishes what it can and lists
what it could not, with why (an offline hub leaves the device on the
account page of the others), before it restarts. It never touches
terrier, another flavor's files, or the plain dev instance's profiles
nested in its userData.

**The darwin helper.** A Go program with six verbs: clone a tree
(preserving mode and mtime, as the verifier needs), read and clear
flags, list and strip xattrs, APFS private size, filesystem type, and
`lstat` with nanosecond times (what git's index records, which the
clone checkout's verifier compares, added in step 2). Each
takes a root or a list and streams NDJSON results, validated with
Schema on the engine side. Built and bundled beside `file-sync`.

## Vocabulary

What a mechanism is called today, and what replaces it. For a thread
reading older code, or a review saying "this should not exist
anymore".

| Today | Where | Replacement |
| --- | --- | --- |
| impl slots, `set*Impl`, global setters set at boot | `host/ipc`, `main` | services and Layers |
| `stop()` latches, the hand-ordered `before-quit` chain, the 15-second `app.exit` backstop | `main/index.ts`, every subsystem | `Scope` finalizers; the layer graph's shutdown |
| `createLimiter`, `singleFlight`, `ttlCache`, `coalesce` | `shared`, `host/lib/util` | `Semaphore`, `Effect.cached`, `Cache`, `Stream.debounce` |
| `supervisor.ts` backoff ladder, `heartbeat.ts`, `Promise.race` timeouts | `shared/remote`, `shared/ipc/socket` | `Schedule` with `Effect.retry`, `Effect.timeout`, the socket's own liveness |
| `channels.ts` credit-based flow control | `shared/ipc/socket` | pull-based `Stream` backpressure |
| `fakeClock` | `test/lib/checkKit.mts` | `TestClock` |
| `registerContract`, `allContractModules`, the triple listing in `client.ts`, the hand-kept `HostApi` | `shared/ipc` | `RpcGroup`s and a generated client |
| error message prefixes matched in the renderer | `shared/errors.ts` | `Schema.TaggedError`, branching on `_tag` |
| the `moves.ts` cancel registry, the per-page `AbortController` rotation, `HandlerContext.signal` | `host/lib/sync`, `main/ipc/register.ts` | RPC interruption |
| broadcasts and listener `Set`s | `shared/ipc`, `host` | streaming RPCs |
| `queryKeys.ts`, `onMutationResolved`, `movesHostState` as a cache ping, push handlers writing into the cache | `renderer/lib`, `main/ipc` | streams and atoms |
| `cliDelegate.ts`, `cliRunner.ts`, `sm --json` from the host | `host/ipc`, `main/electron` | importing the engine |
| `lockFile.ts`, `jsonFile.ts`, `flock` and atomic renames, the state watcher's echo suppression, `noteSelfWrite`, `cliChildCount` | `host/lib`, `cli/state.go` | the SQLite store |
| `test/run.mts`, the harness half of `checkKit`, per-proof lefthook globs | `app/test`, `lefthook.yml` | vitest and `vitest related` |
| `console.*`, `logFailure` | everywhere in main, host, shared | `Logger` and `Tracer`, electron-log as the sink |
| electron-store, `process.env` reads | `main` | `KeyValueStore`, `Config` |
| the Electron IPC bridge, `preloadTransport.ts`, `web/ipc/loopback.ts`, `localHost.ts` gating | `main`, `web`, `renderer/lib` | one RPC client; "local" is a device in the list |
| the host-boundary proof | `app/test` | the package graph |

## Process

Same mechanics as v2, which took 99 PRs over 25 days with 8 merges from
`main`, two test prereleases and one rc. Expect two to three times
that.

- `release/v3` is cut from `main`. PRs target `release/v3`, one worktree per PR,
  finalized and reviewed like any other PR.
- `main` is merged into `release/v3` every few days; conflicts are resolved on
  `release/v3`, never by rebasing `release/v3`.
- At each step's end, `main` is merged into `release/v3`, every PR
  that landed on `main` is reviewed against the merge result, a step
  report goes to the owner, and the next step's work waits for the
  owner's check-in.
- Path identity and host identity are boundary policies every
  consumer shares. After a failure of either, every consumer is
  grepped, across linked data folders, project and custom paths, both
  volumes, denied folders, public and Enterprise GitHub hosts, and both
  Macs.
- Adapters keep the transport's status and the peer's identity in what
  they raise. Lifecycle transitions are logged inbound and outbound. A
  push burst is proved by a bounded call count and zero cancellations.
- Prereleases `v3.0.0-alpha.N`, then `v3.0.0-beta.N` once the owner
  freezes the feature set, are cut from the branch (the release
  workflow signs prereleases from any branch; a full release must be
  on `main`). They build the v3 flavor, point at the dev hub until
  cutover and are dogfooded on every device. `v3.0.0` itself is the
  production cutover, a prod release. A prerelease's notes, by the
  owner's rule: one line saying it is a testing build and not for
  serious work, then a list of the changes, nothing else.
- A prerelease tags only a SHA whose latest `Walks` run passed, checked
  before anything else. At the tip about to be tagged, only a yes from
  this lets the cut go on; a no (a run still going is one) or an error
  stops it:
  ```sh
  gh run list --workflow walks.yml --commit "$(git rev-parse HEAD)" --limit 1 \
  ```
