         Done for the shell: its files are its own

      8. `registerContract` (`shared/ipc`): the channel-string layer

      10. The renderer's `window.api`: 133 reads in 62 files. 71 in 36
         files are the shell's own surface (window, menu, dialogs,
         shell, nav, migration, account, client config, releases, the
         port forwards, and the build's facts), which decision 6 keeps

         each in `AddProjectModal.tsx`, `AppChrome.tsx`, `AppShell.tsx`,
         `DevThemeHotkeys.tsx`, `ClerkGate.tsx`, `AddExistingForm.tsx`,
         `ConfigureForm.tsx`, `FileViewer.tsx`, `AccountSection.tsx`,
         `DeviceRegistry.tsx`, `DataLocationSection.tsx` (2),
         `FolderPickerModal.tsx`, `SidebarHeader.tsx`, `useClerkAccount.ts`,

         `useReleases.ts`, `useTheme.tsx`, `index.tsx`, `agentWatch.ts`,
         `localHost.ts`, `newWindow.ts`, `queryKeys.ts`,
         `sharedSettingsSync.ts`. The leftovers are 27
         host calls in 13 files (`boot.tsx`, `DangerZone.tsx`,
         `WorktreeDetail.tsx`, `useSharing.ts`, `useSettingsSave.ts`,
         `useMirrors.ts`, `useMoveWorktree.ts`, `usePullProgress.ts`,
         `useVillagerData.ts`, `useVillagers.ts`,
         `sharedSettingsSync.ts`, `scriptRuns.ts`,
         `worktreeLifecycle.ts`) and 35 reads of the bare global as the
         local host's api in 22 files (`useHostScope.tsx` 7,
         `index.tsx` 3, `boot.tsx`, `DeviceTabs.tsx`,
         `hostForestScope.ts`, `useDeviceApi.ts` and `remote/devices.ts`
         2 each, and one each in `RemoteScope.tsx`,
         `AddToDeviceSubmenu.tsx`, `ProjectGroupActions.tsx`,
         `TidyForest.tsx`, `useSettingsSave.ts`, `usePortForwards.ts`,
         `usePullProgress.ts`, `useRemoteDevices.ts`, `useScriptRuns.ts`,
         `useUpdater.ts`, `queryKeys.ts`, `routePaths.ts`,
         `runtime/Api.ts`, `runtime/atoms.ts`, `router.tsx`), which take
         the client's `Api` service instead. Counted at 548e9714a,
         before main's merge.
         The `Api` service then reaches both surfaces through clients
         typed by the contracts, and `buildClient.ts` and `client.ts`
         (`shared/ipc`), which build `window.api` and the service's
         Promise surface today, go with it (8).
         Recounted at the start of the work (273281382 on
         `release/v3`): 141 reads in 67 files, the guide and the pull
         request features having added eight since the count above.
         It goes in three: the global, the stores (11), then the
         Promise call sites onto typed runtime calls, which takes
         `buildApi`, `buildClient` and `client.ts` with it.
         The global is gone: no module reads or sets `window.api`. A
         page's entry builds its api and hands it to the boot, which
         puts it in a React context (`useApi`,
         `renderer/lib/runtime/pageApi.tsx`) and hands it to what it
         starts outside React; the host-scoped calls that read the
         global go through the device-scoped api, and the bare reads
         take the page's. Tools outside the page (the lab's scripts,
         weblab steps) read `window.__smLab.api`, which a dev build,
         the fake host and an app started with `--sm-lab` hand over and
         no module of the app reads (`test/host-boundary.mts`).
         Done for the global: no module reads or sets `window.api`. Each
         entry builds the page's api (`renderer/lib/runtime/pageApi.tsx`:
         the shell's facts, the device id and the `Api` service's
         surface) and hands it to the boot; `mountWindow` puts it in a
         React context that containers and hooks read with `useApi()`,
         and `startApp` passes it to what it starts (the device and
         shared settings syncs, the agent watch, the villager moves, the
         stores, made per api). The local device id reaches the atoms
         through the registry (`localDeviceIdAtom`), the route tree is
         built for the page's flavor, and the lab's scripts find the api
         in React's tree (`lab/reliability/pageApi.mts`). The calls are
         still the Promise surface.
         Done for the calls: the `Api` service builds each contract
         module as an `EffectClient` over the link its scope rides
         (`effectClient`), and a peer's (`peerApi`, its host modules
         only) over the hub hop. Every api, the page's and a device's,
         carries `run` (the client runtime's, so a call ends with it,
         and its Promise rejects with the error the call failed with:
         the contract error's class, `RemoteCallError` with its
         message), `listen` (a stream until the stop it returns), and
         `pushes` and `view` (a push or a view of one of its modules, by
         name, as a stream). 246 Promise call sites in 99 files (231 in
         96 of the renderer, 15 in the lab's scripts) run their calls
         with `api.run(...)`, fire-and-forget ones too (`void
         api.run(...)`); a helper that took a Promise-returning call
         takes one returning an effect and runs it. 22 push
         subscriptions in 16 files are `listen(pushes(module, key))`,
         and the terminal's `attach` is `listen(view(...))`. Every query
         (70 in 51 files) hands React Query's `signal` to `run`, so a
         fetch a push supersedes or a page drops interrupts its call,
         and the far side stops its work; the hub hop forwards the
         interruption to the peer (`hub:invokePeer`, desktop and web).
         A read outside React (`fetchQuery`, `ensureQueryData`, ten of
         them) that joins a page's fetch asks again when the page going
         cancels it (`throughCancel`, `lib/queryClientOptions.ts`).
         `window.api` reads 0, host bypasses 0, bare-global reads 0;
         `buildApi`, `buildClient.ts` and `client.ts` are deleted, the
         fake host's table type (`FixtureHandlers`) lives with it, and
         `ClientTransport` and `linkTransport` stay for the host's peer
         reach (`PeerReach`) and the hub bridge. The answer's decode,
         which `buildClient` repeated for a non-local transport, is the
         link's RPC client's: one from a far end on another build that
         this build does not read fails the call or ends the view as
         `UnreadableAnswerError`, naming the call, and a push is dropped
         and the channel heard again (`rpcLink`, `pnpm test
         client-validation`).
      11. The `useSyncExternalStore` stores, 12 files. Four hold the
         page's own state and stay (`useTheme`, `useViewport`,
         `useChangesPicks`, and `store/pullRequestDrafts`, this
         window's unsent pull request form). Eight hold a host's or the link's state
         and become atoms on the client's runtime: `store/scriptRuns`
         with `useScriptRuns`, `store/worktreeLifecycle`,
         `useProjectGitFetching`, `useHubStatus`, `lib/remote/devices`
         with `useRemoteDevices` and `useCommandAccess`.
         Done: the eight are atoms on the page's registry, read
         through the atom hooks, and the four stay, so four files of the
         twelve still use `useSyncExternalStore`. A device's script runs
         (`hooks/scripts/scriptRuns.ts`), its worktree lifecycle
         (`hooks/worktrees/useWorktreeLifecycle.ts`) and its git fetches
         are atom families over the device's pushes (`hostPushes`,
         `atoms.ts`); the hub's status is an atom over its pushes and a
         seed, which every snapshot reaches the device sync through
         (`hubStatusAtom`, `remoteDeviceSync.ts`); and the device list,
         which combines that status with the account's device list (a
         query, which stays one), is an atom the sync writes
         (`remoteDevicesAtom`), read by `useRemoteDevices` and
         `useCommandAccess` through selectors. What must hear a push with
         no reader is mounted: this machine's runs, lifecycle and fetches
         by the boot where it has a host, a peer's lifecycle from its
         session landing until the account goes, the hub's status by the
         sync. A peer's runs and fetches start on first read, and what
         the atoms keep of a peer starts over with the account
         (`accountsLeftAtom`) and, for its lifecycle and fetches, on a
         new session. The removal follow-ups and the carry-over toasts
         moved from the boot into the lifecycle atom, which reads the
         page's query client from the registry (`queryClientAtom`).
         Proved in `test/push-atoms.mts`.
         Two more external stores, outside the twelve: the agent watch's
         waiting agents (`lib/agentWatch.ts`), drawn from the hosts'
         worktree views, is an atom the watch writes on the page's
         registry (`waitingAgentsAtom`); the Visitors album's log
         (`lib/villagers/visitLog.ts`) is the page's own record, kept in
         localStorage and known to no device, and stays. Of the
         fourteen, nine moved and five stay.
      12. Service modules named in lowercase, which the lint rules for
         services do not read: `host/lib/hostPushes.ts`, `views.ts`,
         `engine.ts`, `terrier.ts`, `sharing.ts`,
         `git/backgroundFetch.ts`, `scripts/pty.ts`,
         `scripts/persistence.ts`, `host/direct/cloudflared.ts`,
         `host/socket/loopback.ts`, `server.ts`, `host/mirror/daemon.ts`,
         `main/ipc/shellLink.ts`. Each is named for its service; the two
         under `scripts/` reach `node:fs` and convert with it
         (persistence goes with 7). `VillagerData`, `GitWatcher` and
         `Ports` went first, onto `FileSystem`, and `scripts/persistence.ts`
         with 7, as `scripts/OrphanSweep.ts`. With 8: `HostPushes.ts`,
         `socket/DeviceLink.ts` (the link's `server.ts`) and
         `main/ipc/ShellLink.ts`. With 15: `host/lib/BackgroundFetch.ts`.
         Done: the last eight are `host/lib/Views.ts`, `Engine.ts`,
         `Terrier.ts`, `Sharing.ts`, `scripts/ScriptRuns.ts`,
         `host/direct/Tunnel.ts`, `host/socket/Loopback.ts` and
         `host/mirror/MirrorDaemon.ts`. `ScriptRuns` checks the login shell
         on `FileSystem`; the shell's passwd entry, which no platform
         service reads, is a helper's (`scripts/loginShell.ts`).
      13. `logFailure` (`shared/log.ts`), which the Vocabulary gives to
         `Logger`: `host/process/wires.ts`, `handlers.ts`,
         `main/ipc/handlers.ts`, `web/ipc/register.ts`. Each step it
         guards becomes an effect that logs its failure
         (`Effect.catchCause` with `Effect.logWarning`) and carries on;
         it goes with the Promise code around it.
         Done: its five steps are effects that log a failure as a
         warning through the app's logger and carry on: the direct
         listener's and the tunnel's on the host's graph, waited for as
         before, and the account steps, the host's two and the shell's
         two, on their own (`warnOnFailure`), since they need no graph
         and a sign-out can run as the app quits. `logFailure` is gone. A warning now carries
         the cause as Effect prints it rather than its message alone.
      14. The shell's hold on its host (`main/hostProcess.ts`) starts at
         import: it registers the shell's calls and opens their port
         server in a scope nothing closes, and forks the host from
         module state. It becomes a scoped layer in the shell's graph
         (`main/shellLayer.ts`), whose closing stops the server and the
         child, as the graph's shutdown is the quit (EFFECT.md,
         section 5).
         Done: the hold is the top layer of the shell's graph, so the
         quit closes it first. Its acquiring registers the shell's
         calls, opens their port server in its scope and forks the
         host; its closing asks the host to quit and kills one that
         does not within the deadline (`Effect.timeoutOrElse`). The
         shell's link to the host is made on first use, so a window
         that asks before the hold is up waits for the host's address.
         A shell graph that fails to build now takes the host with it;
         it fails only on a data-dir setting the shell's own boot
         check refuses first.
      15. `host/lib/git` beside the engine's `Git`: every worktrees
         handler calls the host's git library (`app/host/lib/git`:
         refs, changes, merge, history, sync), and nothing in the host
         imports the engine's `Git`, whose `overwriteFromUpstream` has
         no caller outside its test. Main's #799 added `resetHard`,
         `headAt` and `resolveCommit` on the host side only. The
         handlers come to call the engine's `Git`, which takes what the
         host's library has that it lacks, and `host/lib/git` goes.
         Six pull requests by area, each landed before the next: refs,
         remotes and branches; worktrees, clone and init; operation
         state, changes and diffs; hunks, commit and stash; history,
         merge and sync; then the last raw `run` callers, and the
         library deleted.
         The engine's methods that read differently from the host's
         (the sync's rebase-or-merge, `commitDiff` on a merge,
         `resetSoft` across a merge, `overwriteFromUpstream`,
         `fileDiff`, `discard`, `restoreDiscard`) have no caller
         outside the engine's own tests, written ahead of this
         switch-over, so they take the app's behaviour as they are
         reached and `sm` reads nothing different. The engine's spawn
         slots now hold only the read-only probes a row fans out over
         (`SLOTTED_SUBCOMMANDS`), so a write that runs the user's hooks
         (a commit's, a checkout's, any ref update's
         reference-transaction) or waits on a remote never holds up the
         rows' probes; the host had no slots. A probe still runs what
         the user configured for it under its slot, as before (a
         status's fsmonitor, a diff's external driver).
         Refs, remotes and branches done: their callers in the host
         call the engine's `Git`, which took `resolveCommit`, `headAt`,
         `upstreamName`, `countCommits` and `ownCommitCounts`, and
         `listBranches` reads git's short names as the app's did (no
         caller in `sm`). `refs.ts`, `branches.ts`, `remotes.ts`,
         `ignoreRules.ts` and `isGitRepo` are deleted. The engine's
         `BranchNotMergedError` is the contract's, so the branch
         manager's force-delete prompt still keys on it. A git the
         engine ran fails in git's own words wherever the host relays
         it: on the wire (`hostCallFailure`, both registrars) and in a
         log or a status (`errorWordsOf`, in place of `errorMessageOf`
         across the host), `pnpm test git-failures`. One change the
         replacement implies: an overlapping fetch of a project is the
         engine's one fetch, which goes on while any caller still waits,
         where the host's stopped for all when its first caller was
         interrupted.
         Worktrees, clone and init done: the engine's `Git` makes a new
         repository (`git/repositories.ts`: `checkDestination`, `clone`
         with git config pairs for the gh credential helper the host
         passes, and `createRepository`), the auto-pull sweep reads
         `aheadBehind`, the mirror's branch check `listWorktrees`, the
         wipe `pruneWorktrees`, and a checkout's id is the engine's
         `worktreeIdFromPath`. The two row lookups that were never git
         (`listWorktrees`, `findWorktreeIdentity`) joined `findProject`
         in `host/lib/projects`, and `BackgroundFetch` left the folder
         for `host/lib/BackgroundFetch.ts`. `clone.ts` and `init.ts` are
         deleted; `worktrees.ts` keeps the history reads until the
         history pull request.
         Operation state, changes and diffs done: the engine's `Git`
         reads and sees through an operation under way
         (`git/operation.ts`: `readOperation`, `refuseMidOperation`,
         continue, abort, `resolveConflict`), and its index area takes
         the app's undo (refused mid-operation, a merge on top taken off
         whole) and `resetHard`, whose refusals now come before its
         status read, so a refused reset never cuts a status short with
         its index.lock held (the race behind #831's one failed run).
         `commitDiff` gives a merge its first-parent patch. The host's
         changes page, diffs, discards and banner call it; the files
         still to move (hunks, commit, stash, history, merge) take the
         engine's index lock (`withIndexLock`) so every write to an
         index still waits its turn. A refusal reads as before
         (`GitRefusedError`, the host's words). `changes.ts`,
         `operation.ts` and `diff.ts` are deleted.
         Hunks, commit and stash done: the engine's `Git` reads and
         discards a file's hunks (`git/hunks.ts`), commits the page's
         picks (`git/commit.ts`, the index put back when git refuses the
         commit) and keeps the stashes (`git/stash.ts`), each refusal in
         the host's words. A file it can't read fails with the
         platform's error rather than the host's own. `hunks.ts`,
         `commit.ts` and `stash.ts` are deleted.
         History, merge and sync done: the engine's `Git` merges
         (`git/merging.ts`: the preview, the four ways, the merge kept
         on its conflicts), rewrites history (`git/rewriting.ts`: revert,
         cherry-pick, reword, squash), and syncs as the app did (a rebase
         only where it is safe, the merge with `--ff` by the upstream's
         name, `SyncConflictsError`, the overwrite through `resetHard`);
         its `listCommits` takes the app's search, start and first-parent
         stats as options, and `readBranchHistory` is the History tab's.
         `history.ts`, `merge.ts`, `sync.ts` and `worktrees.ts` are
         deleted, `core.ts` is left for the raw callers. A window here
         gaining focus reads every project's rows again in the host
         (`BackgroundFetch.setWindowFocused`), so the window no longer
         sends `worktrees:reread`, which a peer's still does. The one
         behaviour change: a file that starts with a byte order mark
         keeps it through a hunk discard (the engine's runner reads
         git's output with the mark kept, which both runners had
         stripped).
         Done: the last raw callers (the mirror's git state, the
         clone from a peer, hygiene, carry-over, the data dir move and
         the GitHub helpers) run git through the engine's `Git` (`run`,
         and `runLenient` for the reads with an answer either way), and
         `host/lib/git` is deleted. Six pull requests: #829, #831, #833,
         #836, #839 and this one's.
         #834's follow-up rode protocol 13 (#856): `worktrees:reread`
         is gone, and `git:sweep` says whether it is a renewal, so the
         host reads every project's rows again on a peer's first ask
         and not on each renewal (`pnpm test working-tree-edits`).
         Decided by the owner: `listCommits` counts every merge
         against its first parent, what it brought in, for the app and
         `sm` alike (the listing and the status card, whose `--json`
         carries a merge's numbers where it had none), and the
         `firstParentStats` option is gone. The search and the start
         stay options, the app's alone.
      Done, all fifteen. What it took:
      `window.api` and `buildApi`, `buildClient.ts` and `client.ts` are
      gone (246 call sites in 99 files run as typed calls); nine of
      fourteen external stores are atoms and five stay the page's own;
      `host/lib/git` is gone (18 files, about 3,700 lines), the engine's
      `Git` the one git the engine, `sm` and the host run; the helper
      modules the Vocabulary replaces (`implSlot`, `ttlCache`,
      `coalesce`, `limit`, `singleFlight`, the cancel registry,
      `atomicJson`, `registerContract.ts`, `logFailure`) are gone, each
      for what its item's Done line names (`cacheTtl.ts` stays, the
      answers-only `Cache`'s time to live); and every service module is
      named for its service. Three behaviour
      changes, each said in its pull request: overlapping fetches of a
      project share the engine's one fetch, which goes on while any
      caller waits; a file that starts with a byte order mark keeps
      it through a hunk discard; and `sm` counts a merge's +/- against
      its first parent, as the app does (item 15 above).
      Still open: two paths no proof drives, the data folder's worktree
      repair (`host/lib/dataDirMove.ts`) and the shell's report of each
      window's focus (`main/hostProcess.ts`, which Electron's events
      reach and no proof can).
      Also open, in the lab's tooling (found by the terminal drawer's
      polish, #917):
      - `pnpm scenes:compare` can stall: its
        `page.evaluate(() => document.fonts.ready)` in `shoot`
        (`app/lab/scene-compare/compare.mts`) sometimes never returns,
        on either side and in any scene (guideParts on the base and
        palettePicked on the head, on Lychee), and the run then sits
        idle with no output. Racing that wait against a few seconds
        from the Node side let the run finish.
      - weblab's `shot` on an attached Electron window drops `:hover`
        by the time it captures, so a hover state can't be shot that
        way. Forcing the state through CDP
        (`CSS.forcePseudoState`) and shooting with Playwright works.
      - Done: the lab's weblab recipes assumed weblab hands `start` a
        `PORT` (`app/lab/devices.md`'s recipes, and
        `app/scripts/dev-device.mts`, which read it before
        `SHIGOMORI_DEBUG_PORT`), which sent agent threads into
        debug-port startup failures and retries (at least ten, Oct 8
        to 16). weblab runs `start` as written and supplies no `PORT`.
        Every recipe now sets `SHIGOMORI_DEBUG_PORT` to the port its
        `attach` and `address` name, and `pnpm device` reads only
        that; checked by launching `devices.md`'s recipe on a fresh
        profile. The siblings swept (every `start`, `PORT` and
        `SHIGOMORI_DEBUG_PORT` under `app/lab` and `app/scripts`): the
        fake host and web client take their ports from `.env.ports`,
        and the walks, reliability and smoke set the debug port in the
        environment they spawn, so none needed a change.
      A maintainability review of the branch at 43b560f01 (2026-10-15,
      against main) found the architecture sound and three seams, kept
      here with their owners:
      - An interrupted worktree move strands its metadata under the
        old id: `worktrees/moving.ts` runs `git worktree move`, prunes
        the old parents, and only then rekeys the registry and the
        worktree data, each in its own transaction, so a crash or an
        interrupt between leaves git on the new path and the marks
        (autoPull, titles) on the old id; `removing.ts` rekeys the two
        in separate transactions too. Reproduced with a blocking rmdir
        on the sandbox PATH and a fibre interrupt (the test is kept
        outside the repo). The migration's worktree move walks the
        same code, so the migration hardening makes the move
        recoverable (the intended move recorded first, finished at the
        next start or by the doctor) and adds the interruption tests.
        Done: the move's record and its recovery ("Commit points",
        under Designs).
      - The host's worktree handlers (`host/ipc/modules/worktrees.ts`:
        relocate, rename, delete, deleteStack) still orchestrate the
        guards, the scripts and terminals, the busy markers, the
        removal announcements and the mirrors themselves. The
        simplification pass's host PR moves that into a host-level
        worktree service with thin adapters, keeping host state in the
        host.
      - The GitHub merge-settings query, decoder and mapping exist
        twice, in `host/lib/githubCli/GithubCli.ts` and the engine's
        `GitHub.ts`. The simplification pass's engine PR keeps one,
        with the host's caching, gating and failure words as they are.
        Done: `GitHub.repoMergeConfig` is the one read, the engine's
        `mergeSettings` and the host's hour-long cache built on it.
      - `app/DESIGN.md`'s container rule still said a diff stays a
        query; it now states decision 18. Done with this record.
      A maintainability review by Sol at ae8982c17 (2026-10-18), in
      three slices (the engine and contracts, the host, the renderer),
      found four P1s and two P2s in the engine slice, each recorded
      here as its PR lands:
      - The updater's removals answered success whatever `rm` did, so
        a confirmed "nothing newer" whose clear of the staged update
        was refused answered up to date with the update still staged.
        A removal a later step needs (the staged update's clear, the
        extract folder before an extraction, the swap's scratch names
        before a rename) fails with `RemoveFailed` and its path now,
        and the leftovers nothing needs gone are pruned on purpose
        (`updater/files.ts` `prune`; `Updater.test.ts`); the clear
        after a finished install, or of a staged copy no newer than the
        build, is one of those. The class's siblings (a fallible step
        exposed as an infallible one), left, each a recovery or
        bookkeeping step whose failure no later step reads: the
        relaunch after a failed install (its install's own failure is
        reported), the host's publish of the loopback file a terminal
        finds it by, the migration's `git worktree repair` after its
        moves, and the tunnel's pid file for its orphan sweep.
      - A project's removal deleted and re-keyed the settings and
        worktree data tables itself, a second writer beside their
        owners. It calls `Config` and `WorktreeData` now:
        `forgetProject`, and `rekeyProject` for the hand-off to
        terrier, beside each table's other writes (`Registry.ts`;
        `Terrier.test.ts` hands both over). The class, storage another
        domain owns written from an orchestrator, left: the listing's
        shelving, which writes the Registry's marks, shelf snapshots
        and unshelve times in a transaction of its own
        (`worktrees/listing.ts`); a move's record of `wt_moves`, which
        is `WtFolder`'s (`worktrees/kept.ts`); and the data-folder
        move's empty write to the store's `changes` for the write lock
        (`host/lib/dataDirMove.ts`).
      - Engine types repeated the contracts' field for field. The
        harness status is the contract's, and the agent session,
        `sm worktrees list`'s rows, the carry-over report and a
        worktree's disk usage are the contract's with only what the
        engine adds or holds stronger (the waits a session keeps, the
        agent fields always present, the project's name, no id). The
        class's siblings left, each its own shape, not a copy: the
        scripts list, the launcher rows, a pull request's summary and
        `RunningScript`, which shares only a name with the contract's,
        and the shelf snapshot, whose schema the contracts don't
        export as a type.
      - Discard snapshots were named and ordered by the wall clock: a
        backward step ranked the one just made oldest and pruned it at
        once, and two worktrees in one millisecond wrote one ref. Each
        is now a number one past the repository's highest, created by
        `update-ref`'s compare-and-set (a ref taken meanwhile sends it
        to the next), and the 40 highest are kept. The compare-and-set's
        "must not exist" is git's empty old value, which a sha256
        repository takes too; the forty zeros it replaced are sha1's
        alone, and the mirror's branch create used them as well
        (`git/gitIndex.ts`; `Git.changes.test.ts`, on a clone of this
        repository). The refs a wall-clock build named are numbers
        below the new ones and age out with them.
        The class's siblings: the harness hooks file's temp sibling
        was named by the millisecond alone, so the app and a terminal
        `sm` writing at once shared it, and it is named by a random id
        now (`Agents.ts`). Left, each judged harmless or recorded:
        restored terminals ordered by their opening's wall time (a tab
        order only), the orphan sweep's birth check (a step past its
        slack leaks an orphan, never kills another process), the hub's
        eviction by last seen (the Worker's clock), the PR form's
        attachments removed after a day by mtime, and a description's
        last writer winning across devices (by design).
      - Land skipped its guards after a merge GitHub answered at once,
        so a commit made while gh merged went with the branch (Commit
        points); refused after its merge, it says what a removal says
        ("push them to a new PR, or pass --force or --keep-branch"),
        since the PR has merged. The app's finish called `done`
        forced, trusting the merged-PR box, so a commit made since the
        merge went the same way; it runs `done`'s proof now, and its
        refusal is the box's toast (`engineOps.ts`; `pnpm test
        finish-branch`, on a clone of this repository). Checked: a
        stack land plans its removals
        again after its merge. Left: a plain `rm` deletes its branch
        (`deleteBranchOnRemove`) without the commits guard a stack
        `rm` runs, as the Go `sm`'s did (decision 13, the owner's
        call); a mirror's stop removes its copy forced, by design; and
        a transfer's teardown checks its receipt before its cleanup,
        the host slice's first finding, its own PR.
      - Left for the re-rank, its class: words the terminal and the app
        share that tell the app's user to pass a terminal flag, with no
        way past the refusal in the app. `done`'s refusals
        (`not-merged`, `unpushed`, `head-unread`, `MergeUnverified`:
        "pass --force", `landing/documents.ts`), a removal's (the
        uncommitted changes and the unreadable check, "pass --force";
        changes during cleanup, "pass --force --skip-cleanup",
        `worktrees/documents.ts`) and an apply's ("pass --force",
        `Dirty.ts`).
      - A setup or teardown script waited for its output's end, not its
        shell's exit, so `pnpm dev &` in one kept the create or the
        removal running for good: the child holds the pipes. The
        shell's exit ends the run now, what came within 200 ms of it
        still reported, and the service reads the pipes on, dropping
        what comes, so the child never blocks on them or meets a
        closed one (`Lifecycle.ts`, `processes.ts` `outputUntilExit`;
        `Lifecycle.test.ts`, and a create on a clone of this
        repository whose setup is the owner's, `pnpm install && pnpm
        run postinstall`, its postinstall leaving a watcher). The class,
        a pipe's lifetime taken for a process's, had siblings wherever
        a command runs in a group of its own and its output is read to
        the end: git, which hands a hook its stdout and stderr (a
        hook's child hung `commit` and `push`), the hooks a clone runs
        itself, an editor's command, and every `capture` and `exec`.
        Each command's exit ends its group now, as closing its scope
        did once the pipes ended, and only then are they read to the
        end, so nothing it wrote is lost (`readToExit`; `Git.test.ts`,
        `CloneCheckout.test.ts`). That ends a hook's background work on
        purpose too (a post-commit `ctags &`), as the scope's close
        already ended one that left the pipes alone. Left for the
        re-rank, a parity difference: the Go `sm` ran git with its
        output in buffers and no group of its own, so it waited for
        such a child to end and left it running (`cli/gitx.go` on
        `main`). Left, the terminal's half: the terminal `sm` prints
        its result once the script exits, then stays up until the
        child it left ends, since Bun waits for a loop the child's
        pipes hold (`runMain` exits by itself only on a signal or a
        failure). An exit a moment after the command can't tell those
        pipes from `sm`'s own output still queued for a slow reader,
        which Bun's stream state doesn't show, so it would cut that
        output (`sm worktrees list --json | less`); and an unreferenced
        child keeps its pipes. It needs `sm` to know a script left its
        pipes held, or the scripts' output on something no loop waits
        for. A custom
        launcher's command, started in the group of the `sh` that
        launched it, went with that group as the launch's scope closed;
        the launch leaves it now (`Open.ts`; `Launchers.test.ts`).
      The host slice (2026-10-18), found three P1s and two P2s:
      - A transfer's source teardown checked its receipt, then ran the
        source's cleanup scripts and a forced delete, so an edit made
        to the source while a teardown script ran went with it. The
        removal on the source's own device checks it again after the
        cleanup, right before the delete; a pull carries what moved to
        the peer's delete (`expect`, optional since the call is every
        delete's, not a transplant's alone; a build on another protocol
        never links, so no older peer drops it) (`pnpm test
        sync-transfer`, a teardown script held on a gate while the
        source is edited). The class,
        a check that grants a later destructive step that can wait:
        land's guards and the app's finish (both #1015) were its
        others. A cleanup script that writes files the capture holds
        now keeps a dirty transplant's source too, as an unforced
        removal keeps one (`changed-during-cleanup`).
      - Known, left for the re-rank after the owner's week (its third
        P1, ranked behind the data-safety items and the wire): a
        terminal's job that ignores SIGHUP outlives a host SIGKILL with
        no owner, since terminals record only their history, not their
        processes, and the next host restores a fresh shell beside it.
        Probes on macOS 27: once the PTY's master closes, the job's tty
        reads `??` and its parent launchd (ppid 1); with job control it
        leads a group of its own, not the shell's; `ps` reports every
        session as 0 and `ps -E` no environment. What it keeps is its
        kernel session, the dead shell's pid, and `getsid()` answers
        across sessions. The design: a batched verb in `macfs` listing
        the user's processes with their session and start, through the
        engine's `Darwin`; the common PTY spawn recording a terminal's
        shell for `OrphanSweep` beside the scripts' (a kind on its
        records, a store migration); and the next host's sweep reaping
        a dead owner's session, members started after the record,
        TERM then KILL with the scripts' identity care, before its
        terminals are restored. Its proof: a HUP-ignoring job across a
        host SIGKILL, gone at the next start, the shell restored. Cost:
        Go, the store and the host, for a case rare in a week's use.
      - A mirror's apply expected the tip and the index it read, not
        the HEAD: a checkout switched to another branch at the same
        tip, or detached there, between the follower's read and the
        apply passed, and the apply moved HEAD back. A pull's apply,
        which runs here with its own read, expects the whole state now
        and refuses as `changed-locally` (`host/mirror/gitState.ts`,
        `sameHead` beside `GitHead`; `pnpm test git-state-lock`). A
        push's names no HEAD until the contract carries it, in the
        protocol's one bump this release (the wire PR).
      - The renderer slice (2026-10-18), its tidy finding: the confirm
        step's acknowledgement was one tick while the risky set it
        described stayed live, so a second selected worktree that went
        dirty with the dialog open was force-removed under the old
        tick. The tick is given for the risky worktrees it names, each
        with the verdict it was shown with: one turning risky since, or
        one whose verdict changed (its unpushed commits joined by
        uncommitted changes), clears it, and a removal is forced only
        for a worktree acknowledged as it is (`tidyModel.ts`
        `acknowledges`, `forcedRemoval`; `tidyAcknowledgement.test.ts`).
        The view looks the same.
- [x] Reliability pass, before the rc, for every client (the desktop
      window, the web client, the terminal, a remote device): a soak
      over the dev hub for hours with the network dropped and restored,
      the hub redeployed mid-session, the host killed and respawned,
      the laptop slept and woken, a tab reloaded and a second tab
      opened, a token expiring. Each must recover on its own, with no
      stuck "connecting", no stale view and no silent failure, and the
      trace log shows what happened. The owner reports the v2 web
      client as unreliable. This pass is what proves v3 is not. Step
      5's tabs check is the first of these scenarios.
      The web client's and the hub's share runs as `pnpm reliability`
      (`app/lab/reliability/README.md`): each scenario against the dev
      hub, with a dev app on this machine as the host, and a tab
      recovered when its hub socket and its session to the host are up,
      the host's chip reads Connected, and the host's worktrees as
      listed through the hub and as drawn in the sidebar both match what
      the host itself says, with no uncaught error and no failed request
      left unlogged.
      - [x] The network dropped and restored (`network-drop`,
            `network-blip`).
      - [x] The hub redeployed mid-session (`hub-redeploy`).
      - [x] The host killed and respawned (`host-kill`,
            `app-relaunch`).
      - [x] The tab slept and woken (`tab-sleep`, now `laptop-sleep`).
      - [x] A tab reloaded and a second tab opened
            (`reload-and-second-tab`, `two-tabs-redial`).
      - [x] A token expiring (`token-expiry`).
      - [x] The soak: the scenarios looped for four hours
            (Measurements).
      The desktop window's, a remote device's and the terminal's share
      runs in the same harness (`--clients`): a second dev app (A) with
      two windows reaching the host over the LAN, a third (C) dialing
      its tunnel only, and `smd` against A, each behind a front the
      harness drops (a hub front for A and C, a TCP front on the host's
      direct listener, `SHIGOMORI_DIRECT_FRONT_PORT`, which carries
      every client's link). A window recovers as a tab does, its sidebar
      narrowed to the host. The terminal recovers when `devices` names
      the host unblocked and `list --remote` lists what the host says, then a
      send to the host and a bring back both exit 0. While the host is
      out of reach each verb ends within 60 s, exits non-zero in words
      naming the cause, and a failed send leaves the worktree home.
      First full run of all four clients on 57bc5e0f2: 12 scenarios, 10
      recovered, longest 8.6 s (`host-kill`). With the terminal's words
      (#771) and the dial now: 12 of 12, longest 16.9 s (`host-sleep`),
      `network-drop` back 3.3 s after the network.
      - [x] The network dropped and restored (`network-drop`,
            `network-blip`), every window reading the outage within
            15 s and the terminal naming its cause.
      - [x] The hub redeployed mid-session (`hub-redeploy`).
      - [x] The host killed and respawned (`host-kill`,
            `app-relaunch`), and the desktop's own host
            (`desk-host-kill`).
      - [x] The laptop slept and woken (`laptop-sleep`: tabs frozen, A
            and C stopped with SIGSTOP, the network gone), and the
            host's machine asleep (`host-sleep`).
      - [x] A window reloaded and a third opened
            (`reload-and-third-window`).
      - [x] A token expiring (`token-expiry`) for the windows.
      - [x] The soak: the scenarios looped for two hours over every
            client (Measurements): 44 scenarios, 42 recovered, longest
            43.9 s (`host-kill`). The two failures were a `laptop-sleep`
            the harness woke without the wake's signal (fixed in the
            lab: a woken desktop app now hears the network back and
            dials at once, 2.3 and 3.4 s after) and a `two-tabs-redial`
            at a load average of 45 with 6 GB in swap, where the
            windows did not answer the harness for 10 s.
      Found for the clients' share:
      - [x] The terminal's cross-device verbs during a hub outage (`devices`,
        `list --remote`, `send`) exit 1 with "An error occurred in
        Effect.tryPromise": the host's device list
        (`host/process/handlers.ts`) wrapped its call in a bare
        tryPromise, which also kept a revoked credential from reading
        as signed out. The list's failure is now the signed-out case
        for a refusal, and otherwise says the account is out of reach,
        with its cause (`AccountUnreachableError`).
      - [x] A desktop window reads the host as Connected through a whole
        outage of 40 s and more: with no word from the OS the link
        gives up only on its heartbeat (40 s silent, pinged every 15 s).
        A window's Chromium does see `offline`, which nothing passed to
        the host. It now tells its shell (`window:networkChanged`),
        which wakes the host: every window reads the outage 5.1 s in,
        and `network-drop` holds them to 15 s.
      - [x] A web tab whose clock runs two hours ahead (`token-expiry` as it
        was, now `clock-skew`) drops every ask as "replayed or expired" (the relay's replay
        record, #766, reads the device's own wall clock), so it never
        gets a session, with only a console warning to show for it.
        Decided: an ask's freshness is judged by receipt, in a replay
        record the responder keeps ten minutes by its own clock, not by
        comparing device clocks (#769). `clock-skew` recovers in 2.0 s
        on every page and is in the soak.
      Left in the web client: a page whose wall clock stepped back two
      hours (the harness undoing its own jump, which a machine's clock
      does not do) kept a peer's view stale until reload, its retry
      schedule apparently reading the wall clock. `token-expiry` only
      moves the clock ahead.
      Found for the host's share of the pass (in `app/shared` and
      `app/host`, left to it):
      - [x] A device whose clock is off by more than two minutes reaches no
        peer at protocol 7: the sealed relay drops every ask as
        "replayed or expired" (`CLOCK_SKEW_MS` in
        `app/shared/hub/link.ts`), and the tab reads Connecting with
        only that console warning to say why. Found by the pass on the
        protocol 7 tip, whose `token-expiry` then still moved the page's
        clock two hours ahead; that scenario now lets real time pass
        instead. Either tolerate the skew or tell the user their clock
        is off. Asks are judged by receipt since #769, and `clock-skew`
        (every page two hours ahead) recovers like any drop.
      - [x] Two tabs of one web device dialing the host at once void each
        other's direct tickets: the host mints a pending set per peer
        device, each mint replacing the last
        (`app/host/direct/connectInfo.ts`), so the loser's hello is
        refused ("bad proof") and its keeper parks the host on "refused
        the connect ticket" until the roster changes. That tab reads no
        session until reloaded (the soak's `reload-and-second-tab` at
        17:07:16). Key the pending tickets by the connection id the
        hello carries, which the encryption thread's link PR does. The
        client half is in: the keeper asks again on a slow ladder after
        a refused ticket instead of parking (#758, proved by
        `two-tabs-redial` against the unfixed host). The host half came
        with the sealed link (#761): a pending set per device and
        connection id.
      - [x] After a host restart its first connectInfo answers come before
        its tunnel is up, so a browser, which dials the tunnel only,
        gets no candidate, parks the dial on "offers no direct
        listener" and waits out its ladder: in `host-kill` the tunnel
        was up 8.8 s after the kill and the session 16.5 s after. A
        host whose tunnel comes up could tell its peers to dial again.
        The host offers what it serves at once (its LAN listener as soon
        as it listens), and holds an ask only its tunnel could answer
        while the tunnel is starting, up to 6 s (under the asker's 10 s
        dial deadline), answering with the tunnel the moment it is up
        (`TUNNEL_HOLD_MS`, `pnpm test direct-plane`). No wire change:
        the answer only comes later. `host-kill` with the device on the
        tunnel, six runs each: median 16.2 s and longest 32.7 s before,
        10.2 s and 17.7 s after (Measurements).
      - [x] A host that restarts or relaunches is unreachable over its
        tunnel for good: the listener took a new port each start, and the
        tunnel's connector kept the ingress written when the tunnel was
        made (cloudflared's own log: config version 1, the first start's
        port, after re-provisions on a new one each answered success).
        The soaks missed it, since the harness pins the advertised port
        (`SHIGOMORI_DIRECT_FRONT_PORT`). The host now listens on the port
        it listened on last (`host/direct/listenerPort.ts`), any other
        only when that one is taken, so the ingress and a peer's LAN
        address stay right (`pnpm test socket-host`). Why the Worker's
        rewrite of the ingress does not reach the connector is the hub's
        to answer: it now reads the configuration back after writing it,
        writes it once more on a mismatch, and otherwise fails the
        provision (the hub unavailable for the tunnel, which the host
        retries), logging the versions each side named (`hub` tests,
        `tunnel.spec.ts`).
      - [x] The shared supervisor (`app/shared/remote/supervisor.ts`, which
        the hub socket and the direct keeper ride) needs a "dial now" on
        the platform's online signal (the browser's `online` event,
        Electron's net status), so the first attempt after connectivity
        returns is immediate instead of waiting out the backoff: after a
        long outage the hub socket redials up to 16 s after the network
        is back (`network-drop`: back at 16:06:58, connected at
        16:07:14). A probe now also ends a backoff wait: the hub
        supervisor's when no socket is live, and each direct peer's on
        the shared ladder (a refused one keeps its slow ladder, clear of
        the host's lockout). The web client already probed on `online`
        and `offline`; a desktop window now tells its shell
        (`window:networkChanged`), which wakes its host as a resume does.
      - [x] A view on a direct session that this side closed (a probe or
        the heartbeat finding it dead) is never told it ended
        (`rpcTransport`'s watch), so it sits on its last value: the
        sidebar kept a peer's old worktrees after `network-drop`. The
        renderer now starts a peer's views again on each new session
        (#753), which covers it, but the stream should end with its
        session. A view the link's scope interrupts now ends with
        `RemoteDisconnectedError`, which a window's host relays as no
        session and the window asks again on (`pnpm test socket-host`).
      - [x] A direct link closed under a tab keeps writing to its socket:
        each sign-out left a few dozen `WebSocket is already in CLOSING
        or CLOSED state` errors in the browser console
        (`sign-out-with-sibling`). The calls a busy page made between
        the host's close arriving and the socket's close event were
        written to the closing socket. Every frame of a link goes
        through its sealed socket, which now refuses a write once the
        socket is closing, so the call rejects as a disconnect
        (`pnpm test socket-host`).
      - [x] Terminal history survives a host crash: today the ring is saved
        at a clean quit only (the design's rule), so a crash loses the
        open shells' history; save the ring on a schedule or at each
        batch and measure the cost. Saved when the output has been quiet
        for two seconds, or a minute after it started changing
        (Measurements), with the restored row kept until the shell
        saves over it. `pnpm test terminals` kills the host
        mid-flood and reads the ring back whole.
      - [x] The hub:watchPeer relay queue is unbounded (memory under a
        flood), found in the byte-channels lab, not fixed. A window's
        host now holds at most 64 values of a peer's view for it (a
        terminal client's backlog), and a window that falls further
        behind has the peer's view stopped at once and its own ended as
        no session once it has taken the backlog, which it asks again on,
        a terminal picking up after its last chunk (`servedWatchPeer`,
        `pnpm test socket-host`). Held back to the peer's pace instead,
        the view stalled its link: effect 4.0.2's RPC client offers a
        stream's values into a bounded buffer from the loop that reads
        the socket, so a full buffer stops every answer on the link, and
        its pings, until the heartbeat cuts it.
      - [x] The host killed and respawned with a package script (`pnpm
        run`) or a nohup'd command running: the sweep reaps only an
        orphan whose group leader survived with its recorded command,
        so either escapes; decide whether to track the group or accept
        it. Tracked by group: a recorded script whose leader is gone
        while its process group goes on is the script's own, since no
        pid is handed out while a group of that id lives, as long as all
        in it started after the script did and the script was recorded
        by a host this one replaces while that host ran: the shell tells
        a host it forks after a crash which hosts died since one was
        last up, each with when it started (`replacesHosts` in the
        host's facts), the last seconds before. Another instance's
        records on the same data dir, those from before a relaunch or a
        reboot, and one a past host of a recycled pid wrote, can be days
        old, by when the group's id may be a stranger's, so their groups
        are left alone. The group list carries no command
        lines, so a busy machine's `ps` stays under what the sweep reads
        (`pnpm test orphan-sweep`). A host is vouched for within the
        shell's crash window (a minute) after it died. Left, each a
        leftover kept rather than a stranger signaled: a script's
        leftovers after the whole app was killed; a replacement that
        dies in the seconds its own sweep is still stopping what it
        claimed; and a script started just as the wall clock stepped
        back. In the lab, a dev profile ran
        a `pnpm run` dev server and a nohup'd `sleep` and had its host
        SIGKILLed: the dev server went with its PTY's hangup, and the
        nohup'd process outlived it under the old sweep and was stopped
        by the new one, nothing left 4 s after the kill.
      - [x] A dev app stopped as a group (a launcher, a terminal's
        Ctrl-C) left its cloudflared running under launchd until the
        next launch reaped it through the pid file: Effect spawns a
        child detached on macOS, so the connector led a group of its
        own. It now runs in the host's group and goes with the app; one
        a host crash leaves is still reaped at the next start, seen in
        the lab (`pnpm test direct-plane`).
      - [x] A window viewing a peer's terminal under `yes` failed every
        call after with "remote device disconnected" until a reload,
        while the terminal kept drawing (found by the byte-channel box,
        step 7). A served stream handed a lagging client its whole
        backlog as one message (64 chunks of up to 1M characters, and
        `hub:watchPeer` relays without a bound), past the 16 MiB a
        frame may be in the binary layout. A stream's messages are now
        at most 1 MiB (`host/socket/boundedMessages.ts`), a terminal's
        chunk at most 256K characters, and a link whose client cannot
        decode a frame drops and is dialed again (`dialDevice`). The
        stuck half is effect 4.0.2's: its framing parser
        (`effect/src/encoding/SchemaBinary.ts:565`) refuses a frame
        past `maxFrameSize` and cannot find the next, and
        `RpcClient.makeProtocolSocket`
        (`effect/src/rpc/RpcClient.ts:1105`, "Error decoding message")
        reports it to the calls in flight and keeps the socket, so every
        later answer fails the same way. The window port
        (`portLink.ts`) carries no views and keeps Effect's behaviour.
        `hub:watchPeer`'s queue is bounded now (below).
      Done: the web client's and the hub's share in the four-hour web
      soak (#764, Measurements, 2026-10-10: 107 of 108), and every
      client's in the two-hour soak (#808, Measurements, 2026-10-11: 42
      of 44, longest 43.9 s in `host-kill`), each failure accounted for
      above. Again on 2026-10-12 on the tip after #888 (protocol 13, the
      stable port and the tunnel fixes), every client but the web one
      and every soak scenario but `hub-redeploy` (Measurements): 52 of
      52, longest 21.9 s in `host-sleep`.
      Left: every soak ran on this one machine, the remote devices two
      more dev apps (one over the LAN, one on the tunnel only) and the
      laptop's sleep its clients stopped with the network cut. A real
      sleep and a host on another machine have not been run.
- [x] The link between the owner's two Macs through the alphas (#921; Lychee,
      always on, and Kiwi IV, a laptop on another network that sleeps
      overnight): Lychee's host dialed Kiwi IV all night, Kiwi IV warned
      "remote device disconnected" for Lychee after a sleep, and Lychee's
      data was at times missing there. Found from Lychee's logs (Kiwi
      IV's had not come):
      - The hub never took a silent socket off the roster. A laptop that
        sleeps leaves its socket open, Cloudflare reports no close, and
        each dark wake (about every 17 minutes) supersedes its own
        socket, so it read online all night and the keeper dialed it
        about every 26 s (its 16 s rung and the 10 s ask), some 1,100
        dials, of which main.log showed only the 139 changes of reason.
        v2's hub did the same. The hub now closes a socket that sent
        no ping and no message for over 90 s (`SILENT_AFTER_MS`, close 4106, which a
        device redials on), judged when the object runs anyway: an ask
        relayed to it, which the sleeper's own dialer sends, and every
        read or announce of the roster. No alarm, so no running cost.
        The one case only an alarm would cover: a silent device nobody
        dials, in practice a web tab (desktops park on it), reads online
        until the next traffic or join, a display matter. Measured in
        the lab on two dev profiles over the dev hub with the peer's
        whole app SIGSTOPped (sockets open and silent): its session gone
        in 42.7 and 43.3 s (the link's pings), off the other's roster in
        87.9 and 88.0 s, and once it went on, back on the roster in
        1.28 s and its session up in 1.38 s, the hub's close waiting
        for it as it woke (`hub.spec.ts`, "a device that went silent").
      - One 10 s budget covered both legs of a dial, the hub-relayed
        ask and the candidate race, so after a wake a late answer left
        the tunnel handshake too little and the next rung won 1.2 s
        later (18 times in two days). Each leg has its own now
        (`pnpm test direct-plane`, "a late answer").
      - A call on a link that died while the machine slept failed as
        `RemoteDisconnectedError`, which crossed `hub:invokePeer` as
        "remote device disconnected" and was toasted for each query a
        woken window refetched. The bridge now answers it, and a dial
        an invoke joined that failed, as `NoDirectConnectionError` with
        the reason in its words: a query waits it out quietly and
        refetches when the session lands, and an action still says so
        (`pnpm test direct-plane`).
      - Nothing logged the hub socket. Its connects, closes (with the
        code), a dead socket and a block are logged now, as are peers
        coming online and going offline, and the keeper's line numbers
        the attempt it logs.
      Left: Lychee's sessions with Kiwi IV while both were up dropped
      about every 17.5 minutes, the hub socket and the links together,
      on Kiwi IV's side; its logs will say why. A peer's tunnel is
      offered while cloudflared is still reconnecting after a wake (a
      530 for the dialer), and the "Local Network access" hint shows
      for machines on different networks.
- [x] The app's own links through a laptop's sleep (#944; the owner on
      Kiwi IV, alpha.9: "Something went wrong / lost the connection to
      this device" toasts, and the Account page's "Couldn't load the
      device list: lost the connection to this device"). Those words
      were only ever `RemoteDisconnectedError(local)`, a drop of the
      window's link to its own app: the hub socket never raises it, and
      the device list is the shell's own fetch over the window's port.
      Kiwi IV's main.log had no host restart at all. Its hub lines ("the
      socket stopped answering its pings", 1006 about every 17 minutes)
      are the sleeps and dark wakes #921 found, and the same wakes broke
      the window's links. Fixed as "Time" and "Local links and liveness"
      (Designs) say, with the time and liveness audit of 2026-10-16's
      section 1 and the first two items of its change order:
      - The ports to the shell, the window's and the host's
        (`portLink.ts`): the pinger's default limit, the hour's interval
        on the wall clock, with no retry, gave the link up for good after
        an idle hour or at the first check after a sleep. Every later
        shell call (the account, dialogs, the window, client config)
        then failed at once with the local words until a reload. Now no
        limit (`pnpm test shell-link`: an idle hour then a sleep, the
        window's link and the host's, each call run once; both fail
        before).
      - The loopback dials to the host, the window's, the shell's
        (`deviceLink.ts`) and the terminal's (`Control.ts`: 5 s pings, a
        15 s limit): cut at a wake, failing the calls in flight, the
        focus refetches a wake brings and a long `land` or `send`. Now no
        limit, and the loopback listener (`DeviceLink.ts`) no 120 s cut
        (`pnpm test host-hold`, a sleep at either end, each half failing
        alone; `pnpm test control`, the terminal stopped 20 s mid-call).
      - Links to other machines: Effect's pinger cut a quiet, healthy
        peer at a hidden page's minute-late turn, before it sent a ping.
        Its pings stay and judge nothing; `liveness.ts` judges, on time
        alone, and any frame that comes after a probe answers it, so a
        link busy with a transfer is not cut for its queued answer
        (`pnpm test socket-host`, "liveness: a dialer cuts a peer only
        on a probe…": ten minute-late turns keep the link, a silent peer
        is cut on time; fails before). The web client gets the same,
        being the same dialer. A hidden page's turns all come late, so
        it judges no dead peer while hidden; the visibility probe does
        as it comes back. The link's socket wrapper keeps the socket's
        own `binaryType` and pause, which Effect sets (`host-hold`, over
        a browser's socket).
      - The watchdog's round whose limit came late (the whole app
        stopped) asks again rather than kill a host whose answer waits
        unread (`pnpm test host-hold`).
      - A drop of the host's link is a state, not toasts. A read it cut
        asks again quietly until the host is back; an action it cut says
        once "The app's background process restarted before this
        finished. Try again."; a band over every page reads
        "Reconnecting to the app's background process…" while the host
        is away, then for ten seconds why the shell started it again
        ("stopped responding for 30 seconds", "quit unexpectedly (code
        N)"), which the window asks the shell for as its link lands
        (`window:hostRestarts`). No wording says "device" for the app's
        own process (`pnpm test own-app-drop`).
      - main.log says why the host stopped and what it was in: the
        reason, the service spans open at its last beat (the beat
        carries the newest four and the oldest), and the last eight
        spans it finished, from host-trace.log; then "starting it
        again" when another starts. A watchdog kill no longer also logs
        "exited unasked (code 0)".
      - The `host-restart` walk on the packaged build: the host killed,
        and the host stopped until the watchdog restarts it, each with
        the band, no error toast, and the page reading the new host; the
        window's renderer stopped 75 s (past the old 40 s limit and a
        throttled minute) and the whole app stopped 130 s (past every
        limit, as a sleep stops it), each with no band, no toast and no
        restart, and a commit sent as the app stopped landing once.
      Every way the alpha.9 host goes silent or exits unasked, from the
      code, with what covers it:
      - A watch's `open(2)` held by macOS (a removable or network
        volume, a TCC prompt): #932. A start that stalls: #932's beats.
      - A user terminal's PTY read error other than EIO or EAGAIN:
        node-pty rethrows it unless a second listener hears it, so it was
        uncaught and the host exited. Fixed here (`Terminals.ts` listens,
        as ScriptRuns did for scripts).
      - SQLite's 5 s busy wait, synchronous on the host's loop, behind
        the terminal `sm` holding the write lock (the driver's default
        `busy_timeout`): six queued writes are a watchdog kill. Left:
        nothing reported it, and lowering it trades a stall for failed
        writes.
      - `existsSync` on a worktree path as a script starts
        (`scripts/index.ts`) and in the removed-worktree sweep, on a
        stale network mount; the store and its WAL watch
        (`StoreChanges.ts`) on a data folder on another volume that
        stalls; libuv's own synchronous open in `fs.watch` after #932's
        probe: left, rare on a laptop, each needs its path read off the
        loop.
      - A big synchronous parse (`Icons.ts` reads untracked files up to
        256 MB), and memory pressure: left.
      Left, from the review: the window's restart count compared with
      the shell's misses one restart notice when its first ask fails;
      no proof yet of the band's count, its refetch on return, or the
      count a silent stop and its exit share; a terminal `sm` stopped
      with Ctrl-Z keeps its loopback slot (of 64) until resumed; the
      host listener's 120 s cut of a remote peer still reads the wall
      clock, so a host that wakes cuts its peers (the other time
      thread's, with the hub).
      Left: the hub socket's pinger and its wake line
      (`shared/hub/connection.ts`) and the audit's change order from its
      third item on, with the other time thread; the listing's 429 from
      the hub that reads "Decode error" (44 in Lychee's trace on
      2026-10-16), the next PR.
- [x] The device list against the hub's rate limiter (#947; Lychee's
      alpha.9 trace, 2026-10-16: 49 `account:listDevices` failures, all
      "Decode error (429 GET …/devices)"). Nothing polled the list. It
      came in storms: about 208 calls in two seconds at 04:41Z and about
      700 between 16:43 and 16:45Z, nearly all cancelled. Each hub status
      push whose roster named a device the list lacked, or lost one,
      asked for the list again (`remoteDeviceSync.ts`). That ask
      cancelled the read already out, and the caller that had joined it
      (`throughCancel`) asked again, so a churning roster (a peer
      flapping on and off, a keeper redialing) became a burst of reads
      the hub turned away. The re-reads a roster, a reconnect or an
      account change ask for now go one at a time and never cancel the
      read out: a read already out (anyone's) may predate the change,
      so one more follows it as it settles, however many asked
      meanwhile, and with none out the read goes at once (`pnpm test push-atoms`: 25 such
      pushes over 150 ms, each read 100 ms, asked the hub 10 times
      before, cancelling as they went; twice or three times after,
      none cancelled). A 429 reads "the hub is rate-limiting this
      device; retrying", and the list's query asks three more times
      (`pnpm test account`).
- [x] An unconfigured hub no longer parks the tunnel for the host's
      lifetime (the retrospective of 2026-10-17, O18). The Worker's "no
      tunnel env" answer is a deployment setting a redeploy changes, so
      the runner asks again every 5 minutes (`UNCONFIGURED_RETRY_MS`),
      at once after a wake or the network changing (`recheck`, which a
      wake during an ask still reaches), and on any reconcile, the same
      port's included; a stop forgets it, and a transient failure of
      a later ask (a wake on a network still coming back) is waited out
      the same way, off the restart ladder, reading as the tunnel down
      with its cause logged; a denial still parks (`pnpm test
      direct-plane`).
      Left: a provision the Worker denies with a 404 (an older deploy
      with no tunnel route) still waits for the next reconcile, as a
      revoked credential's 401 does; a wake does not ask it again.
- [x] A 429 from the hub is one error for every account call (the
      retrospective of 2026-10-17, O29). The account service's shared
      call turns the rate limiter's 429 into `HubRateLimitedError`
      with the hub's Retry-After, for enroll, revoke, update, the
      ticket mint and the tunnel's provision as for the device list,
      in place of "Decode error". It is a contract error, so it reaches
      the window whole. No read retries it inside the hub's minute: the
      device list, the one query on the hub, is not asked again until
      its Retry-After has passed, whoever asks (a page, a push burst,
      the device sync, each of which before drew a 429 of its own), and
      the next ask after it reads the list. A write (a mutation) is
      never asked again, so nothing is replayed. The tunnel's provision
      stays on its restart ladder (`pnpm test account`).
      Left: a 429 on the hub socket's `/connect` upgrade reads as the
      socket's generic connect failure, which its supervisor's ladder
      retries anyway; the terminal's `sm devices` prints a rate limit
      as the account out of reach ("Check the network"), and an
      enrollment refused as enrolled elsewhere hides a parked revoke
      that the rate limiter turned away.
- [x] "Cannot access Lychee" from Kiwi IV on beta.1 (2026-10-17). Not
      the link: Lychee's host trace held Kiwi IV's inbound sessions
      (a hello at 01:59:25Z and its pings every 15 s after, and an
      earlier one from 01:52:20Z that the new hello replaced), which
      read projects and sweeps, while every `scripts:watch` was
      refused "not permitted to run commands": Lychee's Allow control
      was off, as on any fresh v3 enrollment (the dogfood's setup,
      above). v2 starts it off too (it never turns it on by itself;
      its one automatic write carries over a legacy `grants.json`), and both keep it across a
      re-enrollment, so no regression. The trace read as empty for Kiwi
      IV because nothing logged an inbound peer or an answered ask:
      the listener now logs each peer it links and the path it came
      in on, and the connectInfo answer what an asker was offered when
      that changes (`pnpm test direct-plane`). Kiwi IV's hub closes
      with 1006 at 18:52:18 and 18:59:23 (local) came after Lychee's
      session to it had already dropped (Lychee's redial asks went
      out at 18:52:14 and 18:58:59 and went unanswered), and its
      earlier ones recur about every 37 minutes (16:02, 16:39,
      17:16), so they are Kiwi IV's network or sleep cutting its
      sockets, not the relayed ask.
      Left: a refused peer's footer says "Read-only until that device
      allows control…" without the device's name.
- [x] One owner of which hosts a project's images load from (the
      retrospective of 2026-10-17, O02). A window loads GitHub's image
      hosts alone, its policy as static as the web client's. An image
      on any other https host is not loaded by the window: the device
      that holds the project reads it (`projects:remoteImage`), on the
      host of one of the project's remotes, any remote, a subdomain of
      it or the port an https remote names, and nothing else, and hands
      it back as a data: URL, which both policies allow. So an
      Enterprise project's images show in the web client, on the other
      Mac for a project only it holds, for a project added after the
      window loaded, and from a second remote's host; the window's
      per-load image hosts (the shell asking the host, `image-hosts.json`,
      `session:imageHosts`) are gone. A pull request's attachment on an
      Enterprise Server whose URL names a port takes its signed URL too
      (`pnpm test remote-images`, `pull-request-images`,
      `content-policy`). The owner's restricted policy (#821) holds:
      nothing reads an image from a host the project doesn't name. A
      remote is read as written (no `ssh.` folding into a parent
      domain), one that names no dotted host opens nothing, a redirect
      is followed only onto those hosts, no body past 10 MB is read, and
      a read that times out stops its body. The window asks for each as
      it nears the view, and keeps a refused one blank while its cache
      does rather than asking at every render.
      `projects:remoteImage` is a remote, ungated call like the icon,
      but unlike it makes this device fetch a URL a peer chose, from
      inside its network, on its remotes' hosts alone; `session:
      imageHosts` went without a protocol bump, being the shell's and
      its own host's, one build.
      Left: an Enterprise Server reached over ssh whose web answers on
      a port other than 443 can't be read (no remote names that port),
      and a `<picture>` source or a video poster on a remote's host is
      dropped rather than read.
- [x] A refusal names the machine and its switch (the retrospective
      of 2026-10-17, O06). Every read-only, hidden-files and
      hidden-output note says "until <the peer's name> turns on “Allow
      control from other devices” on its account page", the footer,
      the diff, the files, a script's console and run button, the
      palette and the port forwards included; the label is required, so
      no surface can fall back to "that device". The toast for an action
      a peer refused names it too: the end that asked fills in the
      refusing device (`CommandRefusedError.deviceId`, which the peer
      never sends), the window's peer api for its own calls and this
      host's peer calls for what it asks a peer on the window's behalf
      (a forward, a pull or send, a mirror), and the toast reads its
      name off the registry (`pnpm test consent-words`). A Forward all
      a peer refuses says so once, where it said nothing. The dial's
      no-route hint names the LAN addresses it tried, and when a tunnel
      failed too says so beside it with the tunnel's reason, since
      machines on different networks fail their LAN addresses the same
      way (`pnpm test direct-plane`).
- [x] Push-driven reads go one at a time and never cancel a read out
      (the retrospective of 2026-10-17, O28), as the device list's
      have since #947. One helper owns a refresh
      (`renderer/lib/askAgain.ts`): the matching queries go stale and
      each one a page reads is read again, joining a read already out
      and followed by one more, so a burst is two or three reads. Every
      push-driven refresh goes through it: a host's `externalChange`,
      `projectChanged` and `refsRefreshed` (the rows, the branches and
      an open page's pull request), a peer's access flip (its runtime
      info), the account's `changed` push (which
      re-read the device list too), the port forwards' push on every
      connection, and the device list's own re-read, now on the same
      helper. A page that leaves, or a mutation that cancels the read
      for its optimistic write (a read counts as landed by the cache's
      own success or error, since a cancelled one over data resolves),
      leaves the query stale rather than read again, and a query the
      account's leaving removed is not read again; an ask for the device list is kept whoever started the
      read. A session landing still restarts what is out, the failing
      reads its keeper's dial left in backoff. A read outside React
      (`throughCancel`) joins a cancelled fetch three more times, then
      takes the cancellation as its answer (`pnpm test push-reads`: 25
      pushes about 5 ms apart, each read 100 ms, read at most once per
      100 ms of the burst plus one, none cancelled).
- [x] A transient failure is not an authoritative empty (the
      retrospective of 2026-10-17, O17). The host's merge settings read
      fails when gh's call fails, where it answered null (the UI's
      "every method allowed"), and is asked of gh again next time; null
      means only gh not ready or no GitHub remote, which the window
      keeps half a minute (the host's readiness cache), so a gh sign-in
      shows at the next focus, and a remote added once the host's
      five-minute cache of remotes has passed. Each window's read of
      the merge settings, a project's About and its clone URL (its
      image host) asks again after a failure, and keeps an answer for as
      long as the host does (an hour for the settings and the About,
      five minutes for the remote), not for the session; the host's
      About cache keeps its answer an hour, not for the launch. The
      launcher row keeps its order for five minutes, on focus and under
      a palette opened over it, and a visit after that reads it again
      (its comment had promised a read on every visit from a global
      default that could not give one); Settings' tools list reads
      again each time it opens (`pnpm test github-metadata`).
      Left: a project's icon, which a git-state push already reads
      again, keeps a failed read's null until then; a read gh can never
      answer (gh missing or signed out, an Enterprise Server without a
      field the query asks) is asked four times per mount, the window's default
      retries, since its error crosses as words the window can't tell
      apart.
- [x] Signing out in one tab while a sibling tab of the profile holds
      its Clerk session re-enrolls the profile from that tab (found in
      step 5's lab check).
      A window that saw its device enrolled under the Clerk user counts
      that as its one automatic enrollment, so a credential that goes
      while the session lives stays gone, in every tab (#755). Held by
      the harness's `sign-out-with-sibling`, which failed before it
      (the sibling sent an enrollment and both tabs read signed in
      again).
- [x] A pull request whose commits can't be fetched shows its media
      sides as not here, with a retry (the retrospective of 2026-10-17,
      O01). Its diff falls back to `gh pr diff`, which names blobs in
      short, and a side is read only by its full id (`isFullObjectId`,
      the read's own contract, kept): such a side, which read as "Can't
      show this file here", says it needs the pull request's commits,
      which couldn't be read here, and offers Try again, which asks for
      the diff again and so tries the fetch again. A retry joins one
      already out and shows it is out, and one that fails keeps the
      diff shown (the `pull-request-partial` walk: a head no remote
      serves, the diff from `gh pr diff`, each image side saying so,
      then the commits reachable and Try again drawing them).
- [x] A window whose renderer is killed from outside the app comes back
      (the owner's beta, 2026-10-17 18:38Z: an agent's `pkill -f
      "scripts/chrome/service.ts" -U $(id -u)` in another project, where
      macOS's pkill reads `-U` and the uid as more patterns, sent SIGTERM
      to every Electron helper of both apps). The host came back ("exited
      unasked (code 15)"), and both of the v3 app's windows logged
      "renderer exited (killed), not recreating" and stayed blank: a
      render-process-gone whose reason was "killed" or "clean-exit" was
      read as the app's own teardown, which the quit's own check already
      covers, so an outside signal or macOS under memory pressure left a
      dead window for good. Only the app's teardown (a quit, the window
      closing) now explains a gone renderer; any other recreates the
      window where it was, hidden or minimized as it was, within the
      crash-loop budget, where every window gone within a second of the
      last counted one is that one kill (a signal that takes four windows
      spends it once, where it would have given up on the fourth), and
      whose give-up says so in a dialog (`rendererGone` in
      `main/core/liveness/rateLimit.ts`, `pnpm test liveness`). Class: an
      exit's reason read as intent. Siblings, every process-gone and exit
      handler in `app/main` and `app/host`: the host's exit (any code
      restarts it, within its budget), the file-sync daemon and
      cloudflared (each restarts on its ladder whatever ended it), the GPU
      and utility processes (Chromium starts them again; the shell only
      logs), and a second window (its own handler, the same rule). The
      `renderer-recovery` walk, on the packaged build with the window as
      the oracle: the renderer SIGTERMed, then SIGKILLed, then every
      process under the app SIGTERMed at once (the host and its children
      among them), each time the window back on its page and reading the
      repo through the new host, and a window the person closes staying
      closed; the three kills fail before. A cloudflared killed from
      outside (a second such `pkill`, 2026-10-17 19:44Z, took both apps'
      connectors) is started again on the tunnel's ladder, as it already
      was, now held by `pnpm test direct-plane` (its stub dying of a
      signal as the platform reports one), since a signed-out lab build
      runs no tunnel. Left: a renderer that dies every eleven seconds or
