        - [x] The app updating itself, which v2 did and no v3 build had:
              alpha.4 and alpha.6 never staged an update on either of the
              owner's Macs, and never took the install request `smv3
              update` wrote. Two causes. In the app, Electron's `fs`
              takes the `app.asar` inside a bundle for a folder, so a
              recursive remove of a staged `.app` failed on it
              (ENOTEMPTY) and left a husk, which the remove's callers
              ignored; the rename of the next download onto that husk
              failed with ENOTEMPTY, which the platform reports as
              "Unknown". The terminal `smv3` runs on Bun and staged fine.
              The updater removes with `rm -rf` now, in both runtimes,
              and an errno the messages don't word keeps the system's own
              words (`platformErrors.ts`). And the request was watched
              for with `fs.watch` on the data dir, which holds the
              worktrees: under their churn FSEvents drops events and Node
              passes over the drop in silence (a signed build missed 6 of
              15 requests with files churning under `wt/`), and a build of
              alpha.6's code missed the one sent after its stage on a
              quiet folder too. The app polls for
              the request every second instead, and a claim that fails is
              logged and shown as the updater's error. A swap had never
              run on a signed build; its relaunch now keeps a data folder
              the environment chose (`open --env`), or a test run came
              back on the default one. The release job's "Launch the
              signed app" proves the loop on every release:
              `smoke-app.mts` installs a copy of the build, offers a
              second copy signed again as `<version>.1` from a local
              release list, and waits for the app to stage it, take an
              install request, swap and open again on the same temporary
              data folder. A local Developer ID build of 3.0.0-alpha.7
              (the flavor renamed, as the installed v3 app holds the v3
              lock) staged in 64 s, most of it the first check's minute,
              and came back as 3.0.0-alpha.7.1. The same smoke on
              alpha.6's code staged on a fresh folder and stopped at the
              request: an installed alpha.6 moves on through Settings'
              Restart to update (the same install, with no request file),
              once its `updates/staged` and `updates/extract` hold no
              husk, or through `smv3 update` with the app quit.
        - [x] Restart to update, pressed in Settings: on Kiwi IV it
              toasted "Couldn't install the update / remote device
              disconnected" in a window that stayed up. The app's own
              install, unlike a peer's, called `app.quit()` before its
              answer left: the quit's first finalizer tells the host to
              quit over the shell's loopback link, ahead of the answer the
              shell port carried a microtask later, and the host's quit
              closed the loopback with the window's `updater:install`
              unanswered whenever its teardown outran the answer. The
              window then showed the drop, through the whole hurried
              close (up to 20 s) and over an install going fine. A signed
              build idle on this machine won the race every time (an
              answer in 0.3 s, the quit 0.1 s after), so the button had
              worked here and been proved nowhere: alpha.6 to 7 to 8 were
              taken with `smv3 update`, and the smoke sent the request
              file. Four changes. The install answers first and quits
              `QUIT_AFTER_ANSWER_MS` later for every caller, as a peer's
              already did (`main/electron/updater.ts`), and an installer
              that won't start fails the call too, so its reason reaches
              the toast. The window reads this machine's link dropping
              under its install as the restart under way, not a failure
              (`renderer/lib/updates.ts` `restartIntoUpdate`). A
              finish-install that fails (the swap, the bundle it could
              not find, an app that would not quit, the relaunch) leaves
              its reason in `updates/install-failure.json` before it
              opens the current app again, and the next start shows it
              as the updater's error, marked `install` (an optional
              field: no protocol bump), which the window toasts as "The
              update didn't finish" (`InstallFailedToast.tsx`); before,
              it was in `install.log` alone and the app came back
              offering the same update. And a link to this machine's own
              app (the loopback, the shell's port) says "lost the
              connection to this device" where it said "remote device
              disconnected" (`rpcTransport.ts`). `pnpm test
              restart-to-update` holds the window's press over the REAL
              loopback, and the engine's updater tests the failure's
              note. `scripts/press-restart-to-update.mts` presses the
              button from outside the window, as its client does, which
              a packaged build's refusal of a debugger otherwise rules
              out, and the release job's smoke now takes the request
              file into an app folder the swap can't write (the app
              comes back on the old version saying why), then the button,
              which answers and comes back on the new version. Both
              passed on a local Developer ID build of the tip (a test
              flavor set to update, since the dogfood holds the v3
              lock). Kiwi IV's `install.log` would tell whether its swap
              then failed too.
        - [x] `sm update`'s handoff to a running app by its id (the
              retrospective of 2026-10-17, O16; the simplification pass of
              `app/main` and `packages/cli`). The request named only when
              it was asked, and the app took it by its age on the wall
              clock (a sleep or a step dropped a real one or took a stale
              one), never said what became of it, and `sm update` waited
              its two minutes for a new pid whatever happened, so a
              declined dialog, an install already under way or a stage
              the app wouldn't take all read "hasn't restarted yet"; an
              error counted as this request's by `updater.json`'s mtime.
              Now the request carries an id, the app it is for and the
              CLI that waits (`appPid`, `requesterPid`), and only that
              app takes it, only while that CLI still lives: a leftover is
              stale by identity, not by age. The app says in `updater.json`
              what became of it (`handoff`: claimed, declined,
              nothing-staged, failed with its words), and `sm update`
              fails at once on a decline, nothing to install or a failed
              installer, in their own words, and withdraws a request no
              app took within ten seconds ("The app didn't pick up the
              update request…") or that it leaves on an interrupt; a
              withdraw that finds it taken waits on, and nothing is asked
              twice; a request that can't be taken back fails in words.
              The data-folder move's release of the bridge is asked and
              answered, not told: a take under way (the claim to the
              install handed to its fiber) runs whole first, and nothing
              writes `updater.json` after it.
              The quit half a second after an install's answer stays
              pacing: the window reads its own app going away under an
              `update` as the restart, as under an `install`. No protocol
              change (`updater.json` and the request are files between
              the app and the `sm` it bundles; `UpdaterState` is
              untouched). Frozen surface: `sm update`'s failure words for
              those cases are new; its success and exit codes are as
              they were (`Updater.test`, `pnpm test shell-files`). Class:
              a delay or an age standing in for an acknowledgement. Its
              siblings in these packages: the relaunch's and the
              install's 500 ms quit (kept as pacing, the callers tolerant
              of the drop), the error read by mtime (gone with the
              handoff), and `sm nuke`'s unbounded wait on the app (the
              nuke PR of this pass). Left: a peer's drop under an Update
              all still reads as its failure, since only this machine's
              own app's drop is known to be the restart; a request the app
              took while another install of its own was under way waits
              out the clock if that install's start fails (its error is
              the updater's state, which the handoff no longer reads); two
              `sm update`s at once can withdraw each other's request; a
              request for an app that has restarted since is dropped
              without a word, and its CLI ends on "still on" the version;
              the release has no bound of its own over a claim on a
              stalled volume, and an install it let go of can still run
              during the move; and whether a test flavor updates is
              written in the engine's feeds and the shell's updater both.
              Proved on the packaged build too (`update-button` walk):
              Settings' Restart to update with an update staged, offered
              by a stand-in release list, and a terminal job keeping the
              host busy. Declined in the busy dialog (answered through
              System Events), the app stays as it was, no error shown,
              the update still offered; gone ahead with, the app quits,
              the installer's swap refuses the lab build ("no Team ID to
              verify the update against", an ad hoc bundle), and the app
              it brings back says "The update didn't finish" with that
              reason. For it a test flavor's updater runs when, and only
              when, it is given a stand-in list (`updates` in the
              engine's feeds, the shell's updater layer): a dev build has
              no updates still, with a stand-in or without, and a shipped
              flavor's line is unchanged (`Updater.test`, "a dev build").
              The installer's relaunch passes the debugging port a
              debuggable flavor opened (`devCdp.ts` keeps it, since the
              launch's environment rebuild drops the variable), so the
              walk reaches the window it brings back. The terminal path
              can't be walked on a lab build: `sm update` checks the
              installed app's signature before it writes a request, and
              an ad hoc bundle has none; `Updater.test` and `shell-files`
              hold it.

        - [ ] The shell, which no proof drives: three windows on three

        - [ ] Two machines on one LAN, both on the prerelease: a send, a

              ran between profiles on one machine, which the prompt does
              not cover.
        - [ ] A real sleep: the host on a second machine, this laptop's
              lid closed for a few minutes and opened on another
              network. Every window reads the host as Reconnecting, then
              Connected, with its views current and no reload. The soaks
              stood a sleep in with SIGSTOP on the clients, the network
              cut at the harness's proxies and an online event sent on
              the wake; the OS's own sleep, wake and change of network
              are what this step meets.
      - Production cutover. The order the v3.0.0 release deploys in,
        since the production hub, the web client and every installed
        app move together (decision 11):
        1. The D1 migrations on a copy of production. `wrangler d1
           export` of `shigomori-hub`, applied locally with migrations
           0001 to 0006, each device row kept and `public_key` added
           empty.
        2. The release published flagged as a prerelease. The workflow
           builds, signs and uploads it as the prod flavor (its tag is
           `v3.0.0`), and a v2 install's updater passes over a release
           flagged prerelease, so no device runs v3 before its hub is
           there. A v3 device that enrolled on the v2 hub would keep a
           key the hub never stored.
        3. The production hub: the D1 migration, then the Worker
           (`pnpm run deploy:prod`). Its floor turns away every build
           below the protocol with "Update it to connect".
        4. The release marked a full release. A v2 install offers only
           full releases, so this is the one it updates to: the v2
           updater stages it, checks its team, swaps the bundle with the
           v2 `sm`, and the v3 start makes the migration. Between 3 and
           4 every v2 device is turned away, so 4 follows 3 at once.
           The v2 updater's check is `codesign --verify --deep
           --strict` on the staged bundle and its Team ID equal to the
           installed app's (`cli/updater.go`, nothing else: not the
           bundle id). Checked statically on Lychee, without installing:
           the installed 2.37.3 and the signed `v3.0.0-alpha.8` bundle
           from its release zip are both team `63DMJ7W9B3`, and the
           alpha passes the verify, so v2 accepts a v3 bundle the
           release workflow signs. The hand-off itself is not
           rehearsed: it needs a signed prod build on the live v2
           profile.
        5. The web client: `web-client-prod` moved to the release's
           commit by hand, since the workflow's job that moves it runs
           only for a release published as a full one.
        6. Re-enrollment. Every production device holds a credential
           with no key and reads as signed out. Where its Clerk session
           lives, it enrolls again on its own; where the session has
           lapsed, the migration's page (or the account page) asks for
           a sign-in, and until then the device is out of reach to its
           peers and its login item is off.
        7. Clerk's production instance: which ways in it offers. The
           one-click sign-in (`useEnrollment`) goes straight to GitHub
           only where GitHub is the instance's one way; with more, the
           sign-in shows Clerk's choice. The redirect allowlist names
           `shigomori://app/` (`hub/README.md`). The instance must also
           take `shigomori://app` as an allowed origin: the window sends
           it with the SDK's Authorization header, and an instance that
           doesn't know the origin refuses every sign-in with
           `origin_authorization_headers_conflict`. The dev instance
           knows `shigomori-dev://app` and `shigomori-v3://app` only (the
           migration matrix's row 5); production knows `shigomori://app`,
           since v2 signs in with it today.

        Decided by the owner: upgrading is one-way. Once the import is
        verified, the migration deletes v2's files, the production hub
        turns v2 away after the cutover, and no export back to v2 is
        built, so a backup made before the update is the only way back.
        The release notes say to make one.
        A backup of the data folder is not all the way back: the move
        into `wt/` changes git's own records too, outside the data
        folder (each worktree's `.git` file and each repo's
        `.git/worktrees/<name>/gitdir`). The way back, measured on a
        copy of real 2.x data (the migration matrix's row 4), with v3
        quit:
        1. Each worktree the import moved back to where 2.x kept it:
           `mkdir -p` of `<from_path>`'s parent (the import removes a
           2.x root it emptied), then `git -C <project> worktree move
           <where it is now> <from_path>`. The import's moves are the
           store's `wt_moves` rows (read before step 3) whose
           `from_path` is under a 2.x root (the data folder's
           `worktrees/<project>`, the project's own
           `.shigomori/worktrees`, or `.sm/worktrees/<project>` on a
           project's drive; `WtFolder.rootsOf`) and `moved` is 1; one
           with `moved` 0 never left. A later move or rename adds a row
           from that `to_path` on, so where the worktree is now is the
           end of that chain, as `git worktree list` shows it. The move
           rewrites both links, so no `git worktree repair` is needed.
           Git won't move a worktree to another volume ("Cross-device
           link"): one a later move took to another drive is copied
           back instead, as the engine's own move does
           (`moveAcrossVolumes`): `cp -Rp` to `<from_path>`, `git -C
           <project> worktree repair -- <from_path>`, then the copy
           left on the drive deleted.
           A worktree made after the update isn't in the backup, and
           2.x doesn't know it.
        2. The backed-up 2.x files copied back into the data folder.
        3. `store.db` and its `-wal` and `-shm` set aside. A store whose
           import is recorded never imports again, so what 2.x writes
           after the restore would never reach a later v3, and it reads
           the 2.x files beside it as leftovers: those still as the
           import read them are offered for deletion by the doctor,
           the rest reported for a person to check.

        After it, the data files, every repo's worktree list, refs and
        local config, and each worktree's HEAD, changes and stashes
        matched what they were before the update, and a 2.x build
        opened it as it opens a copy that never saw v3.

        The 3.0.0 release notes say:
        - Upgrading is one-way: back up the data folder (`~/.sm`) before
          you update. The update moves its contents into 3.0's store and
          deletes the files 2.x kept them in, and the hub no longer lets
          2.x connect.

- [ ] The simplification pass: what threads built in parallel for one
      job, made one. A walk of the whole branch first (parallel
      mechanisms, then families of modules with one shape), then one
      PR per package in dependency order, then a second pass, with no
      behaviour change. Numbers in Measurements.
      Removed so far: the second `isSameOrInside` and
      `externalVolumeRoot` (contracts' are the one), two copies of
      contracts' `tildify`, a copy of `Paths.expandHome`, the updater's
      files spelled out in seven places (`updaterFiles`, built once by
      the updater's files area), fourteen inline copies of
      `errorMessageOf`, and thirteen "catch every cause" shapes that
      are `Effect.ignoreCause` (ten of them logging it). The host's
      command runner (`exec`, `CommandError`, the pipe reader,
      `resolveOnPath`) is the engine's `processes.ts`, and git reads its
      pipes with it. The tests share their kits: a fresh data dir and
      the store under a service (`freshDataDir`, `onStoreAt`, eight
      engine tests), one cached build of the darwin helper (`macfs`,
      for the engine's tests and the proofs), a fake `gh` on PATH
      (`installFakeGh`, six proofs), the terminal runner without the
      options no proof passed, and the waits the kits already had.
      Left, with why: the clone checkout keeps reading
      `XDG_CONFIG_HOME` per call (the packaged app's environment is
      rebuilt from the login shell after the paths are read). The
      engine's `projectDriveBase` and `worktreeBase` still mirror the
      contracts' previews, which the labels draw; the decisions are the
      host's (the engine pass, below). The four error-to-words helpers say different
      words, which reach the terminal and the UI. The message-text
      branching in the contracts (`mirror.ts`, `sync.ts`, `forward.ts`)
      goes only with a protocol bump. GitHub's and terrier's runners and
      Open's `ran` keep their own spawns: `exec` differs from each at an
      edge (a split UTF-8 tail becomes a replacement character, the exit
      is read after the pipes close, stderr is kept); the clone
      checkout's hook runner reads its output joined on `capture`, as it
      did on a spawn of its own, except that a read of the pipe that
      fails fails the hook, where its own spawn read it as no output (the
      engine pass). `cli/shell.ts`'s Promise probe is gone. The orphan
      sweep's `ps` parser joins the doctor's in the host's PR. Not built,
      after a closer read: one writer for files written whole (the services take `FileSystem` as they are
      built, so a shared helper needs it provided at every call, about
      five lines saved), one account factory for the desktop and web
      shells (the logic is `shared/account/enroll.ts` already; the
      wiring differs on purpose in nearly every handler, and the once
      guards are the Vocabulary's `cachedWithTTL(0)`), one coalescing
      helper (the mirror's queue must exist before the graph, so only
      the port forwards would use it), and the lab reusing the
      reliability harness's helpers (each copy differs at an edge: the
      free port's interface, the poll, a cleanup that throws, the kill
      order). The store's own tests keep their setup while #879 has
      them open, and `shell-link` keeps its 10 ms poll. The updater
      bridge's proof timed out once on its `fs.watch` in CI (#887's
      run 38337712764, job 115101539770) and passed when run again;
      a second time makes it an item of its own. On 2026-10-13 three
      jobs stalled on the runner before any step ran and hit the
      10-minute limit (`licenses` on #895, `ui` and `engine` on #900),
      each green on a re-run: GitHub's side, not the code's.
      On 2026-10-14 the release job's first attempt for
      `v3.0.0-alpha.2` (run 38594965018) failed in "Build
      distributables" on `ERR_PNPM_PACKAGE_MANAGER_SYMLINK_FAILED`,
      pnpm unable to symlink `typescript` for `packages/engine`
      ("Directory not empty") in the install under the forge build,
      and was green on a re-run; a second time makes it an item.
      The views series (decision 18): the host's views take a refresh,
      a request that reads the view now and answers once that read
      landed (`Views.refresh`), and the changes page's list is the
      host's `worktrees:watchChangeStatus` view, refreshed by the
      window's own commits, discards and undos (#895). Then the rest
      of the changes page (#898): the picked file's diff and hunks and
      the git operation are host views on the signals that invalidated
      them (`watchFileDiff`, `watchFileHunks`, `watchOperation`), the
      refresh reads every view of the working tree, and the page's
      `filesChanged` listener, its tree-moved effect (`treeMovedKey`,
      with its proof case) and four query keys are gone.
      Then history and stashes (#900): the branch's own commits and
      the stashes are tree views read again as the project's git moves
      (`watchBranchHistory`, `watchStashes`), refreshed with the tree,
      and their queries, keys and the stash invalidations are gone. The
      paged and searchable log (`worktrees:listCommits`) stays a
      request: a page asked for, keyed by HEAD, not a state the host
      watches. The lab's fake host serves a view from its request form
      (`watchBranchHistory` reads `branchHistory`, `watchStashes`
      `stashes`, and so the changes page's views), where it read the
      module's list before, so the branch and commit diff pages, the
      stash list and the worktree page's Git rows read their fixtures
      in the lab instead of "NaNy ago, on top of undefined".
      Then the pull requests (#907): the project's PR map is the
      host's view of the background fetch's sweep
      (`githubCli:watchProjectPullRequests`), moved as well by a page's
      own read of its branch's PR, a merge, a draft toggle, a title
      edit, a new PR and a publish, all on the host; the sweep's push
      listener, `syncProjectPullRequests`, the renderer's write into
      the map, the map's query and key are gone. The page's PR stays a
      request (decision 18) and asks again when the map disagrees with
      it. `forest-reads` counts the three per-project reads left.
      The calls the views replaced went together with protocol 15
      (#908): `worktrees:fileDiff`, `worktrees:fileHunks`,
      `worktrees:operation`, `worktrees:branchHistory`,
      `worktrees:stashes`, `githubCli:projectPullRequests`
      (`worktrees:changeStatus` stays for the transplant preview's one
      read).
      The engine pass (2026-10-17), what is kept under an id: a removed
      project's primary is forgotten through the owners
      (`Registry.forgetWorktree`, `CloneCheckout.forget`,
      `Icons.forget`) in the removal's transaction, where `unregister`
      listed three of the five tables itself and left the unshelve
      time and the clone records; `icon_cache` has one writer; the
      doctor's prune of a gone worktree and its dormant-state repair
      run in one transaction (`Worktrees.forgetKept`); the bookkeeping
      check counts what `Registry.keptIds` reports, where Worktrees
      read two of Registry's tables for it; a re-key forgets what
      clones proved about the old id rather than leave it for a
      checkout made there later; a relocate commits the new path with
      its re-keys (Commit points). Left from the sibling search: a
      removed project's other worktrees keep their marks while their
      titles go, and its script order and usage stay; the listing's
      idle shelving writes Registry's shelf tables itself; the mirror

      rename handlers, not a relocate, an adopt or the doctor's; a
      re-key outside a move leaves port-pool's lease on the old folder;
      `moving.ts` and `WtFolder.ts` both write `wt_moves`.
      The engine pass (2026-10-17), the host's copies of engine logic:
      the tidy page's facts and sizes are the engine's `Hygiene` (the

      owner each (`Dirty.dirtyRef`, `Bundle.incomingRef`). Left from the
      sibling search, each its own change: port-pool's active check in
      the host (`lib/Ports.ts`, which counts an external worktree the

      watcher.
      The engine pass (2026-10-17), GitHub: the repository's merge
      settings are one read (`GitHub.repoMergeConfig`): the engine's
      `mergeSettings` reads every method on a failure, the host's
      cache keeps only a read that landed, at the host's 30 s gh
      deadline, as before. A check's state is one table
      (`contracts/pullRequestChecks.ts`), the pull request panel's and
      `sm land`'s wait alike: a stale check, which the panel read as
      failing and the wait as pending, is failing for both, so a land
      no longer waits on a required check GitHub gave up on; and a
      CheckRun with a conclusion and no status reads by its conclusion
      in the wait too. A remote on `GitHub.com` is github.com to the
      host, as to gh. Left from the sibling search: which pull request
      is a branch's own (three rules), the fork checkout's ref and the
      whole repository's list are written in both the engine and the
      host (the landing PR); the two gh runners differ in deadline and
      errors; the auto-merge method is cast unchecked in Landing.
      The engine pass (2026-10-17), landing: whether a removal deletes
      the branch is one rule (`Worktrees.deletesBranch`), the removal's
      and the land's commits guard alike, and it is never a detached
      worktree's, whose short hash a removal ran `git branch -D` on, a
      branch of that name going with it. A stack's land checks its own
      worktree is still removable after the merge's wait, as a single
      land does, rather than tear it down and find git refusing; a
      `done`'s branch delete runs uninterrupted once its checkout is
      done, the checkout itself staying interruptible, since it runs
      the user's hooks; git moves HEAD before post-checkout runs, so the
      delete follows where HEAD ends up, read in full after any exit of
      the checkout: on the primary branch (a hook cut short or failed
      included) the branch goes, then the checkout's failure is said
      with the delete's, where a retry from the primary branch found
      nothing to delete; moved on by a hook, it stays. A landing
      verb's document has its read fields typed (`Landing.Document`),
      where the terminal cast them and the host decoded its own shapes;
      a failed cleanup's words have one owner
      (`Worktrees.cleanupFailureWords`). `Bundle.create` reports the
      refs the bundle holds, where it named one git left out because a
      have already held it. Whether a pull request found by its
      branch's name is the branch's own is one predicate
      (`contracts` `isBranchsOwn`) for the engine's two readings and
      the host's.
      The engine pass (2026-10-17), layout (O27): which worktrees a new
      location moves, and where, is the host's (`worktrees:relocationPlan`,
      `Worktrees.relocationPlan`), judged by the folder, however its path
      is spelled: a worktree under a linked data dir or a linked custom
      base reads as in place, where the location form compared strings
      and rewrote a data dir's prefix, and offered a move to where the
      worktree already was. The form applies only on a plan read, and
      moves by one asked at the apply, since worktrees come and go
      between asks. A custom base of `/` is the root, where the engine trimmed it
      to an empty, relative base, and one through `..` is the folder it
      names; a move to an alias of the worktree's own folder is no move,
      where it was refused as an existing destination; a removal prunes
      the folders it left empty under a linked data dir or project too; the
      project's drive is not offered when the data dir is on it by its
      link. The runtime's `dataDirReal` is read by nothing now and goes
      with the next protocol bump.
      The engine pass (2026-10-17), the doctor: what it checks it reads
      from the rule's owner. The v2 files the store holds are
      `v2Files.heldV2Files`, the import's record written and decoded in
      one place (`recordVerified`), where the doctor read the row with
      raw SQL and a cast and wrote its rule twice; port-pool is listed
      once a pass, for the ports check and every project's leases, where
      each listed it; the staging folders beside the worktrees are named
      by one owner (`worktreeLayout.stagingName`, `isStagingName`); the
      port-pool and terrier toggles are read through `Config.device`; the
      doctor's document types extend the contracts' DoctorReport, so a
      field the app reads can't drift; a worktree moved by hand is
      re-linked by the relocate's own rule (`Worktrees.relink`), where
      the doctor copied it. Left: the orphan sweep's `ps` reading (the
      host's PR); the stale-lock check, which reads v2's layout.
      The engine pass (2026-10-17), recovery (O24, O25): a data-folder
      move is the one change under way. It holds a lock of its own alone
      from its first step to its last (`DataDirLock`, a database beside
      the pointer file), every change that touches git, its refs or
      files holds it shared at the engine's entry points (the device
      link's calls that change code, the host's `asChange`, the
      auto-pull, the terminal's commands that change a worktree on disk,
      the doctor's), and its recovery, which moved from the app into
      the engine, runs in every engine graph as it comes up, the
      terminal's included, holding the lock alone, where opening a store
      revived it as soon as its mover was gone and a terminal could write
      over what the move owed. The lock is the kernel's: a hold goes
      with its process, so nothing tells a live mover by its pid. Seven
      review rounds are why. The first six built the move's ownership
      from pid liveness and journal files: a refusal set in the store, a
      journal naming its mover, a row per mutation, a journal claimed by
      renaming it, a store left refusing revived when its setter was
      gone. Each round found a new way two processes could both act, or
      one act on a stale reading of another (a claim taking a newer
      move's journal, a revive racing a new move, a mutation the rows
      missed, a dead owner whose git still ran, a process at the source
      undoing a committed move). The premise didn't converge, so the
      kernel owns liveness now, and whether to finish or undo is read
      from the pointer under the lock, not from which folder a process
      is in. A copy to another volume is flushed whole, its folders and
      the pointer too, before anything removes its source (v2 copies
      across volumes too). The store's own refusal stays for a write
      that touches only the store. A copy's old tree stays until the
      store is opened at the new place.
      The move plans after the refusal starts, writes its
      journal flushed, and its journal is a state table (Commit points): each worktree's
      entry carries its dirty capture and a mark (`planned`, `rekeyed`,
      `returned`), set as each step is done, and the undo reverses by
      the marks, a `planned` entry only when something is kept under its
      new id, which a move starts only without (it is refused
      otherwise), so an undo never imports a stale worktree's data or
      reverses an entry twice. The move refuses a project it can't list,
      settles an earlier journal before it starts, and its recovery
      keeps the journal until each repair, re-key and removal it owes is
      done. A read whose error is an error is used wherever the journal
      trusts a read: a capture ref git can't open (`Git.readRef`, where
      `refTip` answered none), an old tree's or a landed copy's journal
      that can't be read, the journal itself, each keeps the journal
      rather than settle over it. A re-key's capture follows only if it
      is the one its caller saw (`expect`, failing `changed` forward;
      `back` leaves another where it is) and never over one kept under
      the new id (`occupied`). An adopt makes its replacement before it
      removes the external checkout (Commit points); a locked checkout
      is refused, and a removal that fails takes the replacement back
      only while the original's HEAD still resolves to its commit. Left:
      a relocate's and an adopt's capture that can't follow are said,
      not retried; a kill between an adopt's add and its removal leaves
      both checkouts; a crash between an undo's reversal and its mark,
      then something kept under the new id before the next start's
      resume, which runs before anything else could keep it.
      The engine pass (2026-10-17) took an inventory of
      `packages/engine/src` first (kept outside the repo), graded by
      cost and cut into PRs, biggest cost first. One observation for
      every link's liveness: the device links, their wake probe and the
      hub socket judge on `shared/remote/liveness.ts`, whose probe out
      past its timeout is only the cue for a fresh one, judged on
      `PROBE_TIMEOUT_MS` of turns that came on time, and a set-aside
      probe is interrupted (from a hidden page each late turn forked
      another `link:ping` before, piling up on a half-open link). A late
      turn asks a fresh probe, as a wake does, so after a stop a dead
      link is found in that probe's window. A silent peer is cut when it
      was before, a dead hub socket about five seconds sooner, the hub's
      probe whose wait a stop resumed before its answer was read asks
      again rather than redial, and a hidden web tab, whose turns all
      come late, finds a dead hub socket as it comes back rather than
      about a minute behind while hidden. A one-shot question's defect
      is an answer, as a failure was: the watchdog's round no longer
      dies of one, and a quit's busy ask reads it as no answer. The
      device link's wake probe was a bare five-second timeout and is
      the same fresh probe now, answered by any frame; a
      loopback link's is nothing, since the watchdog judges that host.
      The one-shot questions take the same rule (`answerWithin`): the
      mirror daemon's ping, the watchdog's rounds and a quit's busy ask
      (`busyWithin`), so a limit that came late (a stop, a sleep) asks
      again rather than kill the daemon or the host (`pnpm test
      clock-steps`). Left from its sibling search: the terminal's
      five-second handshake reads "app not running" after a Ctrl-Z
      longer than it, and the host's sweep of a silent peer counts a
      stall under its 30 s late mark as silence.
      The engine pass (2026-10-17), the updater: which staged update a
      build can install is the engine's (`Updater.staged`: newer than
      it, its bundle there), where the app offered any version but its
      own, so a bundle older than the running build (a newer one
      installed by hand) was offered on every start and refused by the
      swap; the app reads the stage's typed result rather than decode it
      again. The kept release list answers on its own only from a fetch
      that isn't in the future or a backoff no further out than a limit
      is ever waited, each judged on its own, and the bridge drops a
      request from the future, so a clock stepped back no longer keeps the copy answering
      or a stale request alive; the bridge's window is the CLI's restart
      wait (`RESTART_TIMEOUT`). `--finish-install` relaunches a test
      build with its update stand-ins, as the app's own relaunch does,
      where it came back checking the real feeds. The app's repo is
      named once (`flavor.ts`), where `updateFeed.mts` repeated it under
      a comment saying a build injected it; the changelog reads the
      release list's URL, rate-limit rule, tag and schema from
      `updateFeed.ts`, so an entry the updater reads it reads too, one
      without a tag is left out, and one without a page links to the
      releases page. The swap's aside and incoming names, the newer
      rule and a bundle's version read have one owner each, the doctor
      reading the version as the swap does. Left from the sibling
      search: the changelog still asks GitHub itself, on the web as on
      the desktop, rather than read the updater's kept list; the
      installer's "still running after 2m0s" is written out beside the
      constant; `updater.json`'s read stays lenient on purpose (only a
      missing pid is no app, so a newer app's state never reads as none
      and the swap never runs under it).
      The engine pass (2026-10-17): every child the engine's, the
      host's or the shell's graph spawns gets the 3 s kill grace and
      none inherits the shell hook's directive file (`childDefaults`,
      one wrapper of the platform's spawner), nor any the terminal `sm`

      post-checkout hook, the port-pool
      and rmdir steps of a move and a removal and the migration's

      `Open`'s launch of an editor or a terminal and `sm config edit`'s

      directive file on. Every git fetch from a remote has the project

      orphan sweep reads lstart in local time, and with its pid-only

      The engine pass (2026-10-17), what nothing reads: knip reads the
      engine's exports as it reads the contracts' and ui's (every module

      The engine pass (2026-10-17), the clone checkout: CloneCheckout's
      areas are a file each (`cloneCheckout/`: its documents, the

      The engine pass (2026-10-17), settings: the Settings form's save
      is the engine's patch (`Config.patch`), the keys it was given set
      or removed in one transaction, where the host read the stored
      settings, overlaid the patch and wrote the whole document back

      by a key the compiler checks (`Config.device`): the stored value

      `sm config launcher rm` and `carryover add` and `rm` decide what
      to remove or update from the list their write reads, not from an

      the settings changed while the editor was open. A launcher or a

      Left from the sibling search: a project's settings are still
      saved whole from the form's read (Configure, the location form)

      `Launchers`' all-or-nothing read of the custom launchers, Go's,
      stays; the doctor's device toggles go with the doctor's PR; the
      host's device document decodes whole, one bad key failing every

      The engine pass (2026-10-17), places and environment: the data

      program on PATH is found by one walk (`findExecutable`), by what

      from the sibling search: each host process resolves the data dir

      The host pass (2026-10-17), device settings: the direct listener,

      The host pass (2026-10-17), scripts: the PTY runs, the engine's
      lifecycle runs they book, the marks that refuse a run or a second
      delete (the tombstone), the busy count, the quit's gate and stop,

      `Scripts` service (`host/lib/Scripts.ts`), where they were module
      state behind free functions; the engine's changes are counted in

      under way: a lifecycle run is a script, a delete counts once, and

      A run or a terminal in a folder that is gone is refused through

      The host pass (2026-10-17), worktree moves and removals: the

      The host pass (2026-10-17), mirrors: the gateway is a scoped

      The host pass (2026-10-17), pull requests: one `PullRequestMaps`
      service owns the project's PR map and announces its view on every
      change, where eight callers pushed by hand and the create dialog's
      stack lookup read it silently, leaving the dots stale; the first

      keeps the last good map and is asked again (the first failure kept
      an empty one 5 min). The map and a signed image URL (4 min, never
      within a minute of its token's `exp`) age by whichever clock reads

      The host pass (2026-10-17), durable stamps: a describe stamps
      `describedAt` past the stamp stored, as shared settings do, so one on

      The host pass (2026-10-17), in-memory clocks: every answer the host

      monotonic, and gh's and port-pool's folders are read through Config

      The host pass (2026-10-17), mirrors that follow every move: a
      re-key whose checkout moved (a move, rename, adopt, project relocate,

      The loopback holds 48 terminal callers (no Origin, no shell token),

- [x] Test runs clean up after themselves, killed or not. On
      2026-10-16 Lychee's `$TMPDIR` held what runs that ended by a
      kill never cleaned up: 212 `sm-proof-*` folders (15 GB), 16,350
      `clone-index-*`, 6,326 each of `clone-home-*` and `clone-data-*`
      (1.3 GB), 2,180 `agents-*`, hundreds of `sm-index-*`,
      `sm-picker-*`, `sm-ports-*` and `sm-ports-corrupt-*`, and more
      (`sm-proof-data-*`, `sm-cli-*`, `sm-sync-check-*`,
      `sm-tunnel-orphan-*`, `sm-settings-*`, `sm-clone-*`,
      `clone-hook-*`, `engine-git-*`, `scoped-*`,
      `shigomori-pr-attachments`), beside a 2 GB `node-compile-cache`.
      Every runner (the app's proofs, the engine's, ui's, cli's and the
      hub's suites, the walks, the smoke, the scene comparison, the Go
      tests) now takes a run folder, `$TMPDIR/sm-test/<family>/<id>/`
      with a pid file, and points TMPDIR at it for the whole run
      (`testTemp.mts`). Every family above, whether a test makes it or
      the code under test does, lands in it under its own name. A run
      removes its folder as it exits, and `sm-test` once no other run
      is left. Every run sweeps the folders whose processes are gone,
      at its start and its end, so a proof that kills a process on
      purpose, or a run killed mid-way, leaves nothing past the next
      run (`test-temp`). A vitest a runner spawns joins the runner's
      folder and holds it too. Nothing is kept across runs: the
      proofs' `sm` and `macfs` builds take under a second, once per
      run. The compile cache is Node's, turned on by vite's and
      astro's bins, which put it in `$TMPDIR` unless
      `NODE_COMPILE_CACHE` says otherwise. The scripts that start them
      point it at their package's `node_modules/.cache`, which goes
      with the worktree. A `pnpm check` with TMPDIR at an empty folder
      leaves that folder empty. Left open: a run folder whose pid a
      later process took is held until that process exits, and
      `sm-picker-*` comes from nothing in this repository.
      A folder a proof left without write permission (the data-folder
      move's stuck old tree) failed the removal for good, so its run
      folder stayed: the sweep now gives every folder in a tree it
      can't remove write permission back and removes it again, and the
      proofs that restored a folder's permission inline (the data-folder
      move's, two of adopt's) give it back in their cleanup, a failure
      included. The clone checkout's fuzz scratch, which a step can
      throw out of before its own cleanup, is left to the sweep.
      A run's config folder is its own too (`XDG_CONFIG_HOME`, set by
      `ownTestTemp` beside TMPDIR), so the data dir's pointer and the
      data-folder lock beside it are the run's. The proofs' host runtime
      set only `SHIGOMORI_DATA_DIR`, so its `Paths` and `DataDirLock`
      used the machine's own `~/.config/shigomori-dev/data-dir.lock` (and
      the prod-flavor `sm` and engines of `smBinary.mts`
      `~/.config/shigomori/`), one lock for every run on the machine,
      every thread's checkout and the plain dev app: a run's data-folder
      move or settle, which takes it alone (`socket-host`,
      `data-dir-move`), stalled every change of another run's host until
      its cases timed out. That is the likely common cause of the proofs
      that failed only under lefthook on 2026-10-18 while three threads
      committed at once (`host-views`, eight cases at 300 s each;
      `socket-host`'s tabs; `worktree-removals` with `DataDirMoving`;
      `sync-transfer`; git-watcher's backstop). With the real lock held
      alone, `host-views` passes in 6 s and stalled past 120 s before;
      two runs at once both pass. It also takes git's default global
      `~/.config/git/{ignore,attributes}` and gh's `~/.config/gh`
      fallback out of the tests. pnpm and Bun read their global settings
      from `XDG_CONFIG_HOME` once it is set, the release cooldown and
      ignore-scripts among them, so the run's folder links the user's own
      back in (`pnpm config get minimum-release-age` reads 10080 inside a
      run, undefined without the link); the smoke's sign-in check through
      the installed app's `sm` names the app's data folder, which the
      run's config folder holds no pointer to. Class: a test reaching machine state
      its harness doesn't redirect (#952 made `testTemp` the one owner
      of test temp). Left, each reaching the real machine still: the
      walks' teardown `smlab nuke` with the real HOME (the lab build's
      own hooks, links, login item, keychain item and folders, by
      design, walks.md); the `terminals` proof's real interactive login
      shell, which reads the rc files and appends to `~/.zsh_history`;
      the smoke's packaged app, whose environment rebuild drops
      `XDG_CONFIG_HOME`, so its lock is the flavor's real one; scripts'
      and lifecycle steps' login shells reading the real profile (which
      now sees the run's config folder, so a tool set up from
      `~/.config`, mise or direnv, isn't); `~/.gitconfig` in the proofs
      that don't scrub git; port-pool's `~/.local/share` state; and the
      keychain proof's probe item, removed in its own file.

### Step 8: features

Every feature added during v3 lives here, whatever step was current when it
landed. Steps 0 to 7 keep the refactor and the sweep.

- [x] A per-device "Share with other devices" switch, reimplemented on
      the device link from the owner's unmerged change against main.
      The setting is `shareWithDevices` in the store's device settings
      (`sm config` reaches it, the Settings form cannot), held by the
      host's `Sharing` service with its `sharing:watch` view. Off,
      `SharingGate`, beside `CommandGate` on `LinkGroup` only (the
      loopback is this machine's own), refuses every call but the
      link's own and the ones a mirror this device asked for makes
      (`worktrees:list` and `worktreeData:read` became invitable), with
      `NotSharingError`; a call or view under way ends as it turns off,
      and a push reaches a peer only as `sharing:changed` or about an
      invited mirror's copy. Peers read the switch off the connectInfo
      answer (`sharesData`) and that push (`HubStatus.peerSharesData`),
      show the device as "Not sharing" with nothing of it listed, and
      `sm devices` says `not-sharing`. The fake host poses it with
      `?notSharing=`. Checked in the lab on two dev devices: the switch
      flipped both ways on one, the other's status and calls following
      live on the same link. A mirror between them was left to the
      proofs.
- [x] JetBrains Mono as the mono face everywhere (the owner's
      addition). `font-mono` draws code, diffs, paths, the
      console and the terminal in it, in the app and the web
      client, bundled from `@fontsource-variable/jetbrains-mono`,
      with its ligatures off so code reads as typed. The terminal
      falls back to Symbols Nerd Font Mono (nerd-fonts v3.5.1,
      `packages/ui/src/styles/fonts/`, its license beside it) for prompt icons,
      and opens once both faces have loaded. Doubutsu sets mono at
      550, which the variable face has, in place of 500 plus a
      text stroke.
- [x] The terminal (decision 17), as designed under "The terminal."
      It needs step 4's transport first.
      - [x] The `Terminals` service and the `terminals` RPC group.
            `app/host/lib/terminals/Terminals.ts` over the scripts' pty
            layer, each terminal's shell stopped with SIGHUP. Output goes
            out in 16 ms chunks through a bounded PubSub, and the PTY
            pauses while a megabyte waits on a slow client. The history
            ring (`history.ts`) keeps 512k characters with the queries
            stripped. A quit saves each terminal, its folder read off the
            shell with `lsof`, in the store (`SavedTerminals`, migration
            6), and the next start reopens it there under its history.
            A terminal whose worktree or project is gone closes after a
            delete and on any store change. The group is gated under
            `runCommands`, `list` included, since a terminal's folders
            are as private as its output. `pnpm test terminals`.
      - [x] `TerminalView` and `TerminalTabsView`, and their containers.
            `app/renderer/components/terminal/`: xterm with the WebGL
            addon, only the picked tab attached and drawn, keystrokes
            and query answers sent only from the focused terminal. The
            size each view has room for goes to the host, which sets it
            for all. A view is watched through `watch` on the client
            transport, and a peer's through `hub:watchPeer`.
      - [x] The worktree page's drawer and the device's terminal list.
            The footer's Terminal opens the drawer, per worktree and per
            window, with one height for all. A device's own terminals
            are listed under its name at the sidebar's foot and live on
            `/devices/$deviceId/terminals`. The palette offers that page
            for each device that runs commands from here.
      - [x] Reconnect and sequence numbers.
            The feed (`renderer/lib/terminalFeed.ts`) attaches again
            after the last chunk it holds whenever an attach ends with
            its link, and ignores what an attach it replaced still sends.
            `pnpm test terminals` drops the link five times under a
            300-line loop and gets every line once. Checked in the lab
            from the web client over the tunnel, offline for eight
            seconds while the device typed into the same vim. A shell
            with a foreground job counts as busy at a quit, and the
            history cuts a chunk bigger than its cap to the tail.
      - [x] The script console as a tab in the drawer.
            Opening a script's console opens its worktree's page with
            the console's tab picked in the drawer, beside the terminals,
            and its route went. The console keeps its run-a-slot model
            and draws on the same `TerminalView`, fed from the scripts'
            store, so it shares the theme and the replay gate.
            On a phone the drawer is a sheet over the whole page, the
            room v2's console page gave, closed by its tabs' hide button,
            and its handle drags by touch where it shows (the regressions
            pass, step 7).
      - [x] The drawer made part of the page (the owner's alpha.5
            dogfood: it read bolted on, and its hovers were off). It
            sits over the footer, so the footer and its Terminal toggle
            keep their place, and the toggle stays lit while it is open.
            It slides up from behind the footer when it opens on the
            page (the toggle, a console), not when a page change shows
            it open. The drawer, its tabs and the terminal are one surface,
            the card the footer is, inset to the page's 24px gutter.
            The tabs are the app's pills in sans, the pick in the
            accent: a pill hovers as one (doubutsu stripes it whole, its
            pick sitting out, as on the diff index's rows), and its
            close shows on hover and on the picked tab, with a fill of
            its own over the accent. The terminal draws at the theme's
            mono weight. Its ANSI black, white and bright black come
            from the theme's ink and paper, so a prompt's segments read
            in every palette, and the hues are set per appearance. The
            selection wears the accent in doubutsu, and xterm's
            scrollbar is a thin pill in the native bar's color. A
            device's page with its last terminal closed says so and
            offers a new one, and the console's clear moved from over
            the output into its header. The resize edge stays as bare
            as the sidebar's.
- [x] Worktrees live under `wt/` (the owner's addition):
      `<dataDir>/wt/<project>`, `<project>/.shigomori/wt` and the
      drive's `<dataDirName>/wt/<project>`. The v3 migration moves each
      worktree from v2's `worktrees/` roots once, its id re-keyed with
      what sm keeps under it and its port-pool lease re-made. A bound
      Claude Code session no longer resumes, work running there loses
      its path, and peers see new ids. A mirror into a moved copy goes
      on: the copy's device answers a session's old id as the copy it
      became (`WtFolder.movedIds`), and its invitation loads re-keyed.
      One the move can't take stays put, and `doctor --fix` retries it. The mirror daemon rewrites
      a v2 session's labels into v3's `mode` when it loads it.
- [x] Rename a worktree (the owner's addition): a move to the same
      parent under a new name (`sm worktrees rename`, `worktrees:rename`,
      the pencil beside the page's path), so it changes what a move
      does: a new id, new port-pool ports, and a bound agent session's
      transcript left with the old folder. Refused, like a move, while
      the app runs scripts or terminals there.
- [x] Multiple windows (the owner's addition). Each window is a client
      of the host over the same RPC link, with its own runtime and
      stream subscriptions. The shell gains a New Window menu item, a
      route each window opens on, and quits when the last window
      closes. The terminal drawer (decision 17) is per window.
      The shell keeps the set (`main/electron/windows.ts`): New Window
      on ⌘N in the File menu (Add project moved to ⇧⌘N), "Open in new
      window" in a worktree page's Options and the palette, a deep link
      or a notification's click in the window focused last, the File
      menu's launch tools the focused window's, and at a quit each
      window's page and bounds in `windows.json` for the next start.
      A shell call names its window (`HandlerContext.windowId`). The
      host needed nothing: the loopback already held a connection per
      window with its pushes and views. What assumed one window and
      went: a run started in another window missing from the sidebar
      (it reads the device's running scripts too), and an agent's
      notification shown once per window or while another window had
      focus. A dev reload opens a window on the page it was opened on.
- [x] End-to-end encryption between a user's devices, on every
      transport (the owner's addition), as designed under "End-to-end
      encryption": a terminal session, a diff or a mirror stream is
      readable only on the two devices, whatever carried it. First the
      Noise handshake and its proof against the cacophony vector, a key
      pair at every enrollment, the hub's `public_key` column and the
      keys in its roster, the connectInfo ask and answer sealed through
      the relay as opaque strings, protocol 6 with the hub's floor, and
      re-enrollment for a device from before keys (`useEnrollment`,
      `needsDeviceKey` on the account status), which the move to v3's
      screen drives. Then the device link: every peer's socket is
      sealed (`app/shared/remote/sealedSocket.ts`), opened by its
      ticket and the handshake, the challenge and the nonce proofs gone,
      the loopback's hello carrying its token, protocol 7. Tickets are
      kept per connection, so a web device's tabs dialing at once both
      link (the soak's finding). Checked in the lab on three dev
      profiles and the web client against the dev hub: a terminal, a
      diff and a mirror over the LAN, the tunnel and from the web
      client, and a logging proxy on a LAN link seeing nothing but the
      ticket in clear (Measurements). A refusal of a device on another
      version (an unsealed first frame, another link or protocol
      version) does not count toward the listener's lockout, which is
      for tickets and keys that did not hold, so a mixed-version LAN
      keeps its direct path through an upgrade. The device a sealed ask
      is addressed to answers each one once within ten minutes of
      receipt, so a hub replaying it cannot replace the asker's tickets.
      Signing in is one click where GitHub is the instance's only way
      in (`useEnrollment`), through the Electron bridge on the desktop
      and by redirect in the web client.
      After the second review: a ticket is checked as the socket opens
      and spent only by its authenticated hello, the hello rechecks the
      device's key against the live roster, a device the roster drops
      loses its pending tickets and its sockets still to say hello, and
      the relay's record of answered asks refuses new ones while full
      rather than forget one that has not expired. After the
      verification round: a hello or a dial the listener cannot check
      while the hub's roster is unavailable closes to be dialed again
      and counts nothing toward the lockout; the listener and the
      dialer offer no permessage-deflate (sealed frames do not
      compress, and deflate pushed a frame at the cap past it); and an
      ask is judged by when it arrives and nothing else, so a device
      whose clock is off is answered (protocol 8).
- [x] The v3 migration's screen (the owner's addition), as designed
      under "The migration's screen." The engine's migrator reports
      each step as it goes (`Migration.ts`), the terminal says one line
      per step at its first run, and the window shows the page from what
      the host tells its shell, its sign-in the device key's enrollment
      (`useEnrollment`). The lapsed session's sign-in was seen in the
      fake host (`?deviceKey=lapsed`) only: no trial device was enrolled
      before keys.
- [x] A fresh install's first run (the owner's addition), as designed
      under "The first run". It was a page (`/welcome`) on the
      migration's views, the shell's first-run route (`welcomed` in the
      client config) and terrier's repos (`terrier:repos`), and after the
      alpha's first launch the owner decided there is no onboarding at
      all (decision 19): the page, its route, `welcomed` and
      `terrier:repos` are gone, and a fresh install opens on v2's empty
      home.
      A pick turns terrier on, which lists every repo the person has as
      a project at once, and the sidebar and the projects page read four
      things of each (its pull requests, config, icon and GitHub
      description): past about twenty repos that went beyond the host's
      64 calls in flight on a connection, and the host refused the rest
      ("too many in-flight requests"). Those reads now go to a device
      at most sixteen at a time (`forestRead` in
      `renderer/hooks/remote/hostForestScope.ts`, `pnpm test
      forest-reads`): with 19 repos the page peaked at 49 to 58 calls in
      flight before and 14 to 18 after.
      A worktree page still reached the cap ("Couldn't read the git
      state", "Couldn't load worktree state"), and the pile-up was the
      client's: a push invalidates a query while its fetch runs, React
      Query drops that fetch for a new one (`cancelRefetch`), and the
      dropped call never told the host, whose git went on holding its
      place. With a slow `git status` the window's loopback held up to
      55 copies of `changeStatus`: peaks of 3 to 5 calls in flight in
      normal use, 19 with a 6 s status, 64 (the cap) with a 24 s one,
      and with a 12 s one the cap within 20 s. Now a
      query hands its signal to the call (`api.run(effect, { signal
      })`), so a dropped fetch interrupts it and the host kills the git
      it spawned, through the hub hop too (`hub:invokePeer`, `pnpm test
      direct-plane`), and the host frees the call's place as the
      interrupt arrives, not once its git is gone, as the client's
      gate does; every
      link lets 48 calls out at once and queues the rest in order
      (`fifoGate` in `shared/remote/rpcTransport.ts`), streams and a
      channel's numbered calls aside, so a burst waits instead of being
      refused; and the link's ping holds no place under the cap, so a
      full one no longer reads as a dropped session (`pnpm test
      in-flight`). The forest's own sixteen stay: in a queue that admits
      in order, they keep a terrier first run from putting the page's
      calls behind hundreds of its reads.
- [x] One keymap for the app (the owner's addition), as designed under
      "The keymap": every existing binding on the table with its default
      unchanged, the terminal's toggle on Ctrl+`, and Settings' Keyboard
      section.
      The table and the listener came first (#774), then the section
      (Settings → Keyboard), saved with the local form. Every window
      follows the client config's view (#835), so another window's save
      reaches it at once, and its menu with it. Left: Escape can't be
      recorded (it cancels recording; Go back keeps it unless rebound).
      On macOS ⌃K and ⌃↩ answer as ⌘K and ⌘↩ again, as in v2 (#877,
      #882): the palette's, the commit's, the palette's
      changes' and an edit's save have a Ctrl twin (`ctrlTwin`), and ⌃K
      opens the palette from anywhere but a text field and closes it
      from anywhere but a focused terminal, which keeps it.
      After the regressions pass: a chord's letter or digit is the
      character the key types, as v2's shortcuts were (⌘K on Dvorak is
      the key that types K), the physical key where the layout types no
      Latin one, and every other key physical (Ctrl+`); a ⌘ chord
      answers extra Shift, Alt or Ctrl unless another binding takes the
      press; the description's and reword's ⌘↩ are the keymap's `save`;
      and the menu shows the default keys until a window sends its own.
- [x] The guide (the owner's addition), as designed under "The guide":
      `guideContract` and `guideDriveContract`
      (`packages/contracts/src/modules/guide.ts`), the shell's sessions

      profile with Claude Code and Codex, and twenty how-tos run
      headless through both (Measurements).
      Left: a release's clone of its source, checked with the first
      `v3.0.0-alpha.N` tag.
- [x] The guide from the sidebar (the owner's beta addition): the
      footer's question mark opens the app's own menu
      (`SidebarGuideMenuView`), its heading "Ask a question about the
      app" and an item for each agent, which launches it through the
      shell's `guide:start` as the Help item does (`useStartGuide`).
      The guide became a question-answering launch, not a tour: the
      role as system-level instructions and no opening message
      (`agentGuide.ts`), the Help items "Ask <agent> about the app".
      `pnpm test guide-menu` holds the Help items' words and push, the
      sidebar menu's heading and items, and both ending in
      `guide:start`; `agentGuide.test.ts` that neither launch line

      Left: `pnpm guide-eval` re-done against questions, the launch
      as it is now (Measurements: the guide's numbers were taken
      against the old opening and the tour's instructions).
- [x] Attachments in a PR body on any GitHub host (the owner's alpha.8
      dogfood: a screenshot in a PR on github.ubc.ca, a GitHub
      Enterprise Server 3.20 in private mode, showed broken). New
      ground, not a regression: main's #649 rewrote github.com's
      attachments alone, and v2 never showed an Enterprise one, whose
      URL sends a window without a session to the Server's sign-in.
      The host's `withSignedImages` (`githubCli/pullRequests.ts`) now
      takes an attachment on any host and pairs it, by its id, with the
      URL in the body GitHub renders on that host, a subdomain of it
      (an Enterprise Server's `media.`) or githubusercontent.com; the
      body is read through gh pinned to the repo's host (#897), and no
      token goes to an image host. The window's policy and the
      markdown already allowed the remotes' hosts; the markdown loads
      any image with a query at once, as a signature lives there.
      `pnpm test pull-request-images` holds both hosts, the Enterprise
      one against a stand-in Server. v3 shows no PR comments, so the
      body is all there is to cover; the web client's policy still
      allows GitHub's own hosts alone.

- [ ] The v2 parity net (the gate rule's regression net): every
      behaviour v2 has that a user can reach, walked on the packaged
      `lab` build beside a v2 dev build of main, on one repo with the
      same clicks, and every page proved on real git through the window.
      The inventory (2026-10-16, release/v3 e227c3070 against main
      a36aa48bc): 48 v2 items and the owner's 24. Pages, menus, git
      actions, agent hooks and the two-device paths match v2 or do
      better (outside edits reach the changes page within 3 s, where v2

      page offering to move worktrees to where they are under a data
      folder reached through a symlink. Left as v2 has it: a finished
