      call: the keycap ↩ (v2 drew ↵ in two places) and the menu's title
      case. A "Check out source" bug in the first walk was the walk's
      click on "Quick-create worktree", whose name holds "create
      worktree"; the page checks out the branch, as `new-worktree`
      now proves.
      - [x] Page walks (app/lab/walks.md): the packaged build started on
            real repos with a temp home (a fake gh first on the login
            shell's PATH), its window driven over its debugging port,
            the window and git checked after each step, then the build's
            footprint nuked. `pnpm walks`, about 30 s for 23 checks
            here; the `Walks` workflow runs them after every landing on
            release/v3 and by hand (about 2 minutes of walks and 3 of
            packaging, too long for each pull request, the owner's
            call), and the pre-cut check runs them on the build about to
            be cut, so a regression shows within the hour after a merge
            and before any cut. The pages now proved on the real UI on real git:
            the changes page (the list, an image's sides, leaving a file
            out, a hunk's discard and Undo, a commit, the last-commit
            strip's undo and amend), a commit's page (image, video and
            tone; a long file to its end), the branch diff, the
            timeline's squash and hard reset, the merge dialog, the
            stash list and diff (stash, drop and Undo, restore and
            keep), the files viewer (tree, media, markdown, text), the
            new worktree page (branch off, check out), and a pull
            request's page and diff with images against the fake gh.
      - [x] The terminal sm as v2 had it at a terminal. `cd` or `switch`
            with no name, a pick in the menu, then the subshell: once
            an interactive shell took the terminal's foreground group,
            the reader the menu had attached failed its pending read
            with EIO, which Bun threw (rc 1). `handOver` now releases
            that reader before the program gets the terminal
            (`stdinFd.ts` `releaseStdin`). `--verbose` traces every git
            and gh a command runs on stderr, as Go's did, by wrapping the
            platform's spawner (`verbose.ts`). Kept as it is: a failed
            git call's message is git's stderr alone, without Go's
            `git <args>:` prefix, since an error names the binary and
            never its arguments (`processes.ts`); `--verbose` shows them.
            Proved in `packages/cli`'s "at a terminal" (a pick into
            `zsh -i` that reads a typed line) and "a command line".
      - [x] A rename stays on the worktree's page. The old id leaving the
            list sent the page to its fallback (Projects) and unmounted
            the rename's caller, whose callback was to follow the new id.
            While a rename from this window is under way its page now
            waits, and the hook's answer takes a window still on that
            page to the new id; one that went elsewhere stays there
            (`useWorktreeMutations.ts`, `WorktreeDetail.tsx`). Walk:
            `rename`.
      - [x] The Worktree location page with the data folder reached
            through a link (/tmp, /var/folders, a folder linked to
            another drive) offered to move every worktree to where it
            already was: it compared the layout's path, spelled from the
            data folder, with git's, spelled through the link. The
            host's runtime info now says the data folder's real spelling
            where it differs (`dataDirReal`, optional, so no protocol
            bump), and the page takes either (`LocationForm.tsx`). Walk:
            `location`. Left, found in review: a data-folder move from
            such a folder matches git's worktree paths against the
            folder as spelled (`dataDirMove.ts`), so its worktrees could
            miss their repair and re-key; not walked yet.
            Fixed: the move reads the folder's real spelling as the
            runtime info does, and counts a worktree (and a project its
            refusal guards against) as under the folder by either
            spelling. Before, a move from a linked folder carried none
            of its worktrees. The new paths, which the new ids hash, are
            spelled from the destination's real path, as git will name
            them, and a folder spelled with trailing slashes keeps the
            path below it whole (both found in review). A data folder
            that is itself a link is refused: the rename would move the
            link and leave its worktrees where they are, however it is
            spelled. Proof:
            `data-dir-move`, the folder spelled through a linked parent,
            or with trailing slashes, and moved to a linked one, journals
            its worktree at the link's target and re-keys it.
      - [x] A terminal comes back after a quit with its lines (the owner's
            test list, Terminals come back): the lines replayed on
            screen went blank under the fresh shell's prompt. The
            restored history ended by leaving the alternate screen,
            which on the main screen restores a cursor never saved, the
            top left, and the prompt's clear to the end of the screen
            took the rest. The tail no longer leaves the alternate screen.
            The host follows the alternate screen as output comes in, so
            it knows past the ring's end, and saves for the next start
            the history up to where a full-screen program running at
            the quit went there (none of it if the ring dropped that
            point): the main screen as the program found it. A row an
            earlier build saved is read the same way at the restore
            (`Terminals.ts`). Walk: `terminals`;
            `pnpm test terminals` holds the tail.

#### The owner's test list

What v3 adds over v2 as a person using it meets it, for the owner to try
on Lychee and Kiwi IV and tick; the install's own checks are under "On
the signed prerelease" and not repeated here.

**Windows and the shell**

- [ ] New windows. Press ⌘N (File → New Window), and pick "Open in new
      window" from a worktree page's Options and from the palette: each
      opens a window of its own on its page, and Add project is now ⇧⌘N.
- [ ] Windows as one app. Start a script in one window and look at
      another: it shows running in that window's sidebar too, and an
      agent's notification arrives once, not once per window.
- [ ] One mono face. Code, diffs, paths and the terminal draw in
      JetBrains Mono, `=>` and `!=` stay two characters, and a prompt's
      icons draw in the terminal.
- [ ] No onboarding. A fresh install opens straight on the home ("A
      forest, eventually." and Add a project) with no welcome page;
      alpha.4's first launch already showed it.

**Worktrees and projects**

- [ ] Worktrees under wt. Create a worktree: its path on the page is in
      the data folder's wt folder, under the project's name.
- [ ] Rename a worktree. Click the pencil beside the path on a worktree's
      page and give a new name: the worktree moves to it with new ports,
      and the rename is refused while a script or terminal runs there.
- [ ] Pull requests follow on their own. Merge one, mark one ready or
      draft, retitle one or open one from the app: the project's pull
      requests and the sidebar follow at once, in every window, with no
      refresh.
- [ ] Changes follow git. Commit, stash or switch branches from a
      terminal outside the app: the changes page, the picked file's diff,
      the branch's history and the stash list follow without a reload.

**The terminal**

- [ ] The terminal drawer. On a worktree's page, click Terminal in the
      footer or press Ctrl+\`: a drawer opens with a shell in the worktree,
      more open as tabs, a full-screen program (vim, Claude Code) draws
      smoothly, and Ctrl+\` from inside hides it again.
- [ ] Terminals come back. Quit with an idle shell open and start again:
      it reopens in the same folder with its history above; with a
      program running in it, the quit asks first.
- [ ] Terminals of a device. Open a terminal from a device's terminals
      page (the palette offers it): it is listed under the device's name
      at the foot of the sidebar, apart from any worktree.
- [ ] The script console in the drawer. Open a script's console: the
      worktree's page opens with the console as a tab in the drawer,
      beside the terminals.
- [ ] A terminal on the other Mac (two machines). From Lychee, open a
      terminal on Kiwi IV and type in it on both: both show the same
      screen, and Lychee's Wi-Fi off for ten seconds and on again picks
      up where it was, no line lost or doubled.

**Diffs and pull requests**

- [ ] Every file of a commit draws. Open a commit that changes several
      files, one of them long, and scroll to the end and back: every
      file shows its lines, with no empty box under a header (alpha.4
      drew only the first files).

- [ ] Images, video and audio in diffs (in v2.37 too, rebuilt for v3).
      Change an image in a worktree, on this Mac and on the other: its
      diff, its commit's diff and its pull request's page show the old
      and new image.
- [ ] Undo a branch's only commit (in v2.37 too). On a branch with one
      commit of its own, undo it: the commit goes and its changes are
      back in the changes list.

**Devices, sharing and the hub**

- [ ] Share with other devices (two machines). On Kiwi IV, turn off
      "Share with other devices" on its own row of the devices page:
      Lychee shows it as "Not sharing" with nothing of it listed, and
      anything of it open on Lychee closes; turned on again, it all comes
      back without a reload.
- [ ] Allow control (two machines). With Lychee's "Allow control from
      other devices" off, open a Lychee worktree on Kiwi IV: its footer,
      diff, files and scripts say "Read-only until Lychee turns on “Allow
      control from other devices” on its account page", and Lychee's
      own account page has that switch. Turned on, the controls come
      back without a reload. Then the same from Lychee for Kiwi IV.
- [ ] Two tabs of the web client (a browser). Open the dev web client
      in two tabs of one browser: both connect and reach Lychee,
      reloading one leaves the other connected, and Lychee lists the
      browser once.

**Settings and the keymap**

- [ ] Settings → Keyboard. Every action is listed with its keys: record
      a new chord, see a clash named, reset one or all, and the new key
      works at once in every window and in the menu bar.
- [ ] Rotate palettes (in v2.37 too). In Settings → Appearance, with the
      doubutsu theme on, set Rotate palettes to Each launch and start
      again: the palettes change.

**Agents and the guide**

- [ ] The guide. Help → "Ask Claude Code about the app" (and Codex):
      the agent opens in your terminal waiting for your question; ask
      how to do something, and the window shows a band with Stop and
      a ring with a sentence beside what it points at. It won't delete,
      sign out or type into a terminal for you, and Stop ends it.
- [ ] The guide from the sidebar. Click the question mark in the
      sidebar's footer: a menu opens above it, "Ask a question about the
      app" with an item for each agent installed (one agent too), and
      an item opens that agent waiting for your question.
- [ ] An agent stays with its worktree (in v2.37 too). End a Claude Code
      session in a worktree: the worktree still shows its agent, idle.

**Updates and the import**

- [ ] Updates up the stages. Check for updates in v3: it offers only a
      later v3 prerelease (alpha.5, then a beta), never v2's 2.37 or an
      earlier alpha, and v2 beside it is never offered a v3.
- [ ] The move from v2 (met at 3.0.0 only, after a backup; v3 starts
      empty). The first start of a v2 install shows one page with the
      import, the worktrees' move and the sign-in, names a stuck worktree
      with what to close and offers Retry, then opens with v2's projects,
      worktrees and titles.

## What the audits found and where it goes

| Finding | Step |
| --- | --- |
| Config keys and defaults in Go and TS; state files written by both; data-dir paths in five files | 2 |
| Generated client facade; `grant` tag on gated calls; `DeviceId` schema inlined four times | 1 |
| Retry ladder written four times, request matching three, dispatch twice, error-message matching nine, atomic writes four | 3, 4 |
| Desktop and web deciding "account changed" differently; duplicated hub connection setup | 4 |
| Five optimistic-update copies, hand-built invalidation sets, class strings, tone tables, settings sections | 6 |
| JSON Schema export for Go output; every Go-internal DRY batch; `sm` help text versus the command table | Dropped: the Go code is deleted |
| Old migrations; `hue.go`; old `projects config` flags | 7, 2, 2 |
| Security pass (shell, host, terminal, file-sync, web, secrets, supply chain): a peer's mirror stream reached past its worktree, commands off left a peer's views running, the store readable by other accounts, a loopback lockout anyone could trip, an update checked by team alone, no CSP on the window, Electron and three dependencies behind their advisories | 7 (#793, #794, #795) |
| Security pass, second review: a peer's mirror stream still chose its session id (paths Mutagen writes and removes), where it staged, and whether .git crossed, and could write under .git; file-sync now serves its own copy of Mutagen's endpoint server under the host's rules | 7 (#805) |
| Security pass, second review, the host and the shell: the shell's session calls open to any loopback-token holder, a granted call's pushes (a script's console) outliving both switches, the command gate reading its switch before subscribing, the Clerk token file 0644, remote debugging of a packaged build | 7 (#806) |
| Security pass, the guide: what it leaves to the user was read off names and variants, so the access switches, whose control has no name, took a guided click; the page now declares it (`data-users-alone`), with a ui test that every destructive variant carries it. Also a Makefile target named like an option of make's | 7 (#813) |
| Maintainability pass: stale docs (EFFECT.md's adapters, empty packages), triplicated semver, a refusal matched on its message, five services past 1,500 lines, gh JSON read by hand, cross-area hooks undocumented, the leftover mechanisms the Vocabulary replaces | 7 (#791, #796, #798, #801, #803, #804, #812 #814) |
| Left: exact pins in the catalog (the lockfile pins, the cooldown and ignore-scripts guard the install), an enterprise GitHub's images in the web client (its CSP is a static header that can't name a user's hosts; closed since by O02) | 7 |
| Security pass, the owner's decision: markdown images loaded from any host; now GitHub's and the projects' remotes' hosts alone, inline styles gone, and the window's CSP `img-src` names the same | 7 (#821) |
| Security pass, the owner's decision: the guide could run code from the app (a script's Run, the launch tools, a command field) with its tools pre-approved; they are marked `data-users-alone` and the guide takes no text there | 7 (#822) |
| Left: exact pins in the catalog (the lockfile pins, the cooldown and ignore-scripts guard the install), markdown images from any host (the owner's call) | 7 |
| Security pass, second review, the updater: a staged release could go over a newer app installed since, the bundle's version was not held to the release offered, and team and identifier were read from text rather than required of the certificate (an empty identifier on both sides passed) | 7 (#809) |
| Regressions pass: the file icons unpackaged, outside edits never reaching the rows, the account banner's dead button, an offline peer's rows gone and a never-reached one loading for good, failed reads saving defaults, the client config per window, the console's size, focus and phone drawer, the phone footer, the terminal `sm`'s parsing and verbs against Go, a mirror's copy re-keyed by the wt/ move, shortcuts by physical key | 7 (#832, #834, #835, #837, #838, #840, #842, #843) |
| Risk map, the wire: `hub:invokePeer` and `hub:watchPeer` are served on the loopback, so a holder of the owner-only `loopback.json` token reaches a peer through this machine's link. Reviewed and kept: the loopback is the host's one surface for this machine's clients, the terminal's cross-device verbs (`sm send`, `sm devices`, `list --remote`) reach peers through the host's `control:*` calls, and each peer applies its own gates, the command switch and the sharing switch among them, whoever asks | 7 (reviewed, no change) |
| Performance pass: the store polled every 500 ms and traced each poll, the host's trace log rotated only at start (100 MB a day at rest), the changes list's checkboxes searching the whole document for a label on every render, a web tab behind another window animating every frame | 7 (#865) |
| Time and liveness audit (2026-10-16), left for later, under its rules that persistent ordering is a revision, not the wall clock, and that age alone grants no authority over a live owner's files or processes: a terminal's restart sequence and incarnation; a script's unseen-failure marker; idle shelving, which a forward step can authorise; the clone checkout's attributes race, judged by ctime against a wall-clock start; the doctor's removal of old locks and incoming refs by age | Left |
| Time and liveness audit, left for later, under its rule that a fixed delay is pacing, not an acknowledgement: the relaunch's and the updater's quit 500 ms after their answer (#928's restart-aware window stays; pacing only since the handoff by id); ~~the updater bridge's request matched by age, not by an id~~ (by id, `sm update`'s handoff box); Tunnel parked unconfigured until its wanted port changes (O18, done) | Left |
| Time and liveness audit, left for later, under its rules that a cache is performance policy (monotonic in memory, never a lifetime of failure) and that a delayed confirmation is checked again at the action: the mirror gateway's bind retry, which outlives a stop; `useConfirmTwice`, whose second click reads a flag, not its deadline; the in-memory wall-clock TTLs (port-pool, gh, Terrier, hygiene, PR maps and image URLs) and the loopback's auth lockout; the notification, move-news, progress and theme rotation gates | Left |
| Time and liveness audit, left for later, the hub's arithmetic: the relay bucket's refill on a backward server step, the socket accept order by epoch, bounds on a persisted cache or expiry dated in the future. Ticket and revocation expiry stay the authority's | Left |
| Time and liveness, left from items 4 and 5: a stopped cloudflared found only at a wake, and a stopped `serve` child only when its stream closes; the pinned spawner's TERM-to-KILL grace measured on the wall clock (a backward step in those 3 s delays the KILL) | Left |
| Two shapes of one job, unified in the simplification pass: `shared/remote/liveness.ts` judges the device links and the hub socket alike, a probe past its timeout asking a fresh one before any verdict, and a set-aside probe interrupted (step 7, the engine pass) | Done |
| Time and liveness, left from #946's review: a proof that moves the clocks against BackgroundFetch's fetch and PR windows (fetched, then an hour's sleep, a forward step and a backward one, each followed by a focus that must fetch), which needs the host runtime and real git | Left |

Step 7's comment pass covered packages/contracts, packages/engine,
packages/cli, packages/ui, app/renderer, app/web, app/lab, marketing/,
hub/, app/host, app/main, app/shared and app/test (#748, #756, #770
and the one that adds this line).

## Measurements and facts

The performance pass (step 7), 2026-10-12, on this machine at a load
average of 3 to 6: dev builds, v3 on release/v3 3315bd9c7 signed in on
the dev hub with its tunnel up, v2 on origin/main 7d2c56daf on a fresh
profile with no hub, each with a clone of this repo as its one project.
Per process from `proc_pid_rusage` (CPU, idle wake-ups a second) and
`top`'s energy impact, ten minutes with the window focused on a
worktree page of three worktrees and five behind another app, nothing
happening:

| Process | v3 focused | v3 behind | v2 focused | v2 behind |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| window | 4.70%, 5.4/s, 4.9 | 0.10%, 4.2/s, 0.3 | 6.84%, 7.9/s, 7.2 | 1.69%, 8.1/s, 2.1 (react-grab, below) |
| its GPU process | 4.34%, 9.6/s, 4.8 | 0.01%, 0.3/s, 0 | 6.14%, 18.6/s, 7.0 | 2.83%, 27.7/s, 4.2 |
| host | 0.27%, 0.8/s, 0.3 | 0.21%, 2.3/s, 0.3 | in main | in main |
| shell's main | 0.04%, 0.2/s, 0 | 0.06%, 0.5/s, 0 | 0.06%, 0.3/s, 0 | 0.02%, 0.3/s, 0 |
| file-sync | 0.01%, 0.3/s, 0 | 0.01%, 0.8/s, 0 | 0.01%, 0.7/s, 0 | 0.01%, 0.7/s, 0 |
| cloudflared | 0.06%, 1.2/s, 0.1 | 0.07%, 2.7/s, 0.2 | not running | not running |

A focused window's cost is the wallpaper's drift and the selected
row's stripes, at every frame in both builds; behind another app they
pause. What fires while nothing happens: in the window, the "ago"
clock every 10 s (blurred too), the loopback ping every 15 s and
Clerk's own token refresh while focused, with no query on an
interval; in the host, the ports view's probes every 5 s (v2's
every 20 s with no ports, and only while focused), the shell
watchdog's probe every 10 s (a `ps` only with a terminal open), the
hub ping every 15 s, the links' liveness sweeps every 30 s, the
background fetch's timer every 60 s (a fetch and a `gh` per project
only while a window is focused) and the update check every 10
minutes; in file-sync, its parent's pid every 2 s, as in v2; the updater's
request file every second (a rename attempt: a watch of the data dir
lost it under the worktrees' churn); the git watcher and the
terminal's save only on an event. The store's `data_version` was read every 500 ms, each read
a trace line, which made the host's 2.3 wake-ups a second and its
trace log 100 MB a day. Watching the store's write-ahead log in its
place, the host behind another app went to 0.14%, 0.43 idle wake-ups
and 1.2 interrupt wake-ups a second (from 5.1), 180 trace lines in five
minutes against 777, and the log to 42 MB a day, now rotated past
1 MB; a title written by `smd describe` showed in the window 577 ms
after the command started, its own start included. A writer puts a
commit in the log before it makes it visible, so the store looks again
once the writes settle (100 ms), and every 10 s for a commit no event
announced (one before the watch was up, or after it failed): with
that, 0.12%, 0.43 and 1.1 a second. A web tab (the web client's dev
build in Chrome, signed in, on the same worktree page over the tunnel)
took 4.46% of a core and its GPU process 3.58% focused, and the same
behind another app's window, since the tab's focus followed only
whether the page was visible; following the window's focus and blur
as the desktop does, 0.34% for all the tab's renderers and 0.02%. A dev window that
react-grab has frozen and thawed (it resumes animations with
`play()`, which CSS can no longer pause) keeps the drift going behind
other windows, the v2 column's 1.7% and 2.8%; a packaged build has no
react-grab (`lab/weblab.md`).

Pages on the same data, 40 worktrees, one with 936 changes (1,935
files) and the repo's history, cold after a reload, median of five,
each from the click to the page drawn: v2 against v3, first contentful
paint 140 against 372 ms and largest 324 against 636 ms (a dev build,
its modules served one by one), a project's page of 40 worktrees 76
against 102 ms, a worktree's page 60 against 88 ms, its changes 2,380
against 5,281 ms, and the history 231 against 1,129 ms. Base UI's
checkbox looks for its `<label>` through its input's `labels` on every
render, a walk of the document per row; drawn as a native button the
row needs none, and the changes took 2,626 ms and the history 684 ms.
What is left of the changes is the first layout of 1,935 rows, about
0.5 s, and of the history the dev build's rendering, with no one cause.
A packaged v3 (the v3 flavor, signed out, over a copy of the same data
dir) is what a user sees: first contentful paint 192 ms and largest
268 ms, the project's page 35 ms, the worktree's 39 ms, its changes
1,097 ms and the history 323 ms. A packaged v2 runs only as the prod
flavor, over the installed app's data, so v2's numbers stay the dev
build's.
The 10 and 100 MB sends are above (1.6 and 6.2 s over the LAN, 2.9 and
11.7 s through the tunnel); v2 pairs devices through the hub, which
the dev hub refuses, so there is no v2 number on this machine.

The renderer's production build, v2 against v3: 14.6 against 15.1 MB
of JavaScript, the first chunk 2.60 against 2.96 MB (3.9 against
4.7 MB before minifying: Base UI's 201 KB and 506 KB more of the app's
own). Effect is 646 KB, nearly all of it outside the first chunk, and
zod's 130 KB went. Clerk is 1.8 MB of every window's first chunk in
both. Nothing shipped goes unloaded but the web client's 9 KB touch
icon, copied in with the public folder.

The regressions pass (step 7), 2026-10-12, on this machine: an edit
outside the app reached the worktree page in 0.45 s with no focus, the
host idling at 0.0 to 0.3% CPU with three working trees watched; a
second window followed a theme change 1 ms after the first's patch; a
peer's view that never showed a value gives up after 7 asks with no
session, 7.75 s on the redial's ladder; the v3 package carries the
1,322 file icons the installed 2.35 does (none before #832).

Lines on 2026-10-07: main 8.3k, host 20.7k, shared 16k, renderer 62k,
web 1.2k, proofs 23k, lab 3.9k, hub 3.5k, cli 22k plus 11.5k of
tests, file-sync 1.6k. Remote code is about a third of the repo and
about half of the app backend. Tests are 21% of the repo.

Counts the plan replaces: 111 `console.*` calls, 57 `AbortSignal` and
`AbortController` uses, 10 `process.env` reads, 88 files importing
zod, 64 React Query call sites, 12 store files, 49 proofs, 39 Go test
files. Of 104 commits touching `cli/`, 22 also had to touch the TS
side.

The terminal `sm` on 2026-10-08, compiled with bytecode and ad hoc
signed, median of 40 warm runs on a loaded machine: `--json config
list` 28.6 ms and `--help` 24.4 ms, against the Go binary's 4.7 ms.
The binary is 69 MB, Go's 12.5 MB. Without bytecode `config list`
takes 40.5 ms. Loading the engine only when a command runs saved
nothing measurable: the floor is Bun and Effect starting. Again on
2026-10-08 with every verb in, the same way on a more loaded machine:
`--json config list` 43.2 ms and `--help` 30.5 ms against Go's 10.3 ms
and 6.0 ms, and 79 MB against 12.5 MB. Ink, loaded only when a menu
shows, adds about 5 MB and no startup.

Calls in flight on a window's loopback (2026-10-11), the host's cap
64 a connection: a worktree page peaked at 3 to 5 in normal use, 19
with a `git status` slowed to 6 s, and 64 with one of 24 s (the cap
within 20 s with one of 12 s), where the host refused the rest, up to
55 of them copies of `changeStatus` whose fetch React Query had
dropped. After queries interrupt the calls they drop, a dropped
fetch's git is gone and its place back on the host at once (`pnpm test
in-flight`): in the dev app, thirty commits a second or so apart
against a 6 s status ran at most 5 `git status` at once, none left
12 s after the last, and no refusal. Forty-eight calls dropped while
the host still cleaned them up, and forty-eight new ones made at once:
32 refused while the host freed a place only after its cleanup, none
once it frees it as the interrupt arrives. A burst of 300
calls on one link reached the host 64 at once with 236 refused before
the link's gate, and 48 at once with none refused, in the order they
were made, after it.

The device link's bench (`pnpm test bench/wire`, a shaped 80 ms,
20 Mbit/s link, deflate on) on 2026-10-09, the old wire against Effect
RPC: connect 179 ms against 370 (a round trip more: the challenge is a
call, and a ping orders the push subscriptions before the link counts
as open); a small invoke 86 against 88; 20 concurrent 87 against 91. A
10 MB bundle down a link took 4.1 s on the old wire, 5.0 s with the
link's frames in JSON (bytes as base64) and 4.6 s in Effect's binary
layout (4.4 s with deflate off, which raw bytes gain nothing from);
up one, 4.2, 4.5 and 4.4 s. The floor on that link is 4.0 s. What is
left is the stream's acknowledgement, one round trip per batch of a
channel's bytes. Over JSON a LAN link paid base64's third more; in
binary it pays nothing.

The byte channels on 2026-10-10, sealed (protocol 8, deflate refused
by the listener): the bench's 10 MB bundle took 4.26 s down and 4.31 s
up the 80 ms link with the read a stream, and 4.19 and 4.28 s with it
as numbered calls, eight of 256 KB in flight. A send carrying a 10 MB
uncommitted file fell after 50 s on the stream, in the `sync-transfer`
proof's direct link on the loopback as in the lab. With the calls,
that proof sent 10 MB in 1.4 s and 100 MB in 5.7 s, and between two
dev devices on one machine 10 MB took 1.6 s and 100 MB 6.2 s over the
LAN, 2.9 and 11.7 s over the tunnel, each landing byte for byte.

The byte channels' window on 2026-10-10 (`pnpm test bench/wire`, the
80 ms, 20 Mbit/s link, and `--rtt=1 --down=1000 --up=1000` for a
LAN; the bench's terminal is a 2 KB chunk every 16 ms on
`terminals:attach`, and its calls a small one every 100 ms). Against
the 4.6 s and the old wire's 4.1 s, a 10 MB bundle down the 80 ms link
took 4.19 s with eight 256 KB reads in flight (2 MiB) and 4.40 s with
the window (median of three; up, 4.28 and 4.52 s). Under it a terminal
chunk waited 1,259 ms against 83 idle and a small call 784 ms with
eight 256 KB, and 134 against 88 and 117 ms with the window: the
window's 64 KB of queue is 26 ms at 20 Mbit/s. Fixed counts from 128
KB to 2 MiB in flight ran from 10.6 s with 156 ms of terminal wait to
4.19 s with 1,244 ms. On the LAN the bundle took 0.13 to 0.16 s
either way, a terminal chunk waiting 15 to 20 ms with eight 256 KB
and 5 to 7 ms with the window. A window holds at most 2 MiB a channel
on each end; through the 80 ms link's transfers it held 283 to 326 KB (p10 to p90, median 315 KB). Between two
dev devices on one machine with the window, a peer's terminal under
`yes` drawn in the other's window while it sent a 100 MB worktree and
mirrored a 10 MB one: the window's frame held at 33.3 ms (p95 35 ms),
and a peer call every 100 ms took 74 ms at the median under the flood
alone and 96 ms (p95 167) under the transfers over the LAN, 99 and
120 ms (p95 248) over the tunnel, with none failing. The send took
10.3 s over the LAN (6.5 s alone) and 16.6 s over the tunnel.

Nuke everything on 2026-10-10, on the dev flavor (a dev profile
signed in and enrolled, a project with a worktree, a terminal open in
it, the worktree mirrored to a second profile, a key rebound): from
the confirming click the window was gone in under 0.3 s and the fresh
start logged its first line 2.2 s in; the relaunched window answered
6.0 s after the click, most of it the dev launcher restarting forge.
After it the data dir, userData and logs held only files that start
wrote, the worktree and its mirror were gone, the device was off the
hub's list, and the window opened on the onboarding with an empty
client config.

The same on the dogfood flavor on 2026-10-11 (then named test, now v3,
so its names were `smt` and `~/.smt`), signed out (a packaged test
build, a project picked from terrier's list, a worktree made with
`smt`, a terminal open in it, `smt`'s shell and agent hooks
installed): the window was gone 0.5 s after the confirming click and
the relaunched one answered on the onboarding at 1.5 s; over three
runs the fresh start logged its first line 1.2 to 2.8 s in. After it
`~/.smt`, its userData and logs held only files that
start wrote, the worktree and its branch were gone, `~/.zshrc`,
`~/.claude/settings.json` and `~/.codex/hooks.json` were byte for byte
what they were before the hooks went in, terrier's registry was
unchanged, and no cache folder or `~/.config/shigomori-test` was left.
The relaunch measured is through LaunchServices (the flavors box); the
earlier `app.relaunch()` came back in 1.0 to 1.4 s and then lost its
window.

Signed in, on the v3 flavor on 2026-10-11 (a packaged v3 build from
2639ba179 enrolled through its own Continue with GitHub, beside a dev
profile, the two listing each other; picto-place added from terrier's
list): the window was gone 1.8 s after the confirming click, the new
instance up at 1.9 s and logging at 2.2 s, and on the onboarding by
4.2 s (each look through the accessibility tree costs about a second).
After it the device was off the hub's list, `smv3 devices` said signed
out, `~/.smv3`, the v3 userData and logs held only files that start
wrote, the client config was gone, no child process was killed, and
the window offered Continue with GitHub again.

The host's Promise faces on 2026-10-09, counted as modules that build
one (`PromiseAdapter.make` or `forService`) and the modules outside a
composition root that call one: 19 faces (17 in the host, 2 in the
shell) and 29 callers on `release/v3` before step 7's first adapter PR,
7 (5 and 2) and 14 after it, 6 and 13 after the scripts PR, 5 and 12
after the mirror PR, 4 and 11 after the sync PR and after the engine
calls and the worktrees and projects handlers, and 3 and 4 (all four
in the shell) once the rest of the host left the engine face, then 3
and 7 with git on Effect (the Processes face's Promise callers that
step 7's B5b removes: the git face, the background fetch and control
ops), and 3 and 4 again once they went. Effect
runtime sites in the host outside
`app/host/process/`: 10 before (the adapter, the tracer's
Promise face, the data-dir and shell-environment reads, the store's
failure report, a channel's queue), 11 once the engine face went (the
engine handle the Promise code left is handed), 8 once the Promise
protocols went with it, 7 once every handler answered with an effect
(the tracer's Promise face gone), and 4 once the adapter went (the
boot-time reads before any graph and a channel's queue), with 0 faces
and 0 callers; the root adds 5 inside `app/host/process/` (its door's
three, the graph's runtime and the push hub).

The host as a utility process (decision 6) on 2026-10-09, a dev build
(unminified) on one dev device, signed in with the tunnel up and no
projects: the host process 144 MB resident and the shell's main 256 MB
(decision 6 guessed 60 MB for the host). Forked 3 ms after ready, the
host was up 175 ms later, 180 ms before the window finished loading,
in each of three launches. A call from the window to the host over the
loopback took 0.38 ms on average over 200 (`runtime:info`), and one to
the shell over its port 0.08 ms (`account:acceptsCommands`).

The terminal on 2026-10-09, dev builds on one loaded machine, two dev
devices and the web client. Attaching a terminal, from the call to its
history arriving, median of 30: 4.5 ms on the loopback, 5.4 ms over the
device link on the LAN (through `hub:watchPeer`), and 15.1 ms (of 20)
from the web client over the tunnel. A fresh attach of a vim session
replayed 17.9k characters of history, and the web client's resume after
eight seconds offline 197, what it had missed. After a `yes` flood the
ring holds its cap, 524k characters. An idle login zsh costs the host
about 1.5 MB of RSS (ten opened took it from 68.6 to 83.2 MB) and holds
about 8.7 MB of its own. Under `yes` the window kept the 33 ms frame it
has at rest (this machine composites it at 30 Hz; median and p95 within
2 ms of idle), the client took 15M characters a second in 50 chunks,
and Ctrl-C stopped the output 31 ms after it was sent. Ten terminals
closed in 115 ms.

Saving the terminals while they run, on 2026-10-10, on a loaded
laptop, `yes` for 10 s in one shell and in three, two runs of each.
Saving at each 16 ms batch, coalesced while a write is under way,
made 33 to 54 writes a second, each with an `lsof`, and with three
shells the output fell to 2.6 to 3.6M characters a second against 8
to 90M otherwise. Saved two seconds after a change: 0.5 writes a
second per flooding shell, a write's median 1.1 to 2.3 ms (12 ms at
most), the `lsof` 20 to 70 ms in its subprocess, and the host's
event-loop p99 7 to 14 ms, as with no saving. That rate under output
that never stops (an agent CLI's spinner left open) rewrites the
512 KB ring every two seconds, about 20 GB a day per terminal, so a
save waits for two quiet seconds and comes at least once a minute:
under `yes` for 65 s, one write, at 60 s. An idle shell writes
nothing. A full ring is a row of about 512 KB. The WAL peaks at about
4.5 MB, where SQLite's automatic checkpoint (1000 pages) folds it back,
and three flooded shells take the database from 4 KB to 1.7 MB. In the
dev app with three shells under `yes`, the window kept its 33 ms
frame (median 33.3 ms, p95 34.1 ms over 8 s) and the host took 44% of
a core. With the host and the shell's main killed (`kill -9`) and the
app relaunched, all three came back in their folders, each with its
524k characters of history.

The renderer's runtime (step 6) on 2026-10-09, a dev build on one dev
device with three projects, signed out, median of reloads read off the
page (first contentful paint; the sidebar listing the projects; the
heap after two forced collections): before, 236 ms, 586 ms and
63.65 MB over 9; with the window on its runtime (#720), 196 ms,
580 ms and 64.58 MB over 15; with the peer hop, the web client and the
fake host on it too, 248 ms, 629 ms and 64.71 MB over 15 on a machine
at a load average of 15 against 9 before. The 1 MB is the runtime's
modules. Three adapter files went (198 lines), and part 1 as a whole
is 753 lines of code added against 561 removed: the Effect face of a link
costs more than the hand-rolled glue it replaced, and the adapters
left are the invalidation's.

The atom-react go/no-go on 2026-10-09 (`@effect/atom-react` 4.0.2),
dev builds on two dev devices, a project's worktrees in one window as
an atom over `worktrees:watch` beside the React Query list, ten
host-side shelf flips each, medians: from the change to the view
showing it, 405 ms against 514 ms on this machine and 396 against 507
from a peer over the hub hop; one render per change either way (two
under StrictMode); an optimistic flip shown in 1 to 2 ms either way.
Those paths took about 60 lines for the 90 they replaced. The host
served 9 data views against 84 query-key families.
`Atom.optimistic` takes no success that is still waiting while it
holds one, and a live stream's values all are, so an optimistic copy
over a stream would never update; and at a transition's end it
refreshes its source, which through `Atom.map` restarts the stream.
A restart costs a peer's list over the hub hop 61 ms from the
subscription to its first value (median of 15, a list of two worktrees
on a dev device on this machine). The app's settle wrapper
(`renderer/lib/runtime/atoms.ts`) is a readable of its own, so the
refresh re-reads the value the stream holds and nothing resubscribes.

The mono faces on 2026-10-09, in the web client's build: 1.32 MB
more. JetBrains Mono is 86 KB in six subsets, of which a page in
English loads the 40 KB latin one; the Nerd Font symbols are 1.23 MB,
fetched when a terminal opens; the stylesheet grew 2 KB. The packaged
app's renderer carries the same files.

The scenes on 2026-10-10, on a loaded laptop: the `scenes` proof

Multiple windows on 2026-10-10, a dev build on one dev device with one
project of four worktrees, signed in. A second window opened from the
first painted 267 ms after the call (its first contentful paint 248 ms
into its load), against the first window's 1268 ms at the app's start
(the dev server compiling as it loads). The host's RSS a minute after
the start with one window was 139 MB and a minute after two more
opened 152 MB, about 6 MB a window. A view to three windows: a
worktree's shelf flipped from one of them ten times, 1.5 s apart, came
back to each in a median 810 to 910 ms, the three 104 ms apart first
to last, against 453 ms with one window. Each subscription re-read
its view on every signal, so three windows on a project's page read
its worktrees three times or more per change. With one read per view
and input shared by its subscribers (`host/lib/views.ts`,
`ViewShares`), the same flips on 2026-10-10 came back to three windows
in a median 490 to 498 ms, 12 ms apart first to last, and to one
window in 392 ms.

The marketing site's frames on 2026-10-10, the built site served with
its headers and Brotli (quality 5), Chrome on a throttled Slow 4G link
(150 ms, 1.6 Mbit/s) with the CPU slowed four times, median of three:
largest contentful paint 1064 ms before (the hero's picture) and
1224 ms after (the headline). The script the page loads at first is
0.9 KB before (the theme script and the page's) and 2.2 KB after: the
theme script grew 0.4 KB to dress the frames, and the page's 0.9 KB
with the frames' loader and Vite's import helper. The app and the

End-to-end encryption on 2026-10-10, dev builds on one loaded machine,
three dev profiles and the web client in Helium, against the dev hub.
The handshake makes no round trip the link did not make before (it
takes the challenge's place), so a connection adds its crypto: a full
IK handshake costs the dialer and the listener about 4.8 ms of CPU
each in Node (median of 200, four X25519 operations a side) and about
1 ms each in Helium, on the LAN, the tunnel and the relay alike, the
relay's connectInfo ask and answer being one handshake more per dial.
Attaching a terminal, median of 30 (p90): 1.8 ms (2.0) over the LAN,
7.4 ms (9.1) between two desktops over the tunnel, and 14.3 ms (15.3)
from the web client through the tunnel, beside 5.4 and 15.1 ms before
(the LAN figure replays a fresh shell's history, not vim's). Under
`yes` the window kept its 33.3 ms frame (p95 34.7 against 35.2 idle),
took 20.7M characters a second in 50 chunks, and the output stopped
48 ms after Ctrl-C. Sealing 10 MiB in 256 KiB frames takes 79 ms of
CPU in Node and 49 ms in Helium. A 10 MB file crossed a mirror's
stream in 0.53 s over the LAN and 0.75 s through the tunnel, the
hosts' CPU peaking at 44% sending and 54% receiving on the LAN and 90%
and 56% through the tunnel (ps, every 200 ms). Each frame grows by its
16-byte tag; a connection's handshake is 135 bytes from the dialer
(the ticket in clear and the 96-byte first message) and 48 back. A
logging proxy on a LAN link between two profiles saw the ticket and
nothing else in clear: across 63 frames carrying a terminal and a
diff, neither the command, its output, the diff's text, the file name
nor an RPC call's name appears, and the payloads read 7.8 to 7.9 bits
of entropy a byte.

The migration's screen on 2026-10-10, a dev build as a dev profile on

lines.

The web client's reliability soak on 2026-10-10, `pnpm reliability
--soak 4h` (seed 504596714) on the `release/v3` tip after #755, a dev
app on this machine as the host and the dev hub at protocol 6, headless
Chrome: 108 scenarios in 239.6 min, 107 recovered within their bound
and one failed. The failure was a second tab whose direct ticket its
sibling's ask voided (`reload-and-second-tab` at 17:07), the race #758
closes on the client and the encryption thread's link PR on the host.
Recovery, median and longest, from the disruption's end: `network-drop`
(11 runs) 10.3 and 16.2 s, the hub socket waiting out its backoff
after the browser reported the network back; `network-blip` (15) 0.3
and 1.2 s; `hub-redeploy` (13, the sockets riding through every
deploy and cut after it) 1.9 and 3.0 s; `host-kill` (10) 4.8 and
19.1 s, the longest of the soak, a browser waiting out its dial ladder
for the restarted host's tunnel; `app-relaunch` (16, from the relaunch)
1.7 and 7.8 s; `tab-sleep` (17) 1.9 and 5.3 s; `reload-and-second-tab`
(15 of 16) 0.2 and 1.0 s; `token-expiry` (10) 2.1 and 3.1 s. No
stuck connecting outside that one tab, no stale view, no uncaught
error, and no failed request a tab left unlogged. A browser reporting
the network gone read the host as Reconnecting after 4.0 to 4.6 s.

Connected through a whole outage), and the web soak above (107 of 108

One client and one scenario were left out, by decision: the web
client, since its browser profile signs in through the owner's own
browser (Helium), which was in use, so its two scenarios
(`reload-and-second-tab`, `two-tabs-redial`) skipped as having no
client; and `hub-redeploy`, since the pass changed nothing in the hub
and other threads were on the dev hub. Against the soak of 2026-10-11
(42 of 44, protocol 10), `host-kill`'s longest went from 43.9 s to
8.9 s, the stable port and the tunnel fixes at work, and no scenario
failed. The tip moved to protocol 14 during the run; the soak names the
commit it ran on.

`host-kill` against the device on the tunnel alone (`pnpm reliability
--clients tunnel`, six runs a side) on 2026-10-12, on `release/v3` after
#868 and then with the host holding a tunnel-only ask until its tunnel
is up: before, 8.1, 8.1, 15.9, 16.6, 16.7 and 32.7 s (median 16.2 s);
after, 6.9, 8.8, 9.0, 11.4, 13.3 and 17.7 s (median 10.2 s), every run
recovered. What is left after is the host's restart and cloudflared
registering again. A tunnel just made is refused by Cloudflare's edge
("Tunnel not found") for about 30 s before its connector registers, so
a fresh profile's first minute says nothing about the tunnel.

The full pass again on 2026-10-10 at protocol 7 (`release/v3` at

Failure paths in the lab on 2026-10-12, driven through the window by
an agent, each run on v3 and on v2 by hand, the same steps: v3 a dev
profile on `release/v3` at 24028b196 (protocol 13), v2 a dev profile on
`origin/main` at 97d435b2e, each with a throwaway repo and one on an
8 MB disk image. The hub-bound scenarios (the soaks above, enrollment)
are not comparable: the dev hub refuses v2.

| Scenario | What the user sees (v3) | Recovery (v3) | v2, by hand |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| pre-commit hook fails | the hook's stderr under the commit form, the summary kept | fix the hook, commit again: 0.6 s | the same: the hook's words under the form, the next commit lands |
| `index.lock` held | git's "Unable to create … index.lock: File exists", with its advice | remove the lock, commit again: 0.2 s | the same git words; the edit made with the lock held showed only once the page was entered again |
| index corrupt | was "No changes" on the changes page; now "Couldn't read the changes"; the worktree page and sidebar still "No uncommitted changes" (left above) | rebuild the index (`rm .git/index && git reset`): the page reads 1 file changed | a toast, "Couldn't read change status", over a page reading "No changes"; the worktree page "No uncommitted changes" |
| host killed mid-commit (SIGKILL, hook holding git 6 s) | the window carries on; the commit lands, the tree clean | host forked again in 0.2 s; git finished the commit on its own | v2's host is the app's main process: the window went with it and nothing restarted; git finished the commit |
| disk full on the repo's volume | git's "No space left on device" under the form | free space, commit again: 0.2 s | the same git words, the next commit lands |
| project's drive unplugged | the page keeps its last rows; a refresh says "/Volumes/…/full is not there anymore" | re-plug, refresh: the rows are back | a refresh says nothing; the page keeps its last rows |
| data folder on a drive not connected | a dialog naming the drive, no host forked (#855) | reconnect, relaunch | not run on v2; v3 before #855 forked a host that failed, its modal in 9 s |
| host stopped (SIGSTOP) | (#846) | watchdog forks a new host at 40 s; a quit exits in 5.4 s | not run on v2 (no host of its own to stop); v3 before #846 never quit |

The proofs on 2026-10-10, one run each on a loaded laptop, before
and after the shared fakes (`app/test/README.md`, "Shared fakes"):
74 files and 643 cases both times, the whole run 145.0 s and 142.6 s.
Each proof kept its cases: `direct-plane` 41 in 11.8 and 11.7 s,
`socket-host` 36 in 9.7 and 9.1, `sync-transfer` 21 in 18.2 and 17.5,
`mirror` 29 in 10.6 and 9.7, `control` 15 in 19.2 and 18.4,
`port-forward` 16 in 2.5 and 2.2, `channel-streams` 3 in 2.6 and 2.3,
`mirror-daemon-lines` 7 in 2.0 and 1.8, `mirror-status` 3 in 1.9 and
1.7. The time is the proofs' own (real git, sockets, a built engine);
what went is the copies, `direct-plane` 66 lines shorter with its
dozen boots of a stub hub, a pair and a bridge now one `bridgeTo`.

The classification golden on the same day, one run each: `socket-host`
36 cases in 9.1 s, then 35 in 9.3 s, its hand checks of about 40
annotations and its `read-surface.golden.json` case gone. Their case
is the contracts' `classification` (280 calls, the 60 ungated reads
the old file held among them, and the six `link:` calls it never
listed), so the contracts' suite went from 869 cases in 0.87 s to 870
in 0.72 s.

The terminal's menu tests on the same day, after the ready signal:
"filters by name, and esc cancels" passed 50 runs of 50 in a loop
(206 s, beside other checks). With a menu made to take its keys 500
ms late, the old 150 ms wait lost a key and the new harness passed.
The four tests at a terminal went from 5.4 s to 3.9 s, and
`packages/cli`'s `cli.test.ts` from 16.2 s to 14.7 s, 32 cases both.
A failed terminal case left `script` and the binary running, since the
harness killed `sh` alone. It now ends the whole process group, on a
failure and when the test finishes, and a case that fails with a menu
up proves nothing is left (20 runs of 20). 33 cases in 15.7 s.

The guide on 2026-10-11, `pnpm guide-eval` (`app/lab/guide-eval`): a
dev build as a fresh dev profile on one loaded machine, one project and
one worktree, signed out, twenty how-tos run headless through each
agent (Claude Code 2.1, Codex 0.162), each on a session of its own
from the window's Ask. The first run passed 17 of 20 for each. It
found a toggle row's switch with no name, which both agents read as
nothing (fixed), and two criteria of the harness's own. After the
review's fixes, 19 of 20 for Claude Code and 18 of 20 for Codex, the
three misses the harness not reading a name given by
`aria-labelledby` or a `<label for>` (fixed), and those two how-tos
again: 4 of 4. So 20 of 20 for each. Medians, Claude Code then Codex:
from the Ask to the agent's first action in the window, 9.5 and 15.6 s
(three Asks each, through Terminal). A tour's first action came 17.5
and 11.2 s after it started, its first point at 23.7 and 15.7 s, its
end at 33.3 and 36.0 s. A tour took 319k and 408k tokens, nearly all
of them read again from the cache, of which 2.3k and 1.3k written. Through `sm guide mcp`,
the window on the same machine, median of ten: `look` 37 and 6 ms,
`screenshot` (a PNG at the window's 920 by 720) 85 and 34 ms. A click
on Delete worktree is refused in 10 ms with nothing sent to the page.
Claude Code asks once to trust the guide's folder, its default "No,
exit". No debugger is attached outside a session. A release's source,
a shallow clone at v2.34.1, is 29 MB and took 1 s on this network.

The guide on 2026-10-12, opened from the Help menu (no Ask, the agent
started with the question), the same profile, machine and twenty
how-tos (Claude Code 2.1.296, Codex 0.162.1), each launched the way
the Help menu's item does: 20 of 20 for each again. Started on the
app's own opening instead, each agent said what it could do and asked
what the user wanted, touching nothing in the window, 3 of 3 each,
in 13.3 and 5.3 s (median). Medians, Claude Code then Codex, the
day before's in brackets: a tour's first action at 17.1 and 13.8 s
(17.5, 11.2), its first point at 20.5 and 17.2 s (23.7, 15.7), its
end at 31.1 and 37.1 s (33.3, 36.0), and 335k and 400k tokens (319k,

Both runs above measured the guide as a tour: the instructions of
then, the launch opening the agent on "Show me around Shigoto no
Mori.", and the opening's checks. The guide is now a launch for a
question (the role as system-level instructions, no opening message,
`agentGuide.ts`), and the eval keeps the how-tos alone. The how-tos'
numbers are to be taken again against it.

Casts and suppressions on 2026-10-12, counted over the TypeScript and
JavaScript sources outside `lint/` (whose rules and tests quote them),
a line each: `as unknown as` outside a comment, an `oxlint-disable`
or `eslint-disable` directive, a `@ts-expect-error`, `@ts-ignore` or
`@ts-nocheck`. `main` at 7d2c56daf: 2 double casts, 139 lint
suppressions, 2 TypeScript ones. `release/v3` at 651265493 before the
review: 18, 163 and 1. After it: 11, 143 and 1. Seven casts became a
single cast or a comment once the types allowed it; the eleven left
build a typed client from a contract's own calls, or type an Effect
RPC client that knows its calls only as `Rpc.AnyWithProps`, or stand
in a test fake. Of the suppressions, seven went with those casts,
four with a named type import, six with four loops whose order doesn't
matter now run together, and four were unused; one came with
`effect-fn-service-methods` (`Moves.run`, generic over the move it
runs). The rest each say why on their own line; 90 of them are
`no-await-in-loop`, nearly all in the proofs and the lab, where a step
waits on the one before by design. Then, with the owner's word,
`no-await-in-loop` went off for the proofs, the lab and the packages'
test folders (`.oxlintrc.json`'s override), where a sequential await
is the intent, and those 76 suppressions with it: 67 left, 14 of them
`no-await-in-loop` in source folders, where the rule still holds.

The simplification pass (step 7), 2026-10-12, non-blank lines of
source by package, against `release/v3` before each PR. One helper for
paths and error words (#883): engine 31,520 to 31,492, cli 6,816 to
6,801, shared 8,044 to 8,039, main 6,613 to 6,611, host 28,476 to

The AtomRpc spike (decision 18), 2026-10-12, on this machine: a dev
build, one local device signed out, a repository of 30 files, the
changes page's list read three ways by one container: Q, the
`worktrees:changeStatus` query invalidated by the `filesChanged` push;
V, the host's `worktrees:watchChangeStatus` view as an atom; R, a
request atom with reactivity keys on the client's runtime (what
`AtomRpc.query` builds), the push invalidating its key. Medians of
ten. Renders count the whole changes container, the diff and hunk
queries beside the list included.

| From a file written outside the app to the list naming it | Median | Range | Renders |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Q | 1,081 ms | 1,071 to 1,115 | 10 to 12 |
| V | 1,076 ms | 946 to 1,106 | 10 to 12 |
| R | 1,115 ms | 1,092 to 1,153 | 10 to 12 |

The working trees' watch holds an edit for a one-second window
(`GitWatcher`'s `EDIT_WINDOW`), so the models' own cost is about 80, 76
and 115 ms; V's low end is its two-second poll reading before the
window closed.

| A refresh asked as the file is written, to the list naming it | Median | Range | Renders |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Q (`invalidateTreeState`) | 111 ms | 93 to 258 | 6 |
| V (a fresh subscription) | 96 ms | 79 to 148 | 2 |
| R (`registry.refresh`) | 83 ms | 78 to 94 | 4 |

| Code | Shared | Each read |
| --- | --- | --- |
| R | 38 lines (the request atom, the invalidation) | about 9, and a key per push and per mutation |
| V | the host's refresh (`Views.refresh`) | about 8, the push listener and the read's key gone |

No peer was measured: the earlier go/no-go's gap (about 110 ms) was
mostly the hub hop, which a view saves and the other two pay.

Versions on 2026-10-07: effect 4.0.2 (2026-10-07), @effect/vitest,
@effect/platform-node, @effect/platform-bun and @effect/atom-react
4.0.2, @effect/tsgo 0.51.1, vitest 5.0.3, knip 6.40.0, Node 24.15,
Electron 43.1 with Node 24.18, Bun 1.4.0, Go 1.26.5.

**A watch that froze the host** (2026-10-16, the owner's dogfood
alpha.8). The shell's watchdog found the host silent for 30 s and
started another. That host listened on its loopback and then went no
further. It never renamed its `loopback.json` in (the file aside
stayed half done, still open for writing), never reached the hub, and
had a finished child it never reaped. It sat at 0% CPU while the
terminal read the app as not running. Quitting and reopening the app
stalled the same way at the fresh boot.

A sample of the stuck host showed its main thread in `open(2)` called
from libuv's `uv_fs_event_start`, which is `fs.watch` opening the
path it is about to watch, synchronously, on the loop's own thread.
GitWatcher had just started its watches, one per working tree of each
project. Ten seconds before the first stall, a worktree of the repo
had been made on an external USB disk. The system log at the fresh
boot names the wait: the sandbox asked TCC for the host's access to
it, the app holds no Full Disk Access, and a second request, which
the log doesn't name, never had an answer. By what the host touched,
that is the Files and Folders consent for a removable volume. macOS holds the open until someone answers that prompt, so
the whole host waited on it. The same open from a terminal returns at
once, under the terminal's own grant.

Three things made one held open into a dead app, each fixed in the
host-stall PR:

- A watch is started only once its path has opened on the thread
  pool, without blocking, within 5 s, on a folder or a file
  (`host/lib/watchPath.ts`, for GitWatcher's watches and the mirror's
  index watch). A path that doesn't is tried again every 30 s and
  watched once it opens. The log names it: for a path under
  `/Volumes/`, as waiting for permission to read files on a removable
  or network volume. A held open keeps one of the pool's four threads
  until it returns, and the host's file reads and writes share that
  pool, so no more than two of these opens are under way at once and a
  retry waits on its path's own.
- A host had no watchdog until it said it was up. Its loop now beats
  on its parent port from its first moment, and the shell kills a
  starting host whose beats stop for 60 s. A second such start in a
  row is the failure, in main.log and on the app's host-failure path,
  not a loop of starts. A killed host that doesn't exit within 5 s
  (one stopped by a signal) is killed outright.
- A host gone unasked left `loopback.json` naming its dead port, and
  its `loopback.json.<pid>`. The shell clears both before it starts
  the next host (waiting at most 2 s), so the clearing can't take the
  next host's own file. ~~Its orphaned cloudflared is still reaped only once a
  later host's tunnel starts (`Tunnel.ts`'s pid file).~~ A host that
  never got that far left it running for good: one with ppid 1 ran
  for 10 hours across two dead hosts. One owner reaps it now: the
  host, first thing as it starts (`process/host.ts`, Tunnel's
  `reapOrphanedConnector`), before anything that can stall the boot,
  and awaited before the graph, whose Tunnel writes the file again.
  That covers a host the shell forks again and an app that crashed
  whole. The reap at the Tunnel's first reconcile is gone. Its `ps`
  has a 5 s deadline, and an `exec` past its deadline is now killed
  outright 3 s after its SIGTERM (`processes.ts`), as `captureWithin`
  already was, so the boot never waits on it for good. Proof:
  `tunnel-orphan`, a stand-in named cloudflared recorded in the pid
  file is killed, and a process of another name under the pid is left
  running.

The packaged app's Info.plist now carries
`NSRemovableVolumesUsageDescription` and
`NSNetworkVolumesUsageDescription`, so the prompt says why it asks.

v2 makes the same blocking open: it watches each project's git
directory with `fs.watch` in its main process. It watches no working
trees, so a worktree alone on an external disk doesn't reach it, but
a project kept on one would freeze v2's whole app the same way. That
is not a regression.

Proofs: `watch-open`, the blocking working tree (a FIFO stands in for
the held open: before the fix the real GitWatcher stopped the loop
for the 6 s until the FIFO's writer came, now it doesn't stop it);
and `host-hold`, a real process stuck in a watch's open called silent
by its beats, and the loopback cleanup.

## Risks

- Effect's unstable modules will have breaking minor releases. Pin
  exact versions; upgrades are their own PRs.
- Clone checkout and the updater are the two places where a port can
  lose data or brick an install. Let the parity harness and the
  differential fuzz test run long there.
- The wire break strands any device not updated through the last v2.
  The version floor makes that explicit instead of silent.
- Simple code gets a little wordier under Effect, and debugging through
  fibers is less direct than plain async stacks. The tracer and the
  conventions are the answer. The go/no-go of decision 15 was to be
  the check; it gave way to the owner's trust and the step reports.

## Left alone

Electron, React and the renderer's UI libraries; the three remote
mechanisms (port forwarding, mirroring, transfer); Mutagen in
`file-sync`; cloudflared; Clerk; the hub on Cloudflare; the villagers.
Effect's `workflow`, `eventlog`, `cluster` and `ai` are out, but for
`ai`'s `McpServer`, which serves the guide's tools. zustand
or jotai, form and hotkey libraries, simple-git or
isomorphic-git, Octokit, electron-updater and @parcel/watcher were
considered and rejected. `semver` and `proper-lockfile` were rejected
only because they would disagree with the Go CLI; after step 2 they
are fair game.

## Open

Not decided yet; the owner's call when they come up.

- Auto-merge on green for `release/v3` PRs after finalize (the owner leans
  yes).
- Nightly prereleases from the branch and a packaged-app smoke in CI,
  later, if the dogfooding needs them.
- The npm scope for the packages.
